Abstract  
Agroforestry is increasingly recognised as a restorative land-use strategy capable of 
enhancing climate change resilience, increasing carbon (C) sequestration, and delivering 
multiple ecosystem services. This thesis studied a mature (>30 years) silvoarable 
agroforestry system in northern England, where cropping in the alleys stopped in 2004. 
Of the poplar hybrids studied, Trichobel exhibited the highest aboveground biomass C 
stock (65.8 Mg C ha⁻¹) and greatest rates of C accumulation (mean annual C increment - 
MAIC: 2.1 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹; periodic annual C increment - PAIC: 2.3 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹), 
demonstrating substantial genotype specific differences in C sequestration potential. 
Carbon uptake by Trichobel was 52% greater than that of the least productive hybrid 
Gibecq. Upscaling these findings suggests that widespread adoption of poplar-based 
silvoarable systems could remove between 0.87 and 7.16 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹, equivalent to 
offsetting between 2.1% and 17.5% of projected UK agricultural greenhouse gas (GHG) 
emissions by 2050. Higher summer temperatures reduced PAIC in Beaupre and Gibecq, 
while Trichobel responded positively to rainfall and wind speed, highlighting climate-
driven differences in C sequestration. Agroforestry also increased soil organic carbon 
(SOC) stocks by 27% compared with adjacent conventional arable land, with a net SOC 
sequestration rate of 0.6 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹. Higher SOC concentrations in the topsoil (0-25 
cm), together with positive relationships between soil nutrients availability, particularly 
nitrogen and phosphorus, and both live and dead root traits, indicate that root traits play 
an important role in regulating nutrient dynamics and SOC accumulation. In the  
hedgerow system, decomposition followed a biphasic pattern, with rapid initial mass 
loss (40-60%; 0-223 days) followed by slower decomposition (224-366 days) as readily 
decomposable material was depleted, while buried litter decomposed more rapidly than 
surface litter, reaching 99% mass loss within 12 months. Collectively, the findings 
demonstrate that C sequestration in agroforestry is not driven by tree presence alone, 
but also by the interactions among productive tree genotypes, favourable environmental 
conditions, enhanced SOC storage, root-mediated processes, and active nutrient cycling. 
Consequently, the effectiveness of agroforestry as a net-zero strategy depends not only 
on increasing tree cover, but also on managing system components in ways that 
optimise both aboveground and belowground C sequestration pathways. These results 
emphasise the importance of integrating tree selection, soil management, and ecological 
[PDF PAGE 7]
vii 
 
functioning into future agroforestry design and policy to maximise climate-change 
mitigation benefits.

1 
 
 
Chapter 1. Carbon and nutrient dynamics in a temperate agroforestry 
system  
1.1. Introduction 
Agroforestry is widely regarded as a sustainable land management approach that 
intentionally integrates trees and shrubs with crops and/or livestock within the same 
space, generating both socioeconomic and ecological benefits (Castle et al., 2022; Raj et 
al., 2014; Torquebiau, 2000). Globally, agroforestry systems are estimated to cover 
nearly 1 billion ha (Cardinael et al., 2018c) and support the livelihoods of 1.2 billion 
people (Newton et al., 2022; World Bank, 2004). By integrating trees with crops in 
diverse spatial and temporal patterns, agroforestry systems provide a range of ecosystem 
services (Muhereza et al., 2020; Ntawuruhunga et al., 2023). These systems can also 
enhance human wellbeing through multiple benefits, such as enhancing food security 
(Waldron et al., 2017), improving water quality, combating climate change, and 
controlling the loss of biodiversity (Castle et al., 2022; Jose, 2009; Katila et al., 2015). 
As such, agroforestry is increasingly recognised as an important strategy for achieving 
the United Nations 2030 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) (Agroforestry 
Network, 2018).  
Agroforestry takes several forms: silvoarable systems involve intercropping woody 
perennials with annual or perennial crops; while silvopasture systems combine trees, 
woodland, or shrubs with livestock grazing (Pagella & Whistance, 2019). Other 
agroforestry practices include hedgerows, which are rows of trees or shrubs planted 
along field boundaries (Holden et al., 2019), as well as shelterbelts and wooded or 
shrubby riparian buffers. These practices can improve water quality, reduce erosion, and 
support both crop-based (silvoarable) and livestock-based (silvopastoral) farming 
systems (Tufekcioglu et al., 1999). Forested environments can also support agroforestry 
through practices such as forest grazing and forest farming, the latter involving the 
cultivation of naturally occurring speciality crops such as ornamental or medicinal 
plants within forested ecosystems (Centre for Agroforestry, 2026). In urban and peri-
[PDF PAGE 22]
2 
 
urban settings, home gardens combine trees or shrubs with vegetable production to 
maximise land productivity and biodiversity (Saha et al., 2009).  
The growing impact of climate change on agroecosystems, coupled with global efforts 
to adapt and mitigate climate change (De Stefano & Jacobson, 2020; Masson-Delmotte 
et al., 2019), has renewed interest in agroforestry as a key strategy of sustainable 
development and a land-use practice with significant potential for wider adoption in 
temperate regions. This growing recognition is reflected in international climate policy, 
with approximately 40% of the 148 Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) 
submitted by non-Annex I countries to the United Nations Framework Convention on 
Climate Change (UNFCCC) under the Paris Agreement including agroforestry as a 
climate change mitigation tool (Rosenstock et al., 2019). In total, 41% of the National 
Communications (NCs) referenced agroforestry for climate change mitigation, 43% 
referenced it for adaptation, and 25% incorporated it as a tool for both mitigation and 
adaptation (Rosenstock et al., 2019). Despite this increasing integration of agroforestry 
into climate change policy and planning, the emphasis has focused on quantifiable 
mitigation outcomes, often at the expense of documenting and evaluating adaptation 
initiatives (Mulia et al., 2020; Rosenstock et al., 2019; Zahoor et al., 2021). In addition, 
limited data on the geographic distribution of agroforestry studies limits understanding 
of its global contribution to mitigating climate change (Quandt et al., 2023a).  
Historical evidence suggests that agroforestry systems emerged alongside agricultural 
practices as early as 5,000 years ago in many parts of Asia (Mahmud et al., 2020). In 
temperate regions, however, the origins of agroforestry are closely associated with 
indigenous land management practices that integrated agricultural production with the 
use of forest resources (Bryant, 2023). Over time, agricultural practices in temperate 
zones evolved towards industrial agriculture, characterised by mechanisation, 
specialisation and the widespread adoption of monocultures.  In contrast, agroforestry 
has remained more widely adopted in tropical regions, where favourable social and 
economic conditions, alongside environmental and ecological factors, support the 
growth of woody perennials within farming systems (Mahmud et al., 2020). Adoption in 
temperate regions has been limited over the past two centuries due to a range of 
biophysical, economic, and institutional constraints. These include slower tree growth, 
increasing climatic variability and establishment risk, historical legacies of agricultural 
[PDF PAGE 23]
3 
 
intensification, economic uncertainty and delayed returns, operational complexity at the 
scale of the farm, and the absence of enabling policy frameworks and advisory services 
(Marie‑Laure, 2018; Jovanelly et al., 2025). As a result, despite growing recognition of 
its environmental and climate-related benefits, agroforestry remains less prevalent in 
temperate agricultural landscapes than in tropical regions. More recently, however, 
agroforestry adoption is increasing across temperate regions, including parts of the 
USA, Europe, Australia, New Zealand, Argentina, and East Asia, driven primarily by its 
potential to contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation (Dmuchowski et al., 
2024). However, temperate agroforestry systems generally store 10% less soil organic 
carbon (SOC) than in tropical and subtropical regions (Shi et al. 2018). In addition, Ma 
et al. (2020) reported that soil C stocks increased under agroforestry systems in both 
tropical and temperate regions; however, increases occurred more rapidly in the tropics, 
whereas temperate systems accumulated soil C more slowly and reached peak soil C 
levels later. These results suggest that time lags before soil C benefits become apparent 
may occur in both regions but are likely to be more pronounced in temperate 
agroforestry systems. 
Nevertheless, agroforestry remains a potentially important nature-based solution for 
climate change mitigation due to its capacity to sequester C in both biomass and soils.  
In addition, agroforestry may provide opportunities for participation in C credit 
schemes, subject to robust monitoring, reporting, and verification requirements. The 
prospect of generating C revenues can incentivise farmers and other land managers to 
adopt agroforestry practices and invest in long-term landscape transformation (Marland 
et al., 2001; Pradhan et al., 2023). 
Several studies have demonstrated that agroforestry systems substantially enhance C 
sequestration and are among the most effective nature-based solutions for climate 
mitigation. By integrating trees into agricultural landscapes, agroforestry increases C 
storage in both aboveground and belowground biomass and soil while creating 
favourable microclimatic conditions that improve resource efficiency potential and 
ecosystem functioning (Dhyani et al., 2021; Rehnus et al., 2013; van Noordwijk et al., 
2021; Golicz et al., 2021; Smith et al., 2008; Aerts, 1997; Griscom et al., 2017; Ma et 
al., 2020; Ntawuruhunga et al., 2023). However, the C sequestration performance of 
agroforestry systems varies considerably according to system design and environmental 
[PDF PAGE 24]
4 
 
conditions. Factors such as tree density, species selection, previous land use, tree age, 
and regional climate affect the rate and magnitude of C accumulation, resulting in 
substantial variability in aboveground biomass carbon (AGB C) and SOC stocks (Nair 
et al., 2009ab; Takimoto et al., 2009; Chatterjee et al., 2018). These sources of variation 
introduce uncertainty into estimates of C storage and sequestration potential, limiting 
the ability to predict outcomes across different agroforestry systems and regions. 
Therefore, further research is needed to reduce these uncertainties, improve 
understanding of the factors controlling C dynamics, nutrient cycling and C 
sequestration and support evidence-based agroforestry management. Such knowledge is 
essential for informing policies and incentive mechanisms that promote long-term total 
C accumulation and contribute to greenhouse gas mitigation objectives.  
In this context, this thesis aims to examine the key biotic and abiotic factors controlling 
C storage and sequestration in temperate agroforestry systems, with particular emphasis 
on the aboveground and belowground interactions that influence C and nutrient cycling. 
Given the increasing recognition of agroforestry as an important climate change 
mitigation strategy, a better understanding of the processes governing C accumulation 
and nutrient dynamics is needed to support evidence-based management, optimise 
system performance and support policy development in temperate regions. To address 
these knowledge gaps, this thesis explores the following research questions: 
1. How do AGB C stocks and annual C accumulation rates vary among poplar 
hybrids over a 30-year period, and to what extent are these variations influenced 
by local climate conditions? 
2. What effects does a 30-year-old poplar-based silvoarable system have on SOC 
storage and sequestration, and what potential contribution could the expansion 
of such systems make to achieving UK net-zero targets through enhanced C 
sequestration? 
3. How do spatial variations in soil nutrient availability and root traits interact to 
regulate belowground C inputs, nutrient cycling, and SOC accumulation within 
a silvoarable agroforestry system?  
4. How does leaf litter location (surface vs. buried) influence decomposition rates 
and the dynamics of C and nitrogen (N) content during decomposition? 
[PDF PAGE 25]
5 
 
The remainder of this introductory chapter is structured as follows. Section 1.2 provides 
an overview of the global distribution of agroforestry along with policy interventions 
that have supported its development. Section 1.3 focuses on agroforestry in the UK and 
its potential to help achieve national net-zero targets. Section 1.4 explores the 
multifunctional roles of agroforestry systems, while section 1.5 discusses the key 
challenges and barriers to their adoption. Section 1.6 reviews the C sequestration 
potential of agroforestry and examines the interactions between above and belowground 
components that influence C storage and nutrient cycling. Finally, section 1.7 presents 
the summary of research objectives followed by section 1.8 which outlines the research 
aim and objectives in relation to the identified knowledge gaps, along with an overview 
of the methodological approach and the structure of the thesis. 
1.2. Worldwide agroforestry and its global and local policy drivers 
Land plays a critical role in sustaining agrifood systems, supporting approximately 95% 
of global food production and underpinning a wide range of ecosystem services (FAO, 
2025). However, land degradation driven by human activities such as deforestation, 
overgrazing, and unsustainable agricultural practices lead to soil erosion, reduce soil 
productivity, threatens soil productivity, food security, biodiversity, and exacerbates 
climate change (FAO, 2025). Globally, 90% of deforestation is caused by agricultural 
expansion, with 49.6% attributed to cropland expansion and 38.5% to livestock grazing 
(UN, 2024). Beyond its environmental impacts, the economic costs of reduced 
ecosystem services due to land degradation vary by region, from $16,746 to $29,623 per 
km² in Oceania to $124,191 to $224,434 per km² in Asia (Carswell et al., 2025).  
Increasing global population and changing lifestyles are placing growing demands and 
pressure on food production systems. To achieve global food security, food production 
must increase by approximately 70% between 2005 and 2050 (Noel, 2015). These 
accelerating pressures can compromise soil resilience (Carswell et al., 2025) and 
contribute to the degradation of critical ecosystem services, including provisioning, 
regulating, and supporting functions, thereby exacerbating climate change (Kopittke et 
al., 2019). Conventional agricultural practices also contribute to soil degradation. For 
example, pesticides are harmful to non-target fungi, insects, and bacteria; synthetic N 
fertilisers can cause soil acidification and affect nitrification processes; deep tillage can 
[PDF PAGE 26]
6 
 
reduce fungal networks and invertebrates, leading to declines in soil organic matter and 
nutrient cycling; and plastic mulching may result in plastic residue accumulation in soil 
(Campanale et al., 2024; Goulding, 2016; Kopittke et al., 2019; D. Ma et al., 2024; 
Montràs-Janer et al., 2024; Pan et al., 2021; Schneider et al., 2017; Velásquez et al., 
2018). As a result, there is increasing interest in agricultural practices that maintain soil 
health, reduce erosion, enhance C sequestration, and promote the sustainable 
management of cropland soils. 
In this context, agroforestry is increasingly recognised as a nature-based land 
management strategy with the potential to deliver socioeconomic and ecological 
benefits to both humanity and the planet (Ellison and Wang-Erlandsson, 2019). 
Agroforestry involves the interaction among trees, shrubs, crops, and livestock, creating 
complex ecological relationships. These interactions encompass key ecological 
processes, including mutualism, commensalism, predation, and competition, which can 
have both positive and negative effects on biotic and abiotic components. For example, 
in temperate Europe, Vaccaro et al. (2023) observed potential yield losses for crops 
growing near mature trees, highlighting the importance of species selection in 
agroforestry systems. While the effects of trees on crop productivity are well 
documented, fewer studies have quantified how crop presence and management 
influence tree growth trajectories and the resulting long-term C sequestration potential 
of agroforestry systems. Consequently, uncertainties remain regarding the extent to 
which crop-tree interactions affect tree biomass accumulation and C dynamics over 
time. Since C4 grasses are generally less shade-tolerant (Taiz et al., 2015; Xie et al., 
2020), yield reductions were expected, although some shade tolerant species do exist 
(Horton & Neufeld, 1998). Therefore, site management plays a crucial role in 
minimising resource competition, thereby directly influencing plant productivity and 
maximising ecological benefits (Powell & Bork, 2004; Aschehoug et al., 2016; Swieter 
et al., 2022; Báder et al., 2023). Effective management practices, including appropriate 
tree spacing, species selection, and grazing control, can help balance competitive and 
facilitative interactions, thereby enhancing the overall productivity and resilience of 
agroforestry systems. 
However, large scale agroforestry systems have been practised globally for centuries. 
Agroforestry practices have existed in European countries since ancient times, and its 
[PDF PAGE 27]
7 
 
adoption is increasing due to the wide range of perceived socio-ecological benefits. 
These systems are mainly characterised by trees planted into pastures with livestock 
(silvopastoral) or integrated into arable land (silvoarable) (Eichhorn et al., 2006). 
Several traditional farming systems remain prevalent in Europe. For example, on Crete 
and the Aegean islands, fruit and nut trees are integrated into silvoarable systems, with 
almond trees occupying approximately 18,000 ha and fig trees covering approximately 
10,200 ha (Eichhorn et al., 2006). In Central Europe, Streubost, a traditional method of 
cultivating fruit trees in croplands, meadows, and pastures, is widely recognised and can 
be classified as either silvoarable or silvopastoral. The Mediterranean region is 
characterised by the Dehesa, an agrosilvopastoral system that historically covered 
~2,250,000 ha in southwest Spain, and the Montados in Portugal that occupied 
~896,000 ha (Castro, 2008; McAdam et al., 2008; Moreno & Pulido, 2008). The 
Montados system typically involves cultivating low-density oak trees integrated with 
silvoarable or silvopastoral land. However, due to a decline in oak regeneration and 
plantation establishment, these systems are struggling to survive (Lopes-Fernandes et 
al., 2024).  
The European Parliament has supported the adoption of agroforestry practices through 
the Common Agricultural Policy (CAP), mainly under rural development measures. 
Under this framework, farmers can receive per-hectare payments for establishing and 
maintaining agroforestry systems on their land (Nie et al., 2016). Data from the 
AgForward project estimated that the EU27 contains approximately 15.4 million ha of 
agroforestry, representing approximately 9% of the usable agricultural area and 3.6% of 
the total land area; when reindeer husbandry systems are included, this figure increases 
to 52.4 million ha (Augère-Granier, 2020). Of the 15.4 million ha classified as 
agroforestry, only 35,800 ha are categorised as silvoarable systems, whereas ~15.1 
million ha consists of various forms of livestock-based agroforestry (Augère-Granier, 
2020). This disparity highlights the dominance of silvopastoral and other livestock-
integrated systems across Europe and suggests considerable potential for expanding 
silvoarable agroforestry. As concerns regarding climate change mitigation and 
adaptation continue to grow, the European Parliament has actively promoted 
agroforestry practices; however, significant untapped potential remains for expanding 
its adoption and integration across the region (McNie et al., 2016). 
[PDF PAGE 28]
8 
 
Agroforestry gained formal recognition as a climate change mitigation and adaptation 
policy following the Kyoto Protocol and has subsequently been incorporated into major 
international frameworks. These include the Reducing Emissions from Deforestation 
and Forest Degradation (REDD+) mechanisms, the UNFCCC Paris Agreement, the 
Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the United Nations Convention to Combat 
Desertification (UNCCD), and the United Nations SDGs. It has also gained support 
from international organisations such as the Food and Agriculture Organisation (FAO) 
and the World Bank (Dhyani et al., 2021; Meybeck et al., 2022; Rehnus et al., 2013; 
Meine van Noordwijk, 2019).  
Growing international awareness of the role of agroforestry is reflected in recent climate 
assessments and policy reports. For example, Harrison and Gassner (2020) noted the 
role of trees on agricultural land received increased attention in the Intergovernmental 
Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Working Group III report and earlier land-use 
assessments (Calvo Buendia et al., 2019). In addition, agroforestry is increasingly 
referenced in National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) and NDCs, further highlighting its 
perceived importance for C sequestration and climate change mitigation (Meybeck et 
al., 2020; Adaptation Fund, 2025).  
National greenhouse gas inventories and global modelling studies estimate 
anthropogenic CO₂ emissions from land use at around 5.5 Gt CO₂ per year (IPCC 
Working Group III, Climate Change 2022). Yet discrepancies associated with 
measurement methods and reporting methodology introduce uncertainty into these 
estimates (IPCC Working Group III, Climate Change 2022). Agroforestry has therefore 
been promoted as an effective land-use strategy for mitigating these emissions by 
integrating trees into agricultural landscapes, thereby minimising the environmental 
impacts associated with conventional agriculture while also delivering ecological, socio-
economic, and environmental benefits (Jovanelly et al., 2025; Smith et al., 2020).  
Despite increasing policy support, important knowledge gaps remain regarding the 
magnitude and variability of C sequestration across different agroforestry systems.  
Currently available scientific evidence is often fragmented, system specific, and 
geographically constrained, limiting its usefulness for large-scale planning and policy 
design. Consequently, there is a need for more comprehensive quantitative and 
[PDF PAGE 29]
9 
 
qualitative assessments of C sequestration, alongside long-term monitoring across 
diverse environmental conditions. Enhancing the availability of comparative datasets 
would strengthen evidence-based decision making (Quandt et al., 2023).  
1.3. Agroforestry in the United Kingdom 
Agroforestry in the UK has transitioned from a traditional land-management practice to 
a research-led approach to sustainable agriculture and environmental restoration. 
Historically, integrated tree-livestock systems were widely practised through methods 
such as pannage, pollarding and woodland grazing, which provided a range of benefits 
including timber, fodder, fuel and food production. These practices developed in the 
early modern era into more structured systems, such as orchards, shelterbelts and 
parkland landscapes (Smith, 2010). However, throughout the 20th century, agricultural 
intensification significantly altered the British landscape. Efforts to increase crop yield 
resulted in the removal of trees from fields to provide machinery access, while the 
expansion of agricultural land following the Second World War and the introduction of 
the 1947 Agriculture Act contributed to substantial losses of semi-natural habitats 
(Hooftman and Bullock, 2012; Best and Coppack, 1992).  
The simplification of farmland landscapes continued during the second half of the 20th 
century, when extensive hedgerow removal was undertaken to create larger fields (R. A. 
Robinson & Sutherland, 2002). Since 1947, it has been estimated that approximately 
50% of the UK’s hedgerows have been lost (CPRE, 1999). Despite recent agri-
environment schemes aimed at promoting hedgerow recreation and restoration (Defra, 
2005), the Countryside Survey reported that the length of managed hedgerows has 
continued to decrease by 6.2% between 1998 and 2007 (Countryside Survey, 2008).  
These changes have contributed to biodiversity loss, habitat fragmentation, and 
reductions in ecosystem service provision, prompting renewed interest in agroforestry 
as a means of reintegrating trees into agricultural landscapes while supporting both 
productivity and environmental sustainability. 
The limited adoption of agroforestry in the UK is primarily due to a combination of 
economic, policy and knowledge-based barriers. Farmers often report insufficient 
understanding of agroforestry practices, limited financial support for both adoption and 
[PDF PAGE 30]
10 
 
long-term maintenance, and a lack of awareness of the economic benefits and diverse 
income streams that agroforestry can provide (Farming Forum, 2022; Tranchina et al., 
2024). A major barrier is the long-perceived lead time before trees generate an income. 
In addition, tenancy-related issues such as the need for landowner approval for tree 
planting and the reluctance of tenants to invest in long-term land improvements further 
reinforce negative perceptions among farmers, many of whom view agroforestry as 
offering low financial returns (Lawrence and Dandy, 2014; Valatin et al., 2016). 
Integrating trees into arable and livestock systems can also create practical management 
challenges, including interfering with machinery access and farming operations and 
constraints on future land-use opportunities (Felton et al., 2023).  
Rising establishment and maintenance costs emphasise the need for financial incentives, 
through planting grants and ongoing management payments. Such support is likely to be 
essential if the UK is to meet its net‑zero target, which relies on large-scale tree planting 
and increased tree cover across agricultural landscapes by 2050. Therefore, many of the 
barriers currently limiting agroforestry adoption also represent opportunities for policy 
interventions and the wider implementation of agroforestry systems as a climate 
mitigation strategy. (Kendon et al., 2023; Hanlon et al., 2021; Velásquez et al., 2018; 
Pan et al., 2021).  
Climate change has further increased interest in agroforestry across the UK. The Met 
Office reports that the UK is experiencing wetter, sunnier, and warmer weather, while 
the frequency and severity of extreme events such as heatwaves, floods, and storms are 
increasing (Kendon et al., 2023). These changes are affecting the UK’s agricultural 
sector (Hanlon et al., 2021). High rainfall negatively impacts agriculture by causing soil 
nutrient loss and creating waterlogged conditions, which can lead to water-related 
diseases (Velásquez et al., 2018; Pan et al., 2021). As a result, agroforestry is 
increasingly being recognised as a nature-based solution that can provide multiple 
benefits, such as flood mitigation, improved water regulation, reduced soil erosion, and 
increased ecosystem stability, highlighting its potential to address climate change 
adaptation and mitigation (Woodland Trust, 2020; Judson et al., 2026).  
To maximise these benefits, a range of modern agroforestry systems have been 
developed to enhance land productivity while maintaining sustainable resource use. In 
[PDF PAGE 31]
11 
 
the UK, the main agroforestry systems include silvoarable, silvopastoral, hedgerows and 
home gardens (mixed tree-crop systems around homesteads), all of which aim to deliver 
both ecological and socioeconomic benefits (den Herder et al., 2017). Research and 
development in this field have been supported through several important initiatives, 
such as the establishment of the silvopastoral National Network Experiment in the 
1980s, the National Silvoarable Network in 1992 (Hislop and Claridge, 2000), Martin 
Wolfe’s organic silvoarable site (1994) and Stephen Briggs’ large-scale apple-based 
silvoarable farm established in 2009 (Smith, 2010).  
Tree species selection remains a critical component of successful agroforestry design. 
Broadleaf species such as cherry (Prunus spp.), oak (Quercus spp.), hazel (Corylus 
avellana), and field maple (Acer campestre) are among the most used because of their 
high nut and timber values (Forestry Commission, 2000). Poplar is also widely utilised 
for its rapid growth and economic benefits (Burgess et al., 2003). However, research has 
demonstrated that crop yields may decline in a competitive zone surrounding trees, with 
the magnitude of the effects varying according to crop type and tree species (Burgess et 
al., 2003., Vaccaro et al., 2023). Competition for light, water, and nutrients can also 
influence tree growth, particularly during establishment, with implications for long-term 
biomass accumulation and C sequestration. Hence, careful tree species selection, 
appropriate tree spacing, and effective site management are crucial for minimising crop 
competition and optimising overall system yields (Forestry Commission, 2002). 
In 2020, the UK was the first major economy to legally commit to a net-zero emissions 
target to address climate change. However, current efforts remain insufficient to meet 
these targets (Climate Change Committee, 2022), particularly in the agrifood sector. To 
remain on track, the country must reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 81% from 1990 
levels by 2035 (UK Climate Action Tracker, 2025). While the adoption of green energy 
and more efficient infrastructure is important, they are unlikely to deliver the required 
reductions alone. Large-scale implementation of nature-based solutions that increase C 
sequestration across agricultural landscapes will also be necessary. 
Agroforestry has been identified as a key component of this transition. Achieving 
national net-zero objectives is expected to require that approximately 10% of UK 
farmland be managed under agroforestry systems (Burgess and Graves, 2022). 
[PDF PAGE 32]
12 
 
However, agroforestry currently occupies approximately 550,000 ha of the UK, 
representing about 3.3% of all agricultural land (Defra, 2025). Silvoarable agroforestry 
is rare, representing only 0.05% of the total arable area (den Herder et al., 2017). 
According to Burgess & Graves (2022), if 20% of the UK’s arable land were converted 
to agroforestry, net-zero arable farming could be achieved by 2048. Additionally, 
converting 30% of pastures to silovopasture would be necessary to reach the overall 
targets by 2051 (Burgess & Graves, 2022). Such changes could reduce net agricultural 
emissions by 84% in arable systems and 80% in livestock systems (Burgess and Graves, 
2022).  
Despite growing evidence that agroforestry can enhance C sequestration within UK 
agricultural landscapes, important knowledge gaps remain regarding: (i) the 
quantification of soil and biomass C stocks and sequestration over time; (ii) nutrient 
cycling and belowground interactions; (iii) the long-term C sequestration potential of 
different agroforestry systems across environmental conditions; and (iv) trade-offs and 
synergies between C storage and agricultural productivity. Addressing these gaps will 
improve the evidence base for climate-smart land management. 
1.4. Key functions of agroforestry  
Agroforestry ecosystems provide a diverse range of ecological functions that 
collectively contribute to valuable ecosystem services (Fig. 1.1). Although agroforestry 
practices vary according to their main purpose and whether the trees are mixed with 
crops or livestock, all systems offer significant environmental benefits. As shown in 
Fig. 1.1, agroforestry systems facilitate provisioning, regulating, supporting, and 
cultural ecosystem services by enhancing environmental resilience. Trees can be planted 
in several differing configurations: (i) as windbreaks to reduce wind erosion and protect 
crops (Smith et al., 2021); (ii) along field boundaries as hedgerows; (iii) around mature 
trees to increase biomass accumulation; (iv) with shrubs and woodlands to increase 
habitat diversity (Brown et al., 2018); and (v) adjacent to water bodies as riparian 
buffers to improve water quality. Each of these agroforestry configurations provides 
various ecological and socioeconomic advantages.  
 
[PDF PAGE 33]
13 
 
1.4.1. Provisioning services  
Agroforestry systems increase provisioning services by supplying a diverse range of 
products, such as food crops, fruits, fuelwood, timber, fibre, fodder, biofuels, and 
medicinal products (Awazi et al., 2025b). By integrating trees with crops and/or 
livestock, farmers can generate multiple revenue streams from annual crop production, 
fruit harvests, fodder, and timber production over short-, medium-, and long-term time 
horizons (Wiggering et al., 2006; Mukhlis et al., 2022). However, agroforestry systems 
generally produce a higher net present value than conventional monoculture systems, 
resulting in higher farm profitability and economic resilience (Báder et al., 2023; 
Atapattu et al., 2024). Agroforestry also offers additional income-generating 
opportunities through C markets and C credit schemes. However, significant knowledge 
gaps remain regarding the magnitude and variability of provisioning services delivered 
by agroforestry systems and the trade-offs between provisioning benefits and other 
ecosystem services across different agroforestry configurations (Báder et al., 2023). 
1.4.2.  Regulating services  
Agroforestry systems play a pivotal role in the provision of regulating ecosystem 
services, in particular, climate regulation, water purification, pest control, and disease 
resistance (Fig. 1.1). There is significant evidence regarding the greater potential of C 
sequestration in agroforestry systems compared with monoculture practices due to 
higher AGB and BGB production (Tumwebaze & Byakagaba, 2016; Vigulu et al., 
2019; Schneidewind et al., 2019; Guillot et al., 2021; Bibi et al., 2022). However, the 
magnitude and rate of C sequestration are strongly dependent on factors such as system 
design, species composition, soil type, climate, and temporal scale considered (Lorenz 
& Lal, 2014; Cardinael et al., 2018b).  
Agroforestry can also contribute to pest regulation by interrupting pest life cycles and 
enhancing natural biological control, thereby reducing reliance on chemical pesticides 
(Muhammad Ahmad et al., 2024). However, such effects are often context specific. 
Trees may also provide alternative hosts for pests (Schroth et al., 2000), while increased 
shade, higher humidity, and reduced airflow may create favourable conditions for some 
[PDF PAGE 34]
14 
 
pests and plant diseases, possibly sustaining or increasing pesticide use (Staton et al., 
2018). 
Hydrological regulation is another key ecosystem service that agroforestry systems may 
provide. Trees can increase infiltration rates, enhance soil water storage, improve water 
quality, and buffer storm events by reducing runoff and nutrient loss (Idris & Mahrup, 
2017; Welsh et al., 2018). However, the effectiveness of these outcomes depends 
strongly on climatic, geomorphological, spatial layout, and biological conditions (Seobi 
et al., 2005; Akdemir Eda et al., 2016; Chandler et al., 2018; Judson et al., 2026).  
1.4.3.  Supporting services  
Trees and woodlands in agricultural landscapes promote habitat heterogeneity that 
supports key ecosystem functions, such as primary production, soil formation, and 
nutrient cycling. Agroforestry systems generally provide greater capacity for alpha-, 
beta-, and gamma-level biodiversity than simplified agricultural systems (Santos et al., 
2022). However, biodiversity responses are uneven across agroecosystems and depend 
heavily on species composition, landscape context, and management intensity (Daleo et 
al., 2023; Vanneste et al., 2024).  
The higher structural complexity characteristic of agroforestry systems may improve 
both biomass production and soil health. Appropriate combinations of tree and crop 
species can achieve 20–80% greater total biomass by optimising resource use, such as 
enhanced light capture and the maintenance of favourable microclimatic conditions 
(Rijal et al., 2025). Nevertheless, increased biomass accumulation may also result in 
trade-offs. Competition between trees and crops for light, nutrients, and water may 
reduce crop yields, while elevated soil respiration may lower net C storage (Swieter et 
al., 2022). In conclusion, the benefits of agroforestry systems for supporting ecosystem 
services are not universal and their effectiveness remains highly context-dependent and 
management-dependent. 
[PDF PAGE 35]
15 
 
 
Figure 1.1 Classification of agroforestry ecosystem services based on the Millennium 
Ecosystem Assessment framework showing the contribution of agroforestry systems to 
providing provisioning, regulating, supporting, and cultural services (an original figure 
adapted from ideas in MEA, 2025; Jose, 2009; Mlambo et al., 2025; Mathieu et al., 
2025). 
1.4.4.  Cultural services  
Agroforestry landscapes contribute to cultural services by supporting aesthetic values, 
cultural identity and traditional knowledge systems of land stewardship, with 
gender‑differentiated roles and practices (Jose, 2009). Such multifunctional landscapes 
can enhance visual appeal, provide recreational opportunities, encourage tree planting 
and sustain the culture of interactions between people and agroecosystems (Fagerholm 
et al., 2019; Torralba et al., 2016). Agroforestry systems may also facilitate gender 
equality through increasing women's access to productive resources, participation in 
decision‑making opportunities, and engagement with culturally valued knowledge 
related to tree‑based systems, especially in smallholder and subsistence farming 
contexts (Doss et al., 2018; Meinzen-Dick et al., 2014). 
1.5. Challenges and research needed in temperate agroforestry   
Agroforestry is increasingly acknowledged as a nature-based solution that can 
contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation, biodiversity conservation and 
resilient agricultural production. Many countries have integrated agroforestry into 
climate and land-use strategies, while the success of agroforestry systems has still been 

[PDF PAGE 36]
16 
 
highly dependent on ecological, economic and policy factors (Fig. 1.2) (Tranchina et al., 
2024; Felton et al., 2024). 
 
Figure 1.2 Barriers to establishment of agroforestry systems, categorised into three 
broad groups: environmental and technical, socioeconomic, and policy-related factors 
(an original figure based on concepts presented in Tranchina et al., 2024) 
Climate variation in temperate regions, including changing precipitation patterns and 
higher temperatures, may affect tree growth and crop productivity, making the long-
term performance of agroforestry systems uncertain (McRae, 2024; Jovanelly et al., 
2025). Species selection and system design are important, as poorly designed systems 
may create competition for light, water and nutrients between trees and crops. However, 
appropriate tree spacing and alley widths can reduce these trade-offs while maintaining 
ecosystem benefits (Felton et al., 2024). 
Economic and policy factors also affect adoption. While agroforestry can provide 
additional income streams through timber and non-timber products, financial returns are 
often realised over longer time periods for farmers in the process of establishing the 
system (Felton et al., 2023). In Europe, implementation may also be hampered by the 
administrative complexity of agri-environment schemes and CAP regulations (Augère-
Granier, 2020; Sollen-Norrlin et al., 2020). 

[PDF PAGE 37]
17 
 
Despite increasing policy support and recognition of agroforestry's climate mitigation 
potential, significant knowledge gaps remain regarding the extent and distribution of C 
stocks in temperate agroforestry systems, particularly belowground. Uncertainties in 
soil and root C pools, C sequestration rates and nutrient cycling processes make it 
challenging to accurately assess climate impacts and inform policy decisions (Jha, 2018; 
Biffi et al., 2022; Judson et al., 2024). Thus, addressing these knowledge gaps is crucial 
for better accounting of C and for the evidence-based adoption of agroforestry. 
Thus, this thesis will focus on above- and belowground C stocks, C sequestration rates, 
nutrient cycling, and root characteristics, including root biomass density, root length 
density, root C stocks, and root C and nitrogen concentrations, in temperate agroforestry 
systems. These components are examined to better understand the mechanisms by 
which agroforestry contributes to climate change mitigation and long-term ecosystem 
sustainability. 
1.6.  Carbon stocks, sequestration and nutrient cycling in agroforestry 
systems  
1.6.1.  Carbon stocks and sequestration rate 
In recent years, there has been a growing interest in the climate change mitigation 
potential of introducing woody species into agricultural landscapes via agroforestry 
(Judson et al., 2023). Trees capture carbon dioxide (CO2) through photosynthesis and 
store it as C in AGB, belowground biomass (BGB) and SOC pools (Báder et al., 2023). 
Agroforestry systems can therefore contribute to C sequestration through various 
pathways. Carbon sequestration refers to the transfer of atmospheric C into long-term 
terrestrial reservoirs (USGS, 2023; Nair et al., 2021bc), and in agroforestry, this occurs 
through the accumulation of woody biomass, litter inputs, root turnover, and 
rhizodeposition occurs through the accumulation of woody biomass, such as AGB 
(trees' main stem, branches, leaves) or BGB (leaf litter inputs, deposition, complex root 
turnover and rhizodeposition. These processes enhance SOC through the decomposition 
of litter and woody residues and incorporation of root-derived C into the soil (Awazi et 
al., 2025b). The detailed mechanisms of C sequestration are discussed in sections 1.6.2–
1.6.4.  
[PDF PAGE 38]
18 
 
The soil C pool is a critical edaphic component that regulates ecosystem structure and 
function, containing approximately 2300 Pg of C in the top 1 m of soil, including 1500 
Pg (1015 g) of organic and 750 Pg of inorganic C (Batjes, 1996; Munoz-Rojas et al., 
2012). This reservoir exceeds both the atmospheric C pool (770 Pg) and the vegetation 
C pool (610 Pg) (Nair et al., 2021b; Canadel & Monteiro, 2021). However, land-use 
change is a key driver of variation in terrestrial C stocks (Yu & Song, 2023), while soil 
CO2 emissions from both human and natural activities make soils the second-largest 
source of CO2 globally (Le Quéré et al., 2009; Van Der Werf et al., 2009). Agroforestry 
practices can mitigate greenhouse gas emissions by increasing C storage in biomass and 
soils, thereby reducing atmospheric CO₂ levels. Where sequestration exceeds system 
emissions, agroforestry may function as a net C sink (Baah-Acheamfour et al., 2017).  
The global removal potential of agroforestry has been estimated at approximately 0.1–
5.7 Gt CO2 yr⁻¹ (Mlambo et al., 2025). Furthermore, increased tree cover on agricultural 
land increased global biomass C stocks by >2 Pg C between 2000 and 2010, while 
further expansion could add an additional ~4–6 Pg C (incremental adoption) to ~12–19 
Pg C (systemic adoption) (Zomer et al.,2016; Zomer et al., 2022). Although temperate 
regions generally contain higher AGB C stocks than tropical regions, C sequestration 
rates are typically greater in tropical regions due to faster tree growth (Mazumder et al., 
2025). In addition to biomass C storage, agroforestry can substantially increase SOC 
stocks. Global meta-analyses of SOC stocks by Shi et al. (2018) and Lorenz and Lal 
(2014) indicated that agroforestry systems could store an additional ~5.3 Pg C in SOC 
worldwide. Reported SOC sequestration rates typically range from ~0.2–0.4 Mg C ha⁻¹ 
yr⁻¹, depending on climate, agroforestry system type, stand age, and baseline SOC 
levels. In temperate regions, Mayer et al. (2022) reviewed 63 observations and found 
that hedgerows and silvoarable systems exhibited the highest SOC sequestration rates, 
particularly in topsoil, ranging from 0.2–0.3 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹. Overall, these findings 
highlight the importance of considering both aboveground and belowground pathways 
in determining the overall C sequestration potential of agroforestry systems.
