Table 1.1 shows regional variations in total biomass C stocks (Pg C) on agricultural 
land in 2000 and 2010, based on observed changes in tree cover derived from remote 
sensing. Baseline C stock (11.08 Pg C) represents the estimated biomass C stored in 
agricultural land excluding additional C stored by tree cover. Tropical regions exhibited 
the largest absolute increases in biomass C stocks between 2000 and 2010, reflecting 
their greater potential for rapid biomass accumulation. In contrast, temperate areas, 
including large parts of Europe, showed smaller absolute changes in total biomass C 
stocks. However, when total biomass C stocks are expressed on an area basis (Mg C ha-
1), temperate agricultural areas often contain relatively high biomass C densities. This 
indicates that temperate agroforestry systems may already contain substantial woody 
biomass stocks, with limited scope for rapid increases in C stocks compared with 
regions with lower baseline C stocks. 
[PDF PAGE 40]
20 
 
Sequestration rates generally decrease with stand age and may approach saturation over 
time (Nair et al., 2010). Soil C accumulation is also very heterogeneous, commonly 
localised in surface horizons and strongly influenced by soil properties, agroforestry 
design, climate, and previous land use, making large-scale upscaling using limited data 
a challenge and potentially associated with large uncertainties (Cardinael et al., 2018c). 
In temperate regions, these challenges are exacerbated by the limited availability of 
long-term datasets and inconsistent reporting of belowground C pools and fluxes, such 
as fine-root turnover and rhizodeposition, which are important components of the 
overall C balance (Cardinael et al., 2018b; Meybeck et al., 2022).  
As such, while agroforestry represents a promising nature-based solution for combating 
climate change, uncertainties in C accounting, long-term permanence, saturation effects, 
and landscape-scale implementation highlight the need for cautious interpretation of 
sequestration estimates and their integration into broader emissions-reduction and land-
management strategies. Despite the high net C removal potential of agroforestry 
systems, considerable uncertainty persists regarding the scalability, permanence, and 
reliability of these estimates. Current projections of CO2 removal (0.1–5.7 Gt CO2yr⁻¹) 
are predicated on widespread adoption, sustained long-term management, and stable 
climatic conditions, all of which may be inapplicable (Osei & Oelbermann, 2025; Shi et 
al., 2018; Zomer et al., 2022). Additionally, C accumulation in both biomass and soil is 
not indefinite.  
1.6.2.  Factors influencing carbon sequestration potential in agroforestry  
Fig. 1.3 illustrates the key biological and management-related factors that regulate C 
sequestration in agroforestry systems, as well as the major pathways through which 
atmospheric C is incorporated into vegetation and soil C pools. The subsequent sections 
focus on how agroforestry system design and management (Section 1.6.2.1) and 
climatic conditions (Section 1.6.2.2) affect the accumulation and long-term storage of C 
in vegetation and soils. 
[PDF PAGE 41]
21 
 
 
Figure 1.3 Conceptual framework illustrating the key factors affecting C sequestration 
in agroforestry systems 
1.6.2.1. Configuration and management of agroforestry systems  
Tree density and the spatial arrangement of trees within agricultural landscapes also 
influence C sequestration potential by affecting biomass production, belowground C 
allocation, and resource competition (Alexander et al., 2025). Under conditions of 
limited soil resources, trees modify the biomass, morphology, and chemical 
composition of fine roots to enhance nutrient acquisition. This response may be 
particularly pronounced in systems with lower tree densities, where individual trees 
have greater access to available resources (Bo et al., 2018). Increasing tree density can 
increase stand-level biomass C storage; however, it may also reduce understory crop 
productivity due to intensified competition for light, water, and nutrients. For example, 
poplar clones grown in wide alleys within temperate silvoarable systems in Italy 
exhibited faster growth rates than those in densely planted poplar plantations, where 
inter-tree competition constrained biomass accumulation (Piotto et al., 2024). 
Collectively, these findings demonstrate that both spatial configuration and structural 
management are key determinants of biomass dynamics and, consequently, the C 
sequestration capacity of agroforestry systems. 

[PDF PAGE 42]
22 
 
Management practices have a profound impact on C sequestration by regulating 
biomass production, SOC inputs, and decomposition processes (Fig. 1.3). Species 
selection, pruning intensity, and tree density influence AGB and BGB accumulation and 
consequently the amount of C captured and stored in vegetation. Pruning can promote 
compensatory growth and improve biomass turnover; however, repeated or intensive 
pruning can reduce woody biomass and limit long-term C storage (Fig.1.3). For 
example, Axe et al. (2017) report that untrimmed hedges stored significantly more AGB 
C (42.0 ± 3.78 t C ha⁻¹) than trimmed hedges, indicating the trade-off between structural 
management and C sequestration.  
Enhanced productivity and greater biomass inputs to soil can also result indirectly from 
management practices, such as manure or fertilisation, especially within alley cropping 
systems, where crop yields are often improved. However, over-application of fertiliser 
may negatively impact soil health through acidification or salinisation, contributing to 
land degradation and potentially offsetting long-term gains in C sequestration (Fig. 1.3; 
Hijbeek et al., 2019; Liu et al., 2023). Litterfall and pruning residues represent major 
pathways of C transfer to the soil. Their contribution to SOC depends on the amount, its 
quality and chemical composition (e.g., lignin and nitrogen content), as well as 
environmental and management conditions that regulate the rate of decomposition 
(Gartner and Cardon, 2004). Moreover, rhizodeposition, fine root turnover, and soil 
aggregate formation all contribute substantially to SOC accumulation (Reid and Goss, 
1982; Nguyen, 2009). However, increased organic matter inputs can also stimulate 
microbial activity and accelerate CO2 release through priming effects, partially 
counteracting SOC gains. 
1.6.2.2. Climatic factors 
Climatic factors, particularly temperature and precipitation, strongly influence SOC 
sequestration rates by regulating primary productivity, litter inputs, and decomposition 
processes. A meta-analysis conducted in China found that shelterbelts and silvoarable 
systems enhanced soil C storage (both topsoil and subsoil), with the magnitude of the 
effects primarily influenced by annual mean temperature, precipitation, land use, and 
soil class; subtropical zones showed the highest SOC storage capacity (Hübner et al. 
2021). Similar findings were reported in other meta-analyses by Chatterjee et al. (2018), 
[PDF PAGE 43]
23 
 
Feliciano et al. (2018), and Shi et al. (2018) for temperate and tropical regions, which 
showed that favourable moisture availability and thermal conditions, combined with 
greater system complexity, generally enhanced SOC stocks relative to less diverse land 
use systems. However, uncertainties persist due to variations in land management 
practices, soil types, plant species, and climatic conditions, which affect the C 
sequestration potential of agroforestry. De Stefano and Jacobson (2018) further 
highlighted methodological challenges, including the lack of standardised sampling 
protocols, variable sampling procedures, inconsistent study designs, and insufficient 
reporting of variance estimators and explanatory variables. These limitations hinder 
precise quantification of climate-driven changes in SOC.  
Biomass allocation among leaves, stems, and roots reflects the balance between 
aboveground and belowground resource acquisition, enabling vegetation to adapt to 
varying environmental conditions (Zhang et al., 2023). The distribution of biomass is 
influenced by the availability of essential resources such as light, atmospheric CO₂, 
water, and soil nutrients, which collectively determine plant growth strategies under 
different climatic regimes. Climatic variables including mean annual temperature, 
annual precipitation, soil temperature, sunshine duration, growing degree days, and the 
frequency of ground and air frost significantly affect biomass accumulation, leaf area 
development, DBH, canopy cover, and overall tree growth. Temperature and 
precipitation are recognised as major drivers of AGB production because they regulate 
biogeochemical processes such as photosynthesis, respiration, and water availability 
(Ma et al., 2023; Yao et al., 2022). The presence of multiple tree and crop components 
may create diverse microclimates, improve resource-use efficiency, enhance soil 
moisture retention, and buffer the impacts of environmental extremes. Consequently, 
agroforestry systems could potentially better sustain tree growth and biomass 
production under adverse climatic conditions than monocultures (Quandt et al., 2023b; 
Ntawuruhunga et al., 2023). 
1.6.3.  Interactions between aboveground and belowground carbon sources 
Aboveground and belowground C pools in agroforestry systems are closely linked 
through continuous C transfer processes that regulate ecosystem productivity and long-
term C storage. Carbon fixed through photosynthesis is initially allocated to AGB, 
[PDF PAGE 44]
24 
 
including stems, branches, and foliage, where approximately 50% of the dry biomass is 
represented as C (Nair et al., 2012; Nair et al., 2021abc; Shepherd & Montagnini, 2001). 
The amount of C accumulated in AGB varies depending on species composition, stand 
age, site characteristics, and management practices (Nair, 2009a). 
The interaction between aboveground and belowground C pools occurs through several 
pathways including, litterfall, pruning residues, root turnover, and dissolved organic C 
movement through the soil profile.  These fluxes regulate the transfer of C and nutrients 
from vegetation to soil and are therefore key determinants of soil organic carbon (SOC) 
accumulation in agroforestry systems (Ramos et al., 2018). Litter quantity and quality 
strongly influence decomposition rates, microbial processing and subsequent C 
stabilisation. In particular, litter chemistry, including concentrations of lignin, nitrogen, 
tannins, and polyphenols, influences microbial activity and the balance between nutrient 
mineralisation and immobilisation, with implications for long-term C stabilisation 
(Cusack et al., 2009).  
Carbon storage within agroforestry systems is also influenced by species-specific 
growth and allocation strategies. Fast-growing tree species can accumulate substantial 
amount of C is stored in AGB, although C losses also occur simultaneously through soil 
respiration and root-associated microbial activities (Bae et al., 2013). Variations in 
wood C concentration and biomass allocation across species and developmental stages 
further affect overall C storage. Climatic conditions also influence these processes; for 
example, the rapid turnover of soil organic matter under warm, humid tropical 
conditions may limit soil C accumulation compared to temperate systems (Shepherd and 
Montagnini, 2001; Nair et al., 2009b). Collectively, these factors highlight the complex, 
species- and climate-dependent relationship between AGB production and belowground 
C storage in agroforestry systems. 
Balanced nutrient supply is essential for sustaining ecosystem services, and plant 
species selection plays a central role in regulating nutrient cycling rates in agroforestry 
systems (Kopittke et al., 2019). Compared with conventional agriculture systems, 
agroforestry can enhance nutrient turnover through increased biomass production and 
decomposition, which replenish C and nutrient soil pools (Sanchez, 1995; Rhoades, 
1997; Khanna, 1997; Young, 1997; Nair et al., 2021bc). Integrating functionally diverse 
[PDF PAGE 45]
25 
 
species, particularly nitrogen (N)-fixing trees can further improve nutrient availability. 
For example, Sharma et al. (1994) demonstrated that combing N-fixing Alnus with 
non-N-fixing trees and cardamom enhanced nutrient cycling in the Eastern Himalaya. 
Tree species also differ in their contribution to nutrient return via litter decomposition, 
with species such as Dalbergia sissoo and Azadirachta indica promoting the 
replenishment of nitrogen phosphorus and potassium (Bisht et al., 2003; Hasauzzaman 
& Hossain, 2015; Hossain et al., 2011). Poplar-based agroforestry systems have 
similarly been identified as sustainable options for maintaining soil nutrient status (Das 
& Chaturvedi, 2005; Tzec-Gamboa et al., 2023).  
Nutrient cycling in agroforestry remains inherently complex due to interactions among 
trees, crops, soil biota and environmental conditions. In temperate regions, nutrient 
dynamics and crop responses are influenced by shading intensity and local 
environmental conditions (Chirko et al., 1996; Dufour et al., 2013; Artru et al., 2017), 
while management practices, such as tree spacing and species composition strongly 
influence nutrient cycling (Nair et al., 1995). Agroforestry systems can modify soil 
biological communities increasing bacterial and earthworm abundance and modifying 
fungal assemblages (Vaupel et al., 2023). Furthermore, soil biodiversity and understory 
vegetation diversity contribute to ecosystem multifunctionality and stability, reinforcing 
the ecological benefits of agroforestry relative to monocultures (Jiao et al., 2022; Meyer 
et al., 2018; Wang et al., 2023a).  
Belowground processes play a critical role in regulating nutrient cycling, nutrient 
retention, and C sequestration in agroforestry systems. Trees access nutrients from 
deeper soil horizons through extensive root systems, while nutrient release from organic 
residues is often accelerated during periods of high moisture availability (Hailu & 
Estifanos, 2023). Longer-term nutrient retention depends on root–soil interactions, 
mineral weathering and geochemical cycling, particularly in deeper soil layers where 
nutrient losses are reduced (Issac and Borden, 2019). Two mechanisms are particularly 
important in agroforestry systems: biological nutrient pumping, whereby trees 
translocate nutrients from deeper soil layers to surface horizons via litterfall, and the 
biological safety net effect, whereby tree roots capture nutrients that would otherwise be 
lost through leaching (Nair et al., 2021b). Nitrogen dynamics are particularly influential 
in shaping nutrient cycling and productivity in temperate agroforestry systems. Tree N 
[PDF PAGE 46]
26 
 
uptake is regulated by site conditions, competition, biomass production, and edge 
effects, all of which affect nutrient acquisition and crop performance (Udawatta & Jose, 
2012; Gamble et al., 2016). Plants exhibit adaptive nutrient capture strategies in 
response to spatial and temporal variability in soil resources (Lambers et al., 2008). 
These responses are strongly modulated by soil microbial communities, which mediate 
nutrient transformations and retention. For example, Beule et al. (2020) reported 
enhanced abundances of microbial groups (Acidobacteria, Actinobacteria, alpha and 
gamma proteobacteria, Firmicutes, and Verrucomicrobia) associated with nutrient 
cycling and denitrification in poplar-based agroforestry systems than in monocultures, 
suggesting enhanced potential for N retention and reduced nutrient losses.  
The interaction between aboveground and belowground C pools can be further 
enhanced by landscape features such as hedgerows, grass strips, and tree strips. These 
components increase AGB production while simultaneously promoting the transfer of 
organic C to the soil through litterfall, root turnover, and rhizodeposition, thereby 
contributing to nutrient retention and C sequestration in agricultural landscapes (Falloon 
et al., 2004). Carbon allocated belowground supports root growth, nodule development, 
and rhizodeposition, which contribute organic matter and biologically fixed N to the 
soil. However, important knowledge gaps remain regarding the pathways through which 
C and nutrients are transferred, transformed, and retained within agroforestry systems, 
limiting the ability to optimise system design and management (Nygren et al., 2012). 
Roots represent the largest component of BGB, typically accounting for 20–40% of a 
tree’s total biomass, and are increasingly recognised as a major contributor to long-term 
C storage. Root derived C can be stabilised through interactions with soil minerals, 
microbial communities and soil aggregates, often exhibiting longer residence times than 
aboveground litter inputs (Lorenz & Lal, 2014). Studies in agroforestry systems have 
shown that root-derived C inputs can equal or exceed those that are derived from 
aboveground biomass, highlighting the importance of root turnover and rhizodeposition 
as drivers of soil C sequestration (Jackson et al., 1997; Dhillon & Van Rees, 2016). 
Consequently, understanding root dynamics is essential for evaluating the long-term 
capacity of agroforestry systems to sequester C, maintain soil fertility, and enhance 
ecosystem resilience (Nair et al., 2009ab).  
[PDF PAGE 47]
27 
 
Fig. 1.4 illustrates the pathways through which above- and belowground C inputs 
contribute to soil C sequestration. Aboveground inputs include litter derived from 
leaves, flowers, branches, fruits, and woody biomass, which, upon decomposition, 
enrich soil organic matter and indirectly affect microbial activity in the rhizosphere. In 
contrast, belowground inputs originate primarily from roots in the form of exudates, 
sloughed-off cells, and other by-products, directly stimulate microbial activity in the 
rhizosphere, altering the decomposition of soil organic matter and potentially 
influencing the priming effect through enhanced mineralisation (Chen et al., 2019).  
 
Figure 1.4 How rhizospheric components link to the formation of particulate organic C 
and mineral-associated organic C. (author’s own figure developed based on concepts 
described in Dhyani et al., 2019). 
Soil microbial activity can be quantified through respiration measurements, including 
basal respiration, which reflect microbial decomposition of soil organic matter 
independent of live roots (autotrophic respiration), and rhizosphere respiration, which 
includes both root respiration and microbial respiration stimulated by root activity 
(Hogan et al., 2023). Together, these above and belowground pathways regulate the 
balance between C storage and C loss from soils and therefore play a fundamental role 
in the long-term sequestration potential of agroforestry systems. 

[PDF PAGE 48]
28 
 
1.6.4.  Root traits as drivers of carbon sequestration and nutrient dynamics 
Root acquisition of water and nutrients depends on multiple root traits. Architectural 
traits (e.g., depth and length) define root system spatial configuration; morphological 
traits (e.g., diameter, size) determine absorptive surface area; physiological traits 
include respiration, nutrient uptake kinetics, and exudation; and biological traits involve 
microbial associations (Malamy, 2005; Osmont et al., 2007; Sousa-Nunes & Somers, 
2010; Contreras-Cornejo et al., 2009; Zamioudis et al., 2013). Root exudates also 
modify root morphology specifically root length, branching density, and biomass 
distribution while mediating plant microbe interactions (Kong et al., 2018; Semchenko 
et al., 2014). In agroforestry systems, complementary root traits between trees and crops 
can reduce direct competition for resources and improve nutrient-acquisition efficiency 
compared with monoculture systems (Isaac & Borden, 2019). 
In agroforestry, root distribution strongly influences SOC dynamics. For example, 
Upson and Burgess (2013) reported deeper coarse roots in poplar intercropped systems, 
which reduced SOC stock below 60 cm depth beneath tree rows, possibly due to the 
priming effect of newly introduced C. Dead roots contribute C inputs comparable to leaf 
litter, highlighting the importance of their turnover in soil C cycling (Defrenet et al., 
2016). While studies using soil cores and allometric equations suggest minimal effects 
of root competition on fine root biomass, competitive interactions can still alter net 
primary production (NPP) in shaded plants (Charbonnier et al., 2017; Bai et al., 2023b). 
NPP, the balance of gross primary productivity and respiration, is central to quantifying 
ecosystem C cycling (Song et al., 2023). Collectively, these findings emphasise the 
central role of root traits, turnover, and species interactions in SOC sequestration and 
agroforestry productivity. Soil bulk density and pore structure largely determine root 
penetration capacity (Hughes & Wilde, 1988). Increased root branching under low 
microporosity conditions enhances root density and exploration. Root length density 
and biomass strongly influence soil volume exploration and nutrient availability (Reich 
et al., 1998; Kembel et al., 2008; Mommer et al., 2011; Ravenek et al., 2016). Within 
the root trait spectrum, low tissue density roots are associated with high N uptake, high 
turnover, and elevated respiration, contributing significantly to soil C cycling (Reich et 
al., 1998). However, elevated atmospheric CO₂ can substantially alter root morphology, 
biomass, and stoichiometry. Nie et al. (2013) observed that elevated CO₂ increased root 
[PDF PAGE 49]
29 
 
length by 26%, diameter by 8.4%, and total biomass by 28.8%. However, it 
simultaneously reduced root N concentration by 7.1% and increased the C:N ratio by 
7.8%, without affecting root C concentration. These changes may enhance soil C 
sequestration but could also alter respiration, rhizodeposition, and fungal colonisation. 
Root turnover, defined as the production and mortality of roots, is a fundamental 
component of ecosystem C and nutrient cycling (Aber et al., 1985). Turnover rates vary 
by root size: coarse roots have low turnover and high maintenance respiration, while 
fine roots exhibit rapid turnover (up to 100% annually), contributing roughly one-third 
of NPP (Jackson et al., 1997; Clark et al., 2001; Jourdan et al., 2008). The 
transformation of live and dead roots through turnover and decomposition drives soil C 
mineralisation and accumulation, while simultaneously regulating N dynamics through 
mineralisation and immobilisation. Root C: N ratios are negatively correlated with 
lifespan, linking nutrient stoichiometry to C cycling processes (De Deyn et al., 2008). 
On average, 40–50% of total soil CO2 efflux originates from root respiration, though 
contributions vary by species, nutrient availability, and mycorrhizal associations (Bond-
Lamberty et al., 2004; De Deyn et al., 2008; Martin & Stutz, 2004). These findings 
reinforce the critical role of root traits in sustaining soil fertility, microbial activity, and 
regulating C and nutrient cycle.  
Studies have found that fluctuations in SOC over time are primarily influenced by the 
balance between C inputs from plant litter (aboveground components) and root turnover 
and exudates (belowground components), which are decomposed by microbes (Ong et 
al., 1991; McCormack et al., 2015; Oliveira et al., 2018; Mayrinck et al., 2019; Panchal 
et al., 2022). Increased biomass input in agroforestry systems, from both above and 
belowground sources, can lead to SOC losses through rhizomicrobial respiration, a 
phenomenon known as the priming effect (Fontaine et al., 2007; Sayer et al., 2011). 
However, studies have shown that greater tree diversity and density generally enhance 
SOC by increasing C inputs from biomass (Lange et al., 2015; Saha et al., 2009). 
Microorganisms with a fungal network associated with roots can alter mineralisation, 
leading to a priming effect (e.g., via root cell respiration, with emissions of CO2 and 
CH4). This labile form of C, derived from root and aboveground decomposition 
products, and a recalcitrant form of C from roots and from microbial interactions with 
soil minerals, promote the formation of soil aggregates (Tisdall, 2020). In contrast, 
[PDF PAGE 50]
30 
 
some aggregates lead to anaerobic, waterlogged soil due to C allocation by roots and 
fungal networks (Reid and Goss, 1982). This effect, though, is not uniform throughout 
the soil profile; it varies with depth depending on root length density, which can 
contribute more organic matter to deeper soil layers (Cardinael et al., 2018a). 
Thevathasan & Gordon (2004) found in alley cropping that about 35% of SOC is 
present at a depth of 0-15 cm within a 2 m distance from poplar trees in Southern 
Ontario. 
Beule et al. (2021) reported that temperate poplar-based agroforestry showed an 
abundance of Basidiomycota fungi in alley cropping compared with crop monoculture. 
The ectomycorrhizal fungi, such as Cortinarius, Geopora, and Inocybe, associated with 
poplar enhance nutrient acquisition. Soil biota and soil aggregates exhibit a reciprocal 
relationship: soil organisms facilitate aggregate formation, while aggregates provide 
habitats for biota (Oades & Waters, 1991; Oades, 1984; Blanco-Canqui & Lal, 2004). 
Macroaggregates are primarily formed and stabilised by soil fauna such as earthworms 
and termites, whereas fungi and bacteria contribute to microaggregate formation 
(Tisdall, 2020). Microaggregates often contain higher microbial biomass and humic 
substances, with plant roots both living and dead supporting microbial communities. 
Beuschel et al. (2019) found that a poplar-based agroforestry system had greater 
variations in ratios of soil nutrient-fungal and soil bacterial C in tree rows compared to 
crop trees because of an increase in the input of lignin and residues of a suberin-rich 
tree. Such shifts in soil microbial and fungal communities influence the C stabilisation 
in soil aggregates. This effect may especially be significant in mature systems and 
deeper soil layers, providing insights into the long-term effectiveness of poplar in alley 
cropping systems. 
In agroforestry systems, the chemical composition of plant residues plays a major role 
in altering decomposition dynamics and long-term soil C sequestration. The C:N 
ratio of plant residues strongly influences decomposition and C sequestration. Low C: N 
residues (e.g., soybean) decompose rapidly, while high C: N residues (e.g., wheat) 
decompose slowly, promoting long term SOC storage (Blanco-Canqui & Lal, 2004). 
Lignin and other recalcitrant compounds (aromatic, phenolic, lipids) enhance aggregate 
stability and prolong SOC residence time (Melillo et al., 1982; Monreal et al., 1997). 
[PDF PAGE 51]
31 
 
Lignin rich materials, due to their slow decomposition and binding properties, play a 
critical role in stabilising macroaggregates and protecting SOC. 
Aggregate-associated SOC protection is a key mechanism for long-term C 
sequestration, particularly in systems like agroforestry that enhance labile organic 
matter. Biochemical recalcitrance and mineral interactions further stabilise SOC within 
aggregates, forming a passive C pool (Gale et al., 2001; Nair et al., 2021b). For 
example, Gunina et al. (2015) found that a no-tillage practice increased the abundance 
of macroaggregates with 70-80% C content in poplar agroforestry. Hence, they 
conclude that C content in the mineral fraction was 1.3-1.5 times greater in poplar-based 
agroforestry and short rotation coppice than in standard cropland management. 
Microclimatic conditions under trees influence the function of microfauna, mesofauna, 
and macrofauna, substantially altering the physicochemical and biological properties of 
soil and further affecting SOC storage (Chauhan et al., 2012; Jinger et al., 2023). 
In summary, root traits influence C sequestration and nutrient cycling through multiple 
interconnected pathways, including resource acquisition, root turnover, rhizodeposition, 
microbial interactions, and aggregate formation. Variations in root architecture, 
morphology, physiology, and symbiotic associations determine both the quantity and 
quality of C and nutrient inputs to soil, ultimately affecting SOC accumulation and 
stability. While increased biomass inputs can enhance long-term C sequestration, they 
may also stimulate microbial activity and accelerate SOC losses through priming 
effects. Furthermore, interactions among roots, microbial communities, and soil 
aggregates play a central role in regulating C retention within soil profiles. Despite 
significant advances in understanding these processes, substantial uncertainties remain 
regarding the relative contributions of living roots, dead roots, and rhizodeposition to C 
and nutrient fluxes across agroforestry systems. Addressing these knowledge gaps is 
essential for improving predictions of SOC dynamics, optimising nutrient-use 
efficiency, and enhancing the long-term sustainability and climate-change mitigation 
potential of agroforestry systems. 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 52]
32 
 
1.7. Summary  
Although numerous studies have demonstrated the potential of agroforestry systems to 
enhance AGB, SOC stocks, and C sequestration (Cardinael et al., 2018ab; Kuyah et al., 
2019; Cardinael et al., 2018c), important knowledge gaps remain regarding the 
mechanisms controlling long-term C storage and nutrient dynamics in temperate 
agroforestry systems. In particular, the relative contributions and interactions of 
aboveground inputs, such as leaf litter, and belowground inputs, including live and dead 
roots, to SOC sequestration and nutrient cycling remain poorly understood. While litter- 
and root-derived inputs are recognised as major drivers of soil C accumulation and 
belowground ecosystem functioning (Gill and Jackson, 2000; Jobbágy and Jackson, 
2001; Lorenz and Lal, 2014; Feng et al., 2022), limited information is available on how 
litter quality, root traits, litter decomposition, soil nutrient availability, and 
environmental conditions interact to regulate C storage and nutrient availability within 
temperate agroforestry systems.  
Furthermore, long-term studies examining root dynamics and root-soil interactions 
across spatial and temporal scales remain scarce (Udawatta and Jose, 2011; Bayala and 
Prieto, 2020). Similarly, the role of litter inputs and decomposition in supporting 
nutrient cycling and sustaining belowground C sequestration processes in temperate 
agroecosystems has received little attention. Consequently, the mechanisms linking 
aboveground productivity, belowground ecological processes, and long-term C 
sequestration remain poorly resolved. 
Addressing these knowledge gaps is essential for improving understanding of the 
biological and environmental controls on C sequestration and for optimising 
agroforestry systems as climate-resilient land management strategies. Therefore, this 
thesis investigates the relationships among tree productivity, leaf litter decomposition, 
root traits, soil nutrient dynamics, and C sequestration within a mature temperate 
agroforestry system, providing an integrated assessment of the aboveground and 
belowground processes that regulate long-term C storage. 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 53]
33 
 
1.8.  Research approach  
The University of Leeds Research Farm provides a unique opportunity to investigate the 
long-term impacts of agroforestry in a temperate agricultural landscape. The farm 
encompasses a range of land-use types, including a mature silvoarable system planted in 
the 1990s and established hedgerows adjacent to conventionally managed arable land 
(Fig. 1.5).  
The mature silvoarable system at the University of Leeds farm consists of four poplar 
hybrids (Beaupre, Robusta, Trichobel, and Gibecq) planted at 10 m intervals in 
replicated experimental blocks covering 2.3 ha (Burgess et al., 2005). These hybrids 
differ in parentage, growth performance, and disease susceptibility, characteristics that 
may influence biomass production and C sequestration. Beaupre (Populus trichocarpa × 
P. deltoides) is a fast-growing interamerican hybrid with high biomass production 
potential, but latterly it has become increasingly susceptible to leaf rust. Robusta (P. 
nigra × P. deltoides) and Gibecq (P. deltoides × P. nigra) are widely used euramerican 
hybrids known for their productivity and adaptability, while Trichobel (P. trichocarpa) 
is an intraspecific selection of black cottonwood (Burgess et al., 2005). Investigating 
these contrasting genotypes provides an opportunity to assess how genetic variation 
influences tree growth, AGB accumulation, and C sequestration within temperate 
agroforestry systems. 
 
[PDF PAGE 54]
34 
 
 
Figure 1.5 Silvoarable and hedgerow study site: (a) Alley-cropping treatments between 
tree rows during the early establishment phase of the plantation (2004) – photo by 
Chris Wright, who managed the Field Research Unit; (b) silvoarable system in 2023, 
showing the development of unmanaged vegetation between tree rows following the 
cessation of cropping treatments in 2004; (c) placement of leaf litterbags within 
hedgerows during winter (January 2024); and (d) dense summer hedgerow vegetation, 
illustrating the challenges associated with litterbag retrieval. 
Diameter at breast height has been collected for over 30 years, providing a unique long-
term dataset for examining trends in AGB C stocks and annual C sequestration among 
poplar hybrids. The presence of an on-site Met Office weather station further enables 
the assessment of genotype-specific responses to climatic variability and the influence 
of environmental conditions on the long-term growth and C sequestration. Furthermore, 
quantifying AGB C alongside BGB C (using an established root: shoot ratio) and SOC 
stocks in both tree rows and alleys, allows a whole-system assessment of C storage and 
sequestration. This information is essential for evaluating the climate change mitigation 
potential of silvoarable systems, developing future land-use scenarios and net-zero 
target strategies. 
In addition to the silvoarable system, the University of Leeds farm contains established 
hedgerows comprising native trees and shrub species, providing a complementary 

[PDF PAGE 55]
35 
 
system for investigating litter decomposition and nutrient cycling dynamics. Although 
hedgerows are well recognised for their contribution to biodiversity conservation, soil 
conservation and water quality protection (Baudry et al., 2000), their role in driving C 
and nutrient cycling within agricultural landscapes remained comparatively 
understudied. Investigating the decomposition of leaf litter from two common native 
shrub species, hawthorn (Crataegus monogyna) and hazel (Corylus avellana), using 
litterbags placed on the soil surface and at depth allows the assessment of species-
specific decomposition rates and nutrient release patterns under contrasting 
environmental conditions. 
Evaluating these processes over seasonal timescales will provide insights into the 
mechanisms that control organic matter turnover, nutrient availability, and C cycling 
within hedgerow systems. Together with the silvoarable experiment, this approach 
facilitates an integrated assessment of the aboveground and belowground processes that 
govern C sequestration and ecosystem functioning in temperate agroforestry systems. 
Such knowledge is essential for improving our understanding of how different 
agroforestry components contribute to climate change mitigation, soil health, and the 
long-term sustainability of agricultural landscapes. 
1.8.1.  Aim and objectives 
The overall aim of this thesis is to evaluate the aboveground and belowground processes 
governing C storage and accumulation in temperate agroforestry systems and to 
examine the biotic and abiotic factors influencing C and nutrient cycling. Specifically, 
the thesis evaluated the effects of agroforestry configuration, tree genotype, time, 
climate variability, soil nutrient availability, root dynamics, and litter decomposition on 
aboveground and belowground C pools and nutrient dynamics.  
This aim will be addressed through the following specific research objectives: 
1. To quantify AGB C stocks and sequestration rates in a 30-year-old poplar based 
silvoarable system and assess the influence of weather variables on annual C 
accumulation (Chapter 2). 
2. To quantify SOC stocks and sequestration rates within a poplar-based 
silvoarable system and evaluate its whole-system C sequestration potential 
[PDF PAGE 56]
36 
 
(Chapter 3). This objective integrates AGB C, BGB C, and SOC sequestration 
rates to estimate the net CO2 uptake potential of four poplar hybrids. Using three 
scenarios for expanding silvoarable agroforestry across UK cropland and arable 
land, the study evaluates the potential contribution of silvoarable agroforestry to 
achieving the UK Climate Change Committee’s targets over 25 years (to 2050). 
3. To investigate the relationships among root traits, soil nutrient availability and 
SOC storage in a 30-year-old poplar silvoarable system, thereby improving 
understanding of the role of belowground processes in C sequestration and 
nutrient cycling (Chapter 4). 
4. To evaluate the contribution of hedgerows to C and nutrient cycling by assessing 
leaf litter decomposition rates and C: N dynamics in relation to litterbag 
placement (surface versus buried) and seasonal variation (Chapter 5). 
Collectively, these objectives provide an integrated assessment of the aboveground and 
belowground mechanisms regulating C sequestration in temperate agroforestry systems. 
By linking tree productivity, SOC storage, root dynamics, nutrient availability, and litter 
decomposition processes, this thesis advances understanding of the factors controlling 
long-term C accumulation and nutrient cycling, while providing evidence to support the 
design and management of agroforestry systems for climate change mitigation, soil 
health enhancement, and sustainable land use. 
1.8.2.  Research methodology 
Following the identification of key research gaps in the literature review, Fig. 1.6 
presents a conceptual framework developed for this study that illustrates the 
relationships among the principal drivers and outcomes investigated. The framework 
incorporates the key factors discussed throughout Section 1.6, including tree species and 
genotype selection, tree age, site management, agroforestry system type 
(silvoarable/hedgerows), and weather variables. These drivers are hypothesised to 
influence three interrelated outcome categories: C and nutrient cycling, soil fertility, and 
C sequestration.  By conceptualising the interactions among these factors within poplar-
based silvoarable systems and hedgerows, the framework provides the theoretical 
foundation for the research questions, objectives, and analyses undertaken in the present 
study. It also highlights the pathways through which aboveground and belowground 
[PDF PAGE 57]
37 
 
processes interact to regulate long-term C storage, nutrient dynamics, and ecosystem 
functioning in temperate agroforestry systems. 
 
 
Figure 1.6 Conceptual framework of interactions between principal drivers and 
outcomes investigated for agroforestry systems 
Table 1.2 summarises the alignment between the thesis objectives, the key factors 
investigated, and the resulting outcomes. Objective 1 was addressed using an 
observational approach (Chapter 2). Annual DBH measurements collected over a 30-
year period were used to calculate AGB, AGB C stocks, and C sequestration rates for 
four poplar hybrids. Statistical analyses were applied to evaluate the influence of 
weather variables on the periodic annual C sequestration rates across the different 
hybrids and to assess differences in growth and C accumulation among hybrids. 
Objective 2 was achieved through a combination of field and laboratory methods 
(Chapter 3). Soil core samples were collected from tree rows (n=24), alleys between 
tree rows (n=24), and an adjacent arable field (n=48) to quantify bulk density and SOC 
content at depths of 0–15 cm and 15–30 cm. These data were used to quantify SOC 
stocks and sequestration rates within the silvoarable system. The resulting estimates 
were integrated with AGB C and BGB C stocks to quantify whole-system C 

[PDF PAGE 58]
38 
 
sequestration and upscaled to evaluate the potential net CO2 uptake of four poplar 
hybrids using three different agroforestry expansion scenarios.

Objective 3 was achieved using a field-based approach (Chapter 4). Soil samples were 
collected from four faces of three soil pits excavated within a tree row, an alley, and the 
adjacent arable field. Root samples were obtained using the soil coring method and 
processed alongside soil samples in the laboratory to determine nutrient concentrations. 
Prior to chemical analysis, root samples were scanned to quantify key morphological 
traits, enabling assessment of the relationships between root characteristics, soil nutrient 
availability, and SOC within the silvoarable system.  
Objective 4 was achieved through a field and laboratory investigation of leaf litter 
decomposition dynamics within a hedgerow adjacent to agricultural land (Chapter 5). 
Leaf litter from two native hedgerow species was collected and placed in litterbags 
either on the soil surface or buried at a depth of 5 cm. Litterbags were retrieved 
periodically over 366 days and analysed to determine mass loss, decomposition rates, 
and nutrient dynamics, including changes in C: N ratios during decomposition.  
Lastly, Chapter 6 synthesises the findings from the individual research chapters, 
evaluates their implications for C sequestration and nutrient cycling in temperate 
agroforestry systems, and identifies the study's principal limitations, along with 
priorities for future research.  
 
 
 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 60]
 
40 
 
Chapter 2. Long-term dynamics of aboveground biomass carbon 
stocks and sequestration rates in a 30-year-old temperate poplar 
silvoarable system 
Abstract  
Poplar (Populus spp.) is well known for its ecological and economic importance and its 
considerable potential for carbon (C) sequestration in agroforestry systems. However, 
the long-term dynamics of aboveground biomass (AGB) C stock and periodic annual 
carbon increment (PAIC) in mature temperate silvoarable systems remain poorly 
understood. This study quantified AGB C stocks, mean annual carbon increment 
(MAIC) and PAIC for four poplar hybrids (Beaupre, Robusta, Gibecq, and Trichobel) 
growing in a silvoarable system in northern England over a 30-year period to 2023. The 
AGB C stocks were calculated using a poplar-specific allometric equation based on 
diameter at breast height (DBH). Significant differences in AGB C stock, MAIC, and 
PAIC were observed among hybrids. By 2023, Trichobel achieved the largest mean 
AGB C stock (65.8 Mg C ha-1), MAIC (2.1 Mg C ha-1yr-1), and PAIC (2.3 Mg C ha-1yr-
1), representing almost double the values recorded for Beaupre, Gibecq, and Robusta. 
Across all hybrids, PAIC increased during the establishment phase (1994 -1998), 
decreased between 2000 and 2005, and subsequently stabilised, whereas MAIC 
increased rapidly during the early years before reaching a relatively stable trajectory. 
Trichobel consistently maintained the highest MAIC and exhibited sustained 
productivity throughout the study period, indicating a later maturity and greater long-
term growth potential than the other hybrids. Cropping management strongly influenced 
AGB C accumulation during the active silvoarable phase (1993-2004). The continuous 
fallow treatment had the highest PAIC and MAIC, exceeding continuous cropping by 
59.5% and 45.1%, respectively, indicating that reduced crop competition promoted 
greater tree growth and C uptake. In contrast, crops growing adjacent to tree rows 
constrained tree growth and C accumulation. Following the cessation of cropping, 
differences among treatments gradually diminished, highlighting the importance of 
crop-tree competition in determining C sequestration in silvoarable systems. Climate-
growth relationships differed among hybrids and were driven primarily by summer 
weather conditions. Higher summer and summer-half-year soil temperature were 
[PDF PAGE 61]
 
41 
 
associated with lower PAIC in Beaupre (-36%, p = 0.02) and Gibecq (-38%, p = 0.04), 
whereas Trichobel showed positive responses with summer rainfall (+29%, p = 0.01) 
and seasonal wind speed (+15-39%, p < 0.05). These findings demonstrate marked 
genotype-specific differences in growth responses to climatic variability and suggest 
that hybrid selection will be increasingly important under future climate change. 
Overall, the results demonstrate the value of PAIC as an indicator of long-term C 
sequestration dynamics and identify Trichobel as the hybrid with the greatest potential 
for AGB C accumulation in temperate silvoarable systems. These findings provide 
important evidence to support hybrid selection, agroforestry design, and climate change 
mitigation strategies aimed at maximising landscape-scale C sequestration.  
2.1.  Introduction  
Climate change is increasingly reshaping land-use systems worldwide, making it vital to 
adopt land-based strategies that can simultaneously support climate mitigation, 
adaptation and food security. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) 
has identified land-use change, particularly deforestation and conversion of land for 
agriculture, as a major contributor to global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, 
accounting for approximately 5.2 Gt CO2 per year (IPCC, 2019). At the same time, 
terrestrial ecosystems remove an estimated 11.2 Gt CO₂ yr-1 from the atmosphere, 
resulting in a net C sink of approximately 6.0 ± 3.7 Gt CO₂ yr-1 from the agriculture, 
forestry and land use (AFOLU) sector. Thus, AFOLU is both a driver of climate change 
and a key opportunity for mitigation through improved land management practices (Jia 
et al., 2022).  
Agroforestry, the intentional integration of trees and shrubs into farming systems, has 
emerged as a promising nature-based solution capable of sequestering C in woody 
biomass and soils while delivering co-benefits such as improved soil fertility, enhanced 
biodiversity, and flood mitigation (Cardinael et al., 2015, 2018b; Mayer et al., 2022; 
Terasaki Hart et al., 2023; Stanton et al., 2022; Judson et al., 2026). Carbon 
sequestration in agroforestry systems occurs through the capture of atmospheric carbon 
dioxide (CO2) by trees via photosynthesis and its subsequent storage in biomass and 
soils. Global assessments estimate that agroforestry practices could mitigate 10–15 Gt 
CO₂eq per year by 2050, contributing 20–30% of the mitigation required to meet the 
[PDF PAGE 62]
 
42 
 
Paris Climate Agreement targets (Griscom et al., 2017; UNEP, 2018). However, this 
potential is unevenly distributed across regions. Western Europe is estimated to 
contribute only 0.18 Gt CO2eq yr-1, substantially less than tropical regions in Africa and 
Asia, where faster tree growth and longer growing seasons prevail (Roe et al., 2021). 
However, this regional gap also reflects substantial uncertainty regarding the mitigation 
potential of temperate agroforestry systems, which remain poorly understood. Carbon 
sequestration rates are highly sensitive to variability in soil type and climate, variation 
in system design (including tree species, planting density, alley width and crop 
rotations) and interactions between trees and crops, such as competition for light, water, 
and nutrients (Quandt et al., 2023; Laub et al., 2025). Although numerous studies have 
quantified total C stocks in agroforestry systems, relatively few have examined how C 
sequestration rates vary throughout system development. In Europe, for instance, 
silvoarable systems (cropping in alleys between rows of trees) have been reported to 
sequester between 0.77 and 3 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ (Palma et al., 2007; Reisner et al., 2007). 
However, these estimates mainly come from modelled C dynamics dominated by 
biomass accumulation (trees and crops) and only indirectly reflect soil C changes. 
Differences in system ages, designs and management further limit direct comparisons 
among studies and contribute to uncertainty in C sequestration estimates.   
In the UK context, uncertainty in C sequestration presents a significant challenge, 
particularly for agroforestry systems. Approximately 70% of UK land is agricultural, 
and the UK Climate Change Committee has recommended expanding agroforestry from 
the current 3.3% of farmland to at least 10% by 2050 to support net-zero emissions 
targets (CCC, 2021; Tosh, 2021). While there is increasing momentum behind tree 
planting, including national targets of planting at least 30,000 ha of new woodland 
annually (Westaway et al., 2023), the evidence base on quantifying the contribution of 
agroforestry to climate change mitigation remains limited.  
Most agroforestry studies in temperate regions focus on relatively young systems, 
which are poorly suited to estimating long-term C sequestration due to tree growth 
patterns and the time required to reach maturity (> 20 years; Dupraz et al., 2019). 
Furthermore, while total C stocks and average sequestration rates are commonly 
reported, the temporal dynamics of C accumulation are not well characterised. Existing 
long-term studies typically express sequestration as a mean annual C increment (MAIC) 
[PDF PAGE 63]
 
43 
 
over the study period (e.g., Wotherspoon et al., 2014). Although MAIC is useful as a 
summary metric, it obscures how the annual C increment varies through time - periodic 
annual C increment (PAIC) - in response to changing tree growth patterns and 
environmental conditions. This lack of temporal resolution limits our understanding of 
when agroforestry systems deliver their greatest climate benefits and how stable those 
benefits remain over multiple decades.  
Poplar (Populus spp.) is frequently identified as a high-potential agroforestry species 
due to its fast growth, high biomass production and substantial C sequestration capacity. 
Across temperate agroforestry systems, average total C stock has been estimated at 
approximately 63 Mg C ha-1 (Montagnini and Nair, 2004), although poplar-based 
systems frequently exceed this average due to their exceptional biomass productivity. 
Wotherspoon et al. (2014) found that 25-year-old poplar intercropping systems (111 
trees ha-1) stored more C in AGB than comparable systems with other tree species.  
However, C sequestration efficiency is thought to vary substantially among poplar 
subspecies and hybrids, driven by differences in wood density, growth form and 
environmental tolerance (Jha, 2018). Despite their recognised potential, few studies 
have quantified annual changes in AGB C within mature poplar systems under 
temperate climates (Montagnini & Nair, 2004; Nair, 2012; Udawatta & Jose, 2012), and 
none have examined long-term PAIC in dynamics in detail. 
Climate variability further complicates the role of agroforestry in climate change 
mitigation. Recent studies show that poplar growth rates are highly sensitive to long-
term climate change (e.g., Kong et al., 2024), and projected warmer, drier summers in 
northwest Europe (IPCC, 2021), are likely to alter growth patterns and rates of C uptake 
(Kumar et al., 2018). Despite this, there is a lack of specific evidence on how climate 
variability affects the growth and sequestration performance of fast-growing poplar 
hybrids in silvoarable systems (Seserman et al., 2018; Trouillier et al., 2020), limiting 
confidence in future mitigation projections.  
Long-term assessments of the C sequestration potential of temperate agroforestry 
systems are thus needed to improve climate change mitigation modelling and support 
evidence-based policy development. This study addresses this knowledge gap through 
the analysis of a 30-year-old silvoarable experiment in Yorkshire, UK, comprising four 
[PDF PAGE 64]
 
44 
 
poplar hybrids and a unique dataset of annually recorded tree growth. Specifically, the 
study aims to: (i) quantify the AGB C stock of four poplar hybrids, (ii) characterise 
long-term and inter-annual variation in C accumulation through analysis of PAIC over a 
30-year period, and (iii) evaluate the impact of local climate variability on PAIC. By 
providing a rare, long-term assessment of C dynamics, hybrid performance, and climate 
sensitivity in a mature silvoarable system, this study advances the understanding of the 
potential contribution of temperate poplar-based agroforestry to climate mitigation and 
achievement of national net-zero targets.  
2.2.  Methods  
2.2.1.  Site description and experimental design  
The experimental site is located at the University of Leeds research farm, in Yorkshire 
(53°53’ N, 1°15’ W), northern England, at an altitude of 42 to 70 metres above sea-level 
(asl). The soil is a well-drained, loamy, calcareous brown earth derived from the 
Aberford series of Calcaric Endoleptic Cambisols, which are characteristic of arable 
farming in this region (Holden et al., 2019; Pun et al., 2024) and underlain by dolomitic 
limestone of the Cadeby formation (British Geological Survey, 2020).   
 
Figure 2.1 Mean annual air temperature and total annual precipitation 1993-2023 at 
the study site with data from Bramham and Church Fenton weather stations

The long-term (1993-2023) mean annual temperature was 10.9°C, and mean annual 
rainfall was 767 mm (Met Office, 2023). However, Fig. 2.1 shows that there has been 
considerable inter-annual variability over this period. Mean annual temperature was 
8.9 °C in 1993 and 10.9 °C in 2023, with a maximum of 11.0 °C in 2022 and it displays 
a general upward trend over the period. Highest rainfall occurred in 2000 (831.5 mm) 
and 2012 (827.8 mm), and lowest in 2003 (445.4 mm) and 2011 (464.3 mm). 
 
Figure 2.2 Layout of the poplar agroforestry experimental site. Each dashed vertical 
line represents the even numbered row of trees, which were used for diameter at breast 
height (DBH) measurements. Each row contains five trees of four poplar hybrids 
(Robusta, Beaupre, Trichobel and Gibecq). In the alleys between the tree rows, three 
cropping systems were used between 1992 and 2004: continuous cropping (CC), 
alternate cropping (AC), and continuous fallow (CF). These treatments are arranged 
across three experimental blocks (Block 1, Block 2, and Block 3) 
The poplar silvoarable experimental system was established in 1992 and comprises four 
fast-growing hybrids, Trichobel, Gibecq, Beaupre, and Robusta, which represented the 
major groups of poplars being grown in Britain at the time (Burgess et al., 2005). 
Between 1992 and August 2004, the alleys between the tree rows were assigned one of 
three cropping systems: continuous fallow (CF), continuous cropping (CC), or alternate 
cropping (AC). After the 2004 harvest, cropping in the alleys stopped due to reduced 

[PDF PAGE 66]
 
46 
 
yields resulting from competition for resources such as sunlight, nutrients, and water. 
Each combination of four poplar hybrids and three cropping systems was included 
within three replicated blocks in a randomised block design (n=3), to account for 
variation within the field and edging effects of the experiment (Fig. 2.2). The site 
consists of 19 tree rows-oriented north-south, with trees planted at intervals of 6.4 
metres (m) apart along each row, and tree rows spaced 10 m apart. Within each tree 
row, the hybrids were planted in groups of five consecutive trees, with a single guard 
tree at each end of the row. The planting design results in a tree density of 166 trees per 
ha. For further information on the establishment and management of trees, see Burgess 
et al. (2005).   
Each autumn, from 1992 to 2003, the 10 m-wide alleys between tree rows were 
ploughed in 8 m strips, resulting in damage to tree roots at a depth of 20 cm in the 
arable area and leaving the tree row as an uncultivated 2-m-wide strip (Burgess et al., 
2005). In the CF, two adjacent alleys never had crops planted in them, but they were 
regularly cultivated so that the measurement row of poplars between them would have 
minimal competition from crops or weeds. The CC included a rotation of cereal and 
non-cereal crops on either side of a measurement tree row, while the AC involved CC 
on one side of the measurement row and the fallow treatment on the other side of the 
tree row (Burgess et al., 2003). All crops were managed as in commercial systems, 
receiving standard fertiliser and pesticide applications as appropriate. Since 2004, the 
alleys have been unmanaged, resulting in the development of a perennial understorey of 
grasses within the alleys, which are not managed.    
2.2.2.  Estimating AGB C stock and increment rates  
Tree DBH was recorded every winter (between November-March) from 1993 until 
2023 for all 20 trees in the even numbered rows (2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 16 and 18) only, 
resulting in DBH measurement for 180 trees (45 for each hybrid) each year. However, 
DBH data were missing for the following years: 1999, 2000, 2003, and 2004. Tree 
diameters were measured using a girth tape at a height of 1.3 m above the ground. Over 
the measurement period (1993-2023), the mean DBH across all trees was 28.6 ± 12.4 
cm.  
[PDF PAGE 67]
 
47 
 
Based on this I estimated tree biomass using the allometric equation developed by 
Fortier et al. (2017). This equation was selected because it was derived using destructive 
harvest data from a poplar hybrid (Populus canadensis × Populus maximowiczii - clone 
DN × M-915508), is applicable for temperate conditions similar to those of the study 
site, relies primarily on DBH measurements, and performs well across a broad range of 
tree sizes (DBH range 7.5-30.8 cm), which corresponded with the DBH range in my 
study. These characteristics made it particularly suitable for the long-term dataset, in 
which DBH was the only consistently measured tree attribute throughout the monitoring 
period. The equation is based on allometric scaling theory, which uses a power function 
of tree growth to estimate biomass, with DBH as the predictor variable:   
ln(𝐴𝐺𝐵 𝑝𝑒𝑟 𝑦𝑒𝑎𝑟) = ln(𝑎) +  𝑏 (ln𝐷𝐵𝐻) (Equation 2.1) 
where a (-2.14) and b (2.26) are allometric coefficients that denote the slope and 
intercept of the line given in Fortier et al. (2017). Equation 2.1 was used to estimate 
AGB for all 180 trees in each year with available DBH data.  
To calculate the AGB C content (kg C) of each tree, the AGB (kg) was multiplied by 
0.5, as a default biomass-to-C standard conversion factor to assess C content of 
temperate forests (Meragiaw et al., 2021; Piper et al., 2003; Salas Macías et al., 2017; 
Fang et al., 2007; Yasin et al., 2021).  
The AGB C stock (Mg C ha-1) was calculated for each hybrid and measurement year (t) 
by scaling tree-level AGB C content (kg C) to a per unit area basis and assuming a tree 
planting density of 166 trees per hectare as:  
𝐴𝐺𝐵 𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘𝑡  = 𝐴𝐺𝐵 𝐶 𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑡 𝑥 
166 (𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟 𝑜𝑓 𝑡𝑟𝑒𝑒𝑠 𝑝𝑒𝑟 ℎ𝑎)
1000     (Equation 2.2) 
Annual AGB C sequestration was expressed in two ways:  
(1) MAIC (Mg C ha-1yr-1) represents the cumulative average annual rate of AGB C 
sequestration from planting to a given tree age. For each hybrid and measurement 
year, it was calculated as:  
𝑀𝐴𝐼𝐶𝑡  =
𝐴𝐺𝐵 𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘𝑡
𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟 𝑜𝑓 𝑦𝑒𝑎𝑟𝑠 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝑐𝑒 𝑝𝑙𝑎𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑔  (Equation 2.3) 
[PDF PAGE 68]
 
48 
 
(2) PAIC (Mg C ha-1yr-1) represents the annual increase in AGB C stock between two 
consecutive measurement years (t1 and t2). PAIC for a given interval was calculated 
as: 
𝑃𝐴𝐼𝐶𝑡1−𝑡2  =   (𝐴𝐺𝐵𝑡2− 𝐴𝐺𝐵𝑡1)/(𝑡2 − 𝑡1)            (Equation 2.4) 
2.2.3. Climate station data  
Daily meteorological data for the study period were obtained from the UK Met Office 
CEDA Archive Bramham automatic weather station (WMO station 00534) located at 
the University of Leeds farm (54 m a.s.l; 53° 52′ 1.2″,1° 19′ 58.8″ W) (Met Office, 
2023). To fill occasional gaps in the Bramham data, linear regressions were developed 
for each meteorological variable using overlapping data from nearby UK Met Office 
automatic weather stations at Cawood (WMO station 00535) (6 m a.s.l; 53° 49' 38.64" 
N, 1° 08' 48.16" W), located 19.2 km away from Bramham, and Church Fenton (WMO 
station 03355) (8 m asl; 53°49′58.8″N, 1°12′0″W), located 14.2 km away from 
Bramham. Minimum grass temperature data were gap-filled using only the Church 
Fenton station because it was the only station with corresponding data available. After 
gap-filling, soil temperature at a depth of 10 cm remained unavailable for 21 days in 
January 1996 and for 12 days in April 2000. Wind speed data were missing for the years 
1993, 2002, 2003, 2006, and 2007. The annual number of windy days was calculated as 
the number of days with wind speeds greater than 17 knots (Beaufort scale 5).  
Growing Degree Days (GDD) were calculated using a reference temperature of 5.5 °C 
consistent with temperate plant phenology (Piña‐Rey et al., 2021). For each day, GDD 
was calculated as the difference between the mean daily temperature and the base 
temperature when the mean daily temperature was above 5.5 °C; otherwise, the daily 
GDD contribution was set to zero.  For example, a mean daily temperature of 7.0 °C, 
contributed 1.5 °C GDD units (7.0-5.5 °C). Days with mean temperatures above 5.5 °C 
were classified as growing days. Air frost days and ground frost days were defined as 
days on which the minimum air temperature and minimum grass temperature, 
respectively, fell below 0 °C.  
To ensure that weather variables corresponded to the period during which growth 
occurred, climatic data were aligned with the biological growth year rather than the 
[PDF PAGE 69]
 
49 
 
calendar year. Thus, weather conditions associated with a DBH measurement in year t 
were assigned to the previous growth year (t − 1). Hence, to evaluate the influence of 
broader seasonal climatic conditions on PAIC of the four poplar hybrids, monthly 
weather data were aggregated into biologically relevant seasonal windows. The seasonal 
classifications were defined as follows: Winter: December of the year preceding the 
growth year plus January-February of the growth year, Spring: March-May of the 
growth year, Summer: June-August of the growth year, Autumn: September-November 
of the growth year, Winter half-year: October-December of the year preceding the 
growth year plus January-March of the growth year, Summer half-year: April-
September of the growth year. 
2.2.4. Statistical analysis  
Statistical analyses were conducted in R version 4.3.3 (R Core Team, 2024). The 
influence of hybrid, cropping treatment, and block on AGB C stock, MAIC, and PAIC 
was assessed across three distinct time periods: The first period (1993-2004) 
represented the active cropping phase, during which cropping treatments were 
maintained between tree rows, showing direct evaluation of their effects on tree growth 
and C sequestration. The second period (2005-2023) represented the post-cropping 
phase following the cessation of cropping in 2005 and was analysed to investigate any 
residual or carryover effects of the earlier treatments. A final analysis covering the 
entire study period (1993-2023) was conducted to assess long-term C accumulation 
patterns and the cumulative impacts of cropping treatments.  
As the AGB data were not normally distributed, according to the Shapiro-Wilk test, 
non-parametric Kruskal-Wallis tests were used to determine differences in AGB C 
stocks, MAIC, and PAIC among poplar hybrids, experimental blocks, and agricultural 
management cropping treatments. For each hybrid and measurement year, median AGB 
C stocks, MAIC and PAIC were calculated. Median values with interquartile range 
were used to reduce the effects of outliers and account for variability within the dataset. 
The interquartile range (IQR), defined as the difference between the third quartile (Q3) 
and the first quartile (Q1), was used to describe the variability of the middle 50% of the 
data. Carbon sequestration dynamics were evaluated by comparing temporal patterns in 
MAIC and PAIC among the four poplar hybrids.  
[PDF PAGE 70]
 
50 
 
The relationships between weather variables and PAIC were investigated using 
generalised linear models (GLM). As PAIC data were non-normal, continuous, positive, 
and right-skewed, gamma regression models with a log link were applied using the 
glm() function. Separate models were developed for each poplar hybrid to examine:  
1. Relationships between PAIC and annual weather variables 
2. Relationships between PAIC and seasonal weather variables (spring, summer, 
autumn, and winter), and 
3. Relationships between PAIC and weather variables aggregated over the summer 
half-year and winter half-year. 
The weather predictors considered in the analysis included mean annual air temperature, 
soil temperature at 10 cm depth, sunshine hours, growing degree days (GDD), number 
of growing days, ground frost days, air frost days, mean wind speed, number of windy 
days, and total rainfall.   
To reduce multicollinearity among predictors, absolute pairwise correlation analyses 
were conducted prior to model fitting to assess relationships among weather variables. 
Variables exhibiting strong intercorrelation (|ρ| ≥ 0.7) were considered collinear and 
were not included simultaneously in the same model. Variance inflation factors (VIF) 
were also calculated, and variables with VIF > 5 were removed to reduce redundancy 
and multicollinearity. All predictor variables were standardised prior to analysis to 
facilitate comparison of effect sizes. Statistical significance was assessed at α = 0.05 to 
identify robust and meaningful relationships.  
For each hybrid, the full model was compared with an intercept-only null model using 
likelihood ratio tests. Model fit was assessed using analysis of deviance (anova()) with a 
likelihood ratio test. Model performance was evaluated using McFadden’s pseudo-R², 
calculated as R2=1−Dmodel/Dnull, where Dmodel and Dnull represent the residual deviance of 
the fitted and null models, respectively. This metric quantifies the improvement of the 
fitted model compared to the null model. Model comparisons were performed using the 
Akaike Information Criterion (AIC), with lower AIC values indicating greater support 
for a model after accounting for both goodness of fit and model complexity 
[PDF PAGE 71]
 
51 
 
2.3. Results 
2.3.1.  Aboveground biomass carbon stock of the poplar hybrids  
There were no significant differences in AGB C stock between replicate treatment 
blocks. The AGB C stocks increased steadily throughout the 30-year monitoring period 
in all poplar hybrids (Fig. 2.3). However, the magnitude and rate of C accumulation 
varied significantly among hybrids. The Kruskal-Wallis test showed significant 
differences in C stock among hybrids during all three periods: 1993-2004 (χ² = 53.873, 
df = 3, p <0.001), 2005-2023 (χ² = 878.65, df = 3, p < 0.001), and 1993-2023 (χ² = 
332.84, df = 3, p < 0.001). Hence, hybrid identity was a major determinant of C stock 
across both the active cropping and post-cropping phases, as well as over the entire 30-
year study period. At the beginning of the study, in 1993, Beaupre had the highest 
median C stock (0.16 Mg Cha-1, IQR = 0.06 Mg C ha-1), followed by Trichobel (0.08 
Mg C ha-1, IQR = 0.04 Mg C ha-1), Robusta (0.02 Mg C ha-1, IQR = 0.03 Mg C ha-1), 
and Gibecq (0.01 Mg C ha-1, IQR = 0.03 Mg C ha-1). By 2002, all hybrids showed 
substantial increases in AGB C stock, with Beaupre still having the highest stock (12.73 
Mg C ha-1, IQR = 3.39 Mg C ha-1), followed by Trichobel (11.79 Mg C ha-1, IQR = 2.98 
Mg C ha-1), Robusta (9.30 Mg C ha-1, IQR = 2.71 Mg C ha-1), and Gibecq (8.80 Mg C 
ha-1, IQR = 3.03 Mg C ha-1) (Fig. 2.3).  
Agricultural cropping treatments (CF, CC, and AC) showed a significant effect on AGB 
C stocks during both the early assessment period (1993–2004), as indicated by the 
Kruskal–Wallis test (χ² = 26.29, df = 2, p < 0.001), and the later period after cropping 
activity (2005-2023) (χ² = 6.479, df = 2, p = 0.039). During the active (1993-2004) and 
post-cropping (2005-2023) periods, AGB C stocks were greatest under the CF, 
exceeding CC by 53.7% and AC by 2.8%, respectively.  However, when the analysis 
was extended to encompass the entire study period (1993–2023), the effect of 
management treatments was substantially reduced and became marginally non-
significant (χ² = 5.91, df = 2, p = 0.052).  
[PDF PAGE 72]
 
52 
 
 
Figure 2.3 Box and whisker plots showing the median and the 25th and 75th percentiles 
of the AGB C stock (Mg C ha-1) over the period 1993 to 2023 for each poplar hybrid 
(n=45 for each hybrid in each year) 
From 2005 onward, Trichobel surpassed Beaupre in AGB C stock and maintained its 
lead for the remainder of the study period. By 2023, more than 30 years after 
establishment, Trichobel had accumulated the largest median C stock (65.99 Mg C ha-1, 
IQR = 11.02 Mg C ha-1), representing the highest long-term C storage among the 
hybrids. In contrast, Gibecq had the lowest AGB C stock (33.77 Mg C ha-1, IQR = 6.66 
Mg C ha-1), approximately half that of Trichobel. Beaupre and Robusta exhibited 
intermediate AGB C stocks of 40.08 Mg C ha-1 and 38.28 Mg C ha-1, respectively (Fig. 
2.3).  
Overall, while Beaupre accumulated C most rapidly during the early stages of stand 
development, Trichobel showed superior long-term performance and ultimately 
achieved the greatest AGB C storage. Gibecq consistently exhibited the lowest C 
accumulation throughout the study period, while Robusta showed moderate growth and 
C sequestration compared with the other hybrids. 

[PDF PAGE 73]
 
53 
 
 
2.3.2.  Mean annual carbon increment and periodic annual carbon 
increment over 30 years   
The Kruskal-Wallis test showed significant differences in MAIC among hybrids during 
1993-2004 (χ² = 146.03, df = 3, p < 0.001), 2005-2023 (χ² = 1839.2, df = 3, p < 0.001), 
and across the entire study period, 1993-2023 (χ² = 948.96, df = 3, p < 0.001). The 
substantially higher χ² value across whole study period indicates greater divergence in 
MAIC among hybrids over time. Between 1993 and 1998, the median MAIC increased 
rapidly across all hybrids as stands developed (Fig. 2.4). For example, Beaupre’s 
median MAIC increased from 0.16 Mg C ha-1yr-1 (IQR = 0.10) in 1993 to 1.43 Mg C ha-
1yr-1 (IQR = 0.47) in 1998 (Fig. 2.4). Over this early growth phase, Beaupre consistently 
exhibited the highest MAIC, followed by Trichobel, Robusta, and Gibecq. 
 
Figure 2.4 Box and whisker plots showing the median and the 25th and 75th percentiles 
of the mean annual C increment (MAIC Mg C ha-1yr-1) over the period 1993 to 2023 for 
each poplar hybrid (n=45 for each hybrid in each year) 

[PDF PAGE 74]
 
54 
 
From 2001 to 2014, MAIC continued to increase gradually across all hybrids (Fig. 2.4). 
MAIC for Beaupre increased from 1.23 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.37) to 1.42 Mg C ha-1yr-
1 (IQR = 0.27), Gibecq from 0.79 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.29) to 1.20 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR 
= 0.25), Robusta from 0.86 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.35) to 1.32 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 
0.37), and Trichobel from 1.09 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.30) to 2.00 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 
0.32). Block effects were not significant during the establishment period (1993-2004; χ² 
= 1.45, df = 2, p = 0.485) or across the entire study period (χ² = 1.45, df = 2, p = 0.48). 
However, significant differences among blocks were detected during the later stage of 
stand development (2005-2023; χ² = 6.43, df = 2, p = 0.040). 
From 2005 onward, Trichobel exhibited the greatest increase in median value of the 
MAIC, rising from 1.58 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.34) in 2005 to 2.13 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 
0.36) by 2023, outperforming all other hybrids (Fig. 2.4). MAIC generally increased 
until approximately 2011, after which values stabilised or declined for Beaupre, 
Robusta, and Gibecq. In contrast, Trichobel maintained elevated MAIC throughout the 
remainder of the monitoring period. 
The Kruskal-Wallis test showed significant differences in PAIC among hybrids during 
1993–2004 (χ² = 63.25, df = 3, p < 0.001), 2005–2023 (χ² = 1036.9, df = 3, p < 2.2 × 
10⁻¹⁶), and across the entire study period (1993–2023) (χ² = 803.78, df = 3, p < 0.001) 
As shown in Fig. 2.5, all hybrids exhibited a rapid increase in PAIC during the early 
establishment phase (1994-1998), reflecting vigorous juvenile growth. During this 
period, Beaupre recorded the highest PAIC values, with the median increasing 
from 0.51 Mg C ha-1yr-1 (IQR = 0.19) in 1994 to 3.02 Mg C ha-1yr-1 (IQR = 0.69) in 
1998.  
Following this initial phase, PAIC trajectories diverged among hybrids. By 2002, 
Trichobel had achieved the highest median PAIC (1.93 Mg C ha-1yr-1; IQR = 0.60), 
exceeding Beaupre (1.84 Mg C ha-1yr-1), Gibecq (1.67 Mg C ha-1yr-1), and Robusta 
(1.39 Mg C ha-1yr-1) (Fig. 2.5). This trend continued, and by 2005, 12 years post-
establishment, Trichobel exhibited the highest PAIC at 2.90 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.59), 
compared with Beaupre (2.12 Mg C ha-1yr-1), Gibecq (2.03 Mg C ha-1yr-1), and Robusta 
(2.01 Mg C ha-1yr-1).  From 2001 onwards, PAIC generally ranged from a maximum of 
1.14 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 0.37) for Trichobel to a minimum of 0.73 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR 
[PDF PAGE 75]
 
55 
 
= 0.24) for Gibecq, illustrating the increasing divergence in growth performance among 
hybrids. 
 
Figure 2.5 Box and whisker plots showing the median and the 25th and 75th percentiles 
of periodic annual carbon increment (PAIC Mg C ha-1yr-1) over the period 1993 to 
2023 for each poplar hybrid (n=45 for each hybrid in each year) 
Trichobel displayed a distinct growth trajectory characterised by sustained productivity 
during stand maturation. In contrast to the other hybrids, which showed progressive 
declines in PAIC with increasing age, Trichobel maintained relatively high PAIC values 
throughout the study period and particularly after 2009. Although a general decline in 
PAIC was observed for all hybrids after 2015, Trichobel consistently retained the 
highest PAIC (Fig. 2.5). Peak PAIC for Trichobel occurred in 2021, reaching 3.72 Mg 
C ha-1yr-1 (IQR = 1.80), representing the highest PAIC across the 30-year study period. 
By 2023, PAIC had declined across all hybrids, yet remained highest for Trichobel 
at 2.33 Mg C ha-1yr-1(IQR = 1.17), significantly exceeding the values of Beaupre (0.7 
Mg C ha-1yr-1), Gibecq (0.42 Mg C ha-1yr-1), and Robusta (0.86 Mg C ha-1yr-1). In 
contrast, no significant effect of block found on PAIC during 1993–2004 (χ² = 0.909, df 
= 2, p = 0.635), 2005–2023 (χ² = 2.451, df = 2, p = 0.294), or across the entire study 

[PDF PAGE 76]
 
56 
 
period (1993–2023) (χ² = 0.901, df = 2, p = 0.637). This analysis indicates greater 
differentiation in PAIC among hybrids over the longer study period, while the effects of 
cropping treatments and block remained non-significant. 
Cropping management significantly affected both MAIC and PAIC during the active 
cropping phase (1993-2004; MAIC: χ² = 47.4, df =2, p < 0.001; PAIC: χ² = 52.6, df=2, 
p < 0.001). Following the cessation of cropping, no significant treatment effect was 
detected for MAIC during the post-cropping phase (χ² = 1.15, df = 2, p = 0.561), 
whereas PAIC remained significantly affected (χ² = 9.12, df = 2, p = 0.010). Across the 
entire study period (1993-2023), treatment effects were significant for MAIC (χ² = 6.38, 
df = 2, p = 0.041) but not for PAIC (χ² = 1.13, df = 2, p= 0.568). 
Continuous fallow treatment exhibited the highest MAIC and PAIC during the active 
cropping period (1993-2004), exceeding CC by 45.1% and 59.5%, respectively. During 
the post-cropping period (2005-2023), CC showed the highest MAIC (10.3-17.6% 
greater than AC and CF) but no significant effect, whereas CF had the highest PAIC 
(3.7-4.4% greater). Across the entire study period (1993-2023), CC maintained the 
highest MAIC and PAIC, surpassing AC by 5.9% and 2.9%, respectively.  
 
Figure 2.6 Comparison of PAIC and MAIC over the experimental period (1994-2023). 
Panels show: (a) Beaupre, (b) Gibecq, (c) Robusta, and (d) Trichobel. The biological 
rotation age (BRA) is defined as the age at which PAIC and MAIC are equal, indicating 
the rotation age that maximises mean productivity. 

[PDF PAGE 77]
 
57 
 
Comparison of MAIC and PAIC trajectories revealed contrasting patterns of stand 
development among hybrids (Fig. 2.6). For Beaupre, Robusta, and Gibecq, MAIC was 
higher than PAIC during the early establishment, with the two measures converging 
during the late 1990s. Thereafter, PAIC declined progressively, particularly after 2011, 
indicating age-related reduction in growth rates. The hybrids shared a similar 
developmental pattern, characterised by high levels of PAIC during the early years 
(1994–2000), followed by declining productivity as the age of the stand increased. 
Trichobel, however, showed a markedly different growth pattern. Periodic annual 
carbon increment remained comparatively high throughout the study period and closely 
tracked MAIC even in later years, suggesting sustained productivity and a weaker age-
related decline in growth. For example, in 2022, both PAIC and MAIC were 
approximately 2.10 Mg C ha-1yr-1. By contrast, other hybrids exhibited larger 
differences between the two measures. Beaupre recorded PAIC and MAIC values of 
1.27 and 1.43 Mg C ha-1yr-1, respectively, in 2006, while Gibecq recorded values of 
1.11 and 1.13 Mg C ha-1yr-1during the same year. Robusta saw PAIC and MAIC 
converge in 2011, with values of 1.38 and 1.37 Mg C ha-1yr-1, respectively. Overall, the 
close correspondence between PAIC and MAIC in Trichobel during stand maturity 
indicates sustained C sequestration capacity and continued productivity well beyond the 
period when growth rates began to decline in the other hybrids. 
2.3.3.  Influence of weather variables on periodic annual carbon increment  
Weather variables were not significant predictors of PAIC in the annual, winter half-
year, or most seasonal models. The only exception was for the summer model, in which 
significant relationships were observed (Suppl. Table A.1).  In particular, the summer 
model explained a substantial proportion of PAIC variation in Beaupre (R² = 49%, p = 
0.01) and Trichobel (R² = 47%, p < 0.01), while the relationships for Gibecq (R² = 43%, 
p = 0.04) and Robusta (R² = 59%, p = 0.02) were significant. As shown in Table 2.1, 
significant individual predictor effects in the summer model were observed only for 
Beaupre and Trichobel. In contrast, none of the individual summer weather variables 
were significant predictors for Gibecq and Robusta. However, summer-half soil 
temperature was significantly associated with Gibecq growth (Table 2.1). This suggests 
[PDF PAGE 78]
 
58 
 
that, for Gibecq, the contribution of summer climate variables to model performance 
arose from their combined effect rather than from any single weather predictor.
