a negative influence. Percentage changes were calculated as (Exp(𝛽) − 1) × 100. 
Because predictors were standardised, all effects correspond to a one-standard-deviation 
increase in the weather variable. 
Several significant associations between seasonal weather variables and PAIC were 
identified (Table 2.1). Negative influences were observed for Beaupre and Gibecq, 
where higher soil temperatures, particularly during the summer half-year, were 
associated with lower PAIC. In Beaupre, increased spring wind speed was also 
associated with reduced PAIC. In contrast, Trichobel exhibited predominantly positive 
associations with climatic variables: higher wind speeds and summer rainfall were 
associated with increased PAIC, whereas autumn rainfall was negatively associated 
with PAIC. Overall, soil temperature emerged as the primary climatic factor associated 
with reduced PAIC in Beaupre and Gibecq, while rainfall and wind speed were the main 
variables associated with PAIC in Trichobel. No individual weather variables were 
significantly associated with PAIC in Robusta.

2.4.  Discussion   
2.4.1.  Aboveground biomass carbon stock and carbon sequestration rate   
Few studies have quantified AGB C stocks in mature (~30-year-old) temperate 
silvoarable systems, and meaningful comparisons between studies are challenging due 
to a wide variation in tree species, management practices, climatic conditions, soil 
types, and planting densities (Abedi et al., 2024; Oliveira et al., 2018; Ramos et al., 
2018). To date, no study has reported PAIC for poplar hybrids in mature silvoarable 
systems, as MAIC is usually presented. Comparisons are further constrained because 
many published studies do not report both system age or tree density alongside AGB C 
stocks and MAIC, limiting the opportunities to benchmark long-term C sequestration 
potential across systems. 
The present study shows that both hybrid type and cropping treatments influenced AGB 
C accumulation within the poplar silvoarable agroforestry system although their 
importance varied throughout stand development (Fig. 2.3). Hybrid effects became 
increasing evident over time, suggesting that genetic characteristics were a major 
determinant of long-term C sequestration potential. In contrast, the lack of significant 
block effect for both AGB C stock and MAIC indicates limited spatial heterogeneity 
across the experimental area. This suggests that differences in productivity were 
primarily associated with hybrid performance and cropping management rather than 
underlying site variation. 
Differences among hybrids are likely associated with variation in growth rate, biomass 
allocation patterns, resource-use efficiency and physiological performance, resulting in 
distinct C accumulation trajectories through time (Truax et al., 2014). However, the 
temporal growth patterns observed among hybrids demonstrate that early growth 
performance is not necessarily a reliable indicator of long-term C sequestration potential 
(Fig. 2.3). While MAIC provides an integrated measure of average productivity, PAIC 
revealed substantial temporal variation in growth rates and showed that early 
productivity rankings did not persist throughout stand development. Although Beaupre 
exhibited the highest AGB C stock and MAIC during the first six years after 
establishment, this advantage was not maintained throughout the study period (Fig. 2.3 
[PDF PAGE 80]
 
60 
 
and 2.4). From 1998 onwards, Trichobel showed consistently greater increases in PAIC 
and ultimately accumulated the highest AGB C stock by 2023 (Fig. 2.3). These 
contrasting growth trajectories illustrate how hybrid rankings can change substantially 
through time as trees mature. Consequently, estimates of C sequestration potential 
derived from measurements made during the early years after establishment may not 
accurately represent long-term productivity. Such temporal variability may reflect 
differences in photosynthetic assimilation, biomass allocation strategies, resource-use 
efficiency, and responses to changing competitive conditions among hybrids (Wang et 
al., 2023b; Hilmers et al., 2025). Importantly, these findings suggest that short-term 
monitoring alone may provide an unreliable basis for predicting the long-term climate-
change mitigation potential of silvoarable systems. 
Cropping management strongly influenced C uptake into AGB during the active 
cropping period (1993-2004), likely through effects on tree growth and increased 
competition for light, water, and nutrients where crops were grown on either side of tree 
rows (Burgess et al., 2003; Jose et al., 2004. The lower rates of C accumulation 
observed under more intensive cropping are consistent with evidence that competition 
between crops and trees can constrain tree growth and C uptake in agroforestry systems 
(Jose, 2009; Udawatta & Jose, 2012; Nair, 2012; Cardinael et al., 2018c; Forrester et al., 
2021). During the active cropping period, the CC treatment exhibited the lowest C 
accumulation, with MAIC 45.1% lower and AGB C stocks 53.7% lower than CF, while 
AC showed intermediate values between CC and CF. These results indicate that higher 
cropping intensity constrained tree growth and AGB C sequestration. Although 
cropping treatment differences in total AGB C stocks were not significant over the full 
study period, the early growth suppression observed in CC demonstrates the influence 
that crop-tree competition can exert on biomass accumulation. Had these competitive 
effects persisted for longer, greater divergence in long-term C sequestration may have 
emerged. Similar observations have been reported in other silvoarable agroforestry 
systems (Vaccaro et al., 2023).  
Following the cessation of cropping, differences among treatments gradually 
diminished over time. Although trees in the previously cropped treatments continued to 
exhibit lower MAIC and AGB C stocks than those in the CF treatment, these 
differences were no longer statistically significant during the post-cropping period. In 
[PDF PAGE 81]
 
61 
 
contrast, PAIC remained significantly affected by treatment, indicating that differences 
in periodic C accumulation persisted after cropping had ceased. While the convergence 
of MAIC and AGB C stocks suggests a degree of recovery in the previously cropped 
treatments, the continued treatment effect on PAIC indicates that growth responses 
remained distinct among treatments during the post-cropping phase. 
 Reduced competition for water and nutrients may have enabled trees to increase growth 
rates and partially recover biomass accumulation lost during the cropping phase. 
Although growth was initially supressed under the CC treatment, subsequent increases 
in productivity enabled trees to compensate for these early limitations, resulting in 
comparable and, in some cases slightly greater long-term AGB C stocks and MAIC.  
Therefore, where aboveground C sequestration is the primary management objective, 
establishing trees in pasture systems or minimising competition from adjacent crops 
may be more effective than maintaining intensive alley-cropping arrangements close to 
tree rows.  
Comparisons with previous studies (Table 2.2) further emphasise the importance of tree 
density and system design in determining AGB C stock and MAIC. Jha et al. (2018) 
reported lower AGB C stocks in a poplar agroforestry system in southern France than 
those observed for Trichobel in the present study. Although direct comparison is limited 
because stand age was not reported, the lower planting density of 139 trees ha⁻¹ 
compared with 166 trees ha⁻¹ in the present study may have contributed to reduced 
biomass accumulation. Similarly, Peichl et al. (2006) reported lower AGB C stocks and 
MAIC values in a 13-year-old Canadian silvoarable system established at a density of 
111 trees ha⁻¹, while Wotherspoon et al. (2014) subsequently reported similarly lower 
values from the same system after 25 years of growth. Although differences in climate, 
genotype, management history, and methodological approaches complicate direct 
comparisons, stand age, tree density, and site management practices are also likely to 
contribute to differences in stand-level C accumulation across these agroforestry 
systems (Table 2.2).   
Further evidence for the importance of stand structure comes from comparisons with 
short-rotation forestry systems. Oliveira et al. (2018) reported substantially higher 
MAIC values in short-rotation poplar plantations across several Spanish climatic 
[PDF PAGE 82]
 
62 
 
regions. However, these systems were established at extremely high densities ranging 
from 5,555 to 13,333 cuttings ha⁻¹ and therefore differed fundamentally from the low-
density silvoarable design employed in the present study. The higher productivity 
observed by Oliveira et al. (2018) likely reflects the intensive management and high 
stocking densities characteristic of short-rotation biomass production systems. Direct 
comparisons should therefore be interpreted cautiously because propagule density at 
establishment does not necessarily reflect surviving tree density during stand 
development, and silvoarable agroforestry systems are designed to balance tree 
production and agricultural outputs rather than maximise biomass production alone. 
The influence of spatial configuration has also been highlighted in recent agroforestry 
research.  Judson et al. (2025) found that combined soil and tree-biomass C stocks 
increased by approximately 0.7 Mg C ha-1 yr-1 relative to an adjacent arable control in a 
mature temperate silvoarable system with 12 m alley spacing. In contrast, no significant 
C benefit was observed where alley widths were 24 m. These findings suggest that the 
influence of tree rows declines with increasing distance into the alley and that wider 
alley configurations may reduce stand-level biomass accumulation. Together with the 
density-related comparisons discussed above, this evidence indicates that both tree 
stocking density and spatial arrangement are key determinants of C sequestration in 
agroforestry systems. 
Overall, the findings of the present study demonstrate that long-term C sequestration in 
poplar agroforestry systems is governed by interactions among hybrid performance, 
stand structure, and management practices. Hybrid selection influenced AGB C stock 
and MAIC throughout stand development, although the magnitude and direction of this 
effect changed through time. For example, Beaupre showed higher C accumulation 
during the early years, whereas Trichobel ultimately achieved the greater long-term 
AGB C stock, demonstrating that early productivity does not necessarily translate into 
superior long-term sequestration. The long-term dataset highlights the importance of 
extended monitoring for accurately characterising productivity trajectories and C 
sequestration rates.

Despite increasing policy interest in agroforestry as a climate-change mitigation 
strategy, empirical evidence quantifying AGB C stock in mature low-density silvoarable 
systems remains scarce, creating uncertainty in estimates of their climate-change 
mitigation potential. These findings have important implications for C accounting 
frameworks and agroforestry incentive schemes, which frequently rely on sequestration 
rates derived from relatively young systems. The observed shifts in hybrid performance 
through time indicate that sequestration estimates based solely on short-term 
measurements may overestimate or underestimate long-term C storage potential. 
Continued long-term monitoring across a wider range of species, genotypes, planting 
densities and management regimes will therefore be essential for improving future C 
accounting, refining climate change mitigation models and informing evidence-based 
agroforestry policy and practice.  
2.4.2.  Variability in AGB C accumulation over 30 years 
Among the four hybrids studied, Trichobel demonstrated the greatest long-term AGB C 
sequestration potential, achieving the highest AGB C stock and maintaining 
productivity throughout the 30-year period. To our knowledge, no previous study has 
examined the relationship between MAIC and PAIC in mature poplar agroforestry 
systems. The inclusion of PAIC provided additional insight into temporal C dynamics 
that would not have been apparent from MAIC alone, enabling changes in C uptake 
through time to be evaluated. These findings highlight the importance of long-term 
monitoring when assessing the C sequestration potential of agroforestry systems.   
The findings are consistent with previous research demonstrating substantial 
interspecific variation in C accumulation within temperate agroforestry systems. For 
example, Wotherspoon et al. (2014) reported considerable differences in total C stocks 
(AGB + BGB + SOC; 91-113 Mg C ha-1) and MAIC (0.8-2.1 Mg C ha-1 yr-1) among 
tree species in a 25-year-old tree-based intercropping system in Canada, illustrating the 
importance of species-specific growth characteristics for long-term C storage. Similarly, 
Cardinael et al. (2018ab) observed that C accumulation rates in agroforestry systems 
tend to decline with stand age, reflecting the transition from rapid biomass production 
during establishment to slower growth as trees mature. This pattern is widely observed 
in forest ecosystems, where C uptake is generally greatest during periods of rapid 
[PDF PAGE 85]
 
65 
 
growth and declines as physiological and structural constraints increase with age (Ryan 
et al., 1997). 
The temporal patterns observed among the poplar hybrids reflected these broader stand 
development processes. By year 15, Beaupre (67%) and Gibecq (64%) had already 
accumulated 67% and 64% of their final 2023 AGB C stock, respectively (Fig. 2.7), 
indicating relatively rapid early growth and C accumulation. Their growth trajectories 
suggest an earlier transition towards maturity and a greater reduction in biomass 
accumulation rates in later years. In contrast, Robusta and particularly Trichobel 
exhibited more sustained C accumulation throughout the study period. Although age-
related declines in productivity were evident across all hybrids, the magnitude of this 
decline differed substantially among genotypes.  
Importantly, whereas Beaupre and Gibecq accumulated a large proportion of their C 
during the establishment phase, Trichobel continued to accumulate C at relatively high 
rates during the middle and later stages of stand development. Consequently, its 
competitive advantage emerged gradually and was only fully apparent after several 
decades of growth. This finding demonstrates that early growth performance is not 
necessarily a reliable predictor of long-term AGB C sequestration potential and suggests 
that assessments based solely on short monitoring periods may misrepresent the long-
term C sequestration potential of individual hybrids. 
Trichobel consistently exhibited higher PAIC values than the other hybrids throughout 
the study period and experienced only small decreases as the stand matured (Fig. 2.6 & 
2.7), indicating a prolonged period of productivity. This pattern was especially evident 
in 2022, when Trichobel recorded a PAIC value that closely matched its MAIC (Fig. 
2.6). Because MAIC typically declines with increasing stand age, the convergence of 
PAIC and MAIC at a relatively high level suggests that annual C uptake remained close 
to the long-term average rate despite the stand approaching 30 years of age. In contrast, 
when PAIC falls substantially below MAIC, current C accumulation is lower than the 
historical average and cumulative AGB C stock reflects C accumulated during earlier 
developmental stages. This sustained productivity exhibited by Trichobel therefore 
contributed to its superior long-term C sequestration performance and ultimately 
resulted in the highest AGB C stock among the hybrids studied. 
[PDF PAGE 86]
 
66 
 
 
Figure 2.7 Mean aboveground biomass (AGB) carbon stock of four poplar hybrids: (A) 
AGB C stock (Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹) at 5,15, 20, and 25 years; (B) Percentage of AGB C stock 
at 5,10,15 and 20 years relative to 25 years 
Over the study period, the progressive reduction in cropping-treatment differences after 
cropping ceased suggests that alley-cropping management largely influenced C 
accumulation in AGB during periods of active crop-tree interaction. Once crops were no 
longer present, trees across treatments were exposed to more similar growing 
conditions, which may have facilitated compensatory growth in previously suppressed 
trees and reduced differences in C accumulation over time. The convergence of AGB C 
stocks and sequestration rates among treatments indicates that early management did 
not impose a permanent limitation on long-term stand-level C sequestration. This 
finding suggests that growth reductions associated with crop-tree competition during 
establishment can be at least partially offset when competition is alleviated. From a 
management perspective, however, the lower rates of C accumulation observed during 
the cropping phase indicate that reducing crop-tree competition may be beneficial when 
maximising long-term tree growth and AGB C sequestration is the primary objective. 
Consequently, less intensive silvoarable designs, wider uncropped buffer zones adjacent 
to tree rows, or silvopastoral systems may provide greater opportunities for long-term 
biomass accumulation and C storage. 
 
 

[PDF PAGE 87]
 
67 
 
2.4.3.  Effect of weather variables on PAIC  
The results indicate that weather variables influenced PAIC primarily through summer 
conditions highlighting the importance of the peak growing season in determining 
annual AGB C accumulation. Summer weather variables explained a substantial 
proportion of PAIC variation, particularly for Beaupre and Trichobel, suggesting that 
growth during peak physiological activity is highly responsive to environmental 
conditions (Table 2.1). This seasonal sensitivity is likely linked to physiological and 
biochemical processes that regulate C uptake and biomass production (Muhammad et 
al., 2021). However, the effects of seasonal weather variables on PAIC differed 
markedly among the poplar hybrids, indicating substantial variation in climatic 
sensitivity (Table 2.1). 
For Beaupre, PAIC was negatively associated with both summer soil temperature and 
summer-half-year soil temperature, while Gibecq showed a similar negative association 
with summer-half-year soil temperature (Table 2.1). These findings suggest that 
elevated soil temperatures were associated with conditions likely to increase evaporative 
demand and moisture stress, thereby limiting growth. Drought and heat stress are 
widely recognised as major constraints on poplar productivity because they reduce 
stomatal conductance, photosynthetic activity, biomass accumulation, and hydraulic 
functioning (Rosso et al., 2023; Wang et al., 2025). The stronger response to summer-
half-year period compared with summer alone suggests that cumulative thermal 
conditions may exert a greater influence on growth than short-term seasonal 
fluctuations. This interpretation is supported by findings from temperate European 
poplar plantations, where prolonged climatic water deficit and heat stress have been 
associated with reduced biomass productivity (Bianchi et al., 2023). In addition to the 
effects of soil temperature, Beaupre exhibited a significant negative association between 
PAIC and spring wind speed (Table 2.1). Because spring corresponds to the onset of 
cambial reactivation and canopy development, increased wind speed during this period 
may enhance evapotranspiration and atmospheric water demand, thereby reducing water 
availability for growth. Wind has been shown to influence plant hydraulic functioning 
and transpiration rates, particularly during periods of active growth (Schymanski & Or, 
2015; Schneider et al., 2025). Together with the negative effects of summer and 
summer-half-year soil temperatures, this pattern suggests that Beaupre and Gibecq may 
[PDF PAGE 88]
 
68 
 
be particularly sensitive to weather conditions that increase water demand and 
physiological stress. 
The susceptibility of Beaupre to climatic stress may also be linked to disease dynamics, 
as Beaupre is known to be particularly susceptible to leaf rust (Melampsora larici-
populina) (Lonsdale & Tabbush, 2002). Severe rust infections can lead to premature 
defoliation, reduced photosynthetic activity, and lower biomass production. Previous 
studies have shown that early-season rust infections result in greater reductions in 
growth because photosynthetically active leaf area is lost during the period of maximum 
C assimilation (Pinon & Frey, 2005). Consequently, the lower PAIC during warmer 
years may reflect the combined effects of thermal stress and increased disease 
susceptibility. Although increased summer rainfall may favour fungal development 
(Elad et al., 2016), no significant direct relationship between rainfall and PAIC of 
Beaupre was detected in the present study. 
The observed negative effect of soil temperature may also reflect local microclimatic 
conditions. Kovács and Vityi (2022) reported significantly lower soil temperatures in 
poplar agroforestry systems compared with conventional plantations and demonstrated a 
strong relationship between soil and air temperature. Their findings suggest that 
moderated soil thermal conditions may help maintain favourable growing environments 
and reduce temperature-related stress, supporting the interpretation that elevated soil 
temperatures can constrain poplar growth, and thus C uptake. 
In contrast to Beaupre and Gibecq, Trichobel exhibited several positive climatic 
responses. PAIC increased with summer rainfall, indicating that water availability 
during the main growing season was an important driver of growth (Table 2.1). This 
finding is consistent with studies across temperate Europe showing that precipitation 
and water availability are among the strongest determinants of hybrid poplar 
productivity, while moisture deficits generally reduce biomass accumulation (Bianchi et 
al., 2023; Rosso et al., 2023). Adequate moisture availability during the active growing 
season supports photosynthesis, nutrient uptake, canopy expansion, and cambial 
activity, thereby promoting growth and C uptake (Wang et al., 2025). 
[PDF PAGE 89]
 
69 
 
Wind speed showed contrasting effects among hybrids. While spring wind speed 
negatively affected Beaupre, winter, summer, and autumn wind speeds were positively 
associated with PAIC in Trichobel (Table 2.1). Moderate wind can enhance turbulent air 
exchange around leaves, reducing boundary-layer resistance and facilitating CO2 
diffusion and water vapour exchange (Charrier et al., 2015). Increased airflow may also 
improve transpiration-driven nutrient transport and promote evaporative cooling during 
warm periods (Schymanski & Or, 2015). Similar relationships between wind conditions 
and transpiration have been reported for temperate forest trees (Jackson et al., 2021). 
However, wind can also negatively affect tree performance through mechanical stress, 
stem deformation, canopy damage, and disturbances to hydraulic functioning (Burgess 
et al., 2005; Gardiner et al., 2019). Therefore, the positive effects observed in Trichobel 
most likely reflect moderate wind conditions that enhanced physiological functioning 
rather than damaging wind events. The contrasting responses of Beaupre and Trichobel 
further emphasise genotype-specific climatic adaptation among poplar hybrids, and 
important consideration depending on the landscape and wind and moisture conditions 
likely to be experienced. 
An important finding was that weather variables from multiple biological seasons 
influenced PAIC. In particular, the influence of winter and autumn weather variables on 
Trichobel suggests that growth responses are not determined solely by climatic 
conditions during the active growing season. Instead, climatic conditions preceding 
growth may affect dormancy release, carbohydrate storage, bud development, and 
subsequent cambial activity, resulting in carry-over effects on annual growth. Similar 
lagged climatic responses have been reported in temperate forest species, where growth 
is influenced by both current and preceding seasonal conditions (Gudynaitė-
Franckevičienė et al., 2022). 
Overall, the predominance of negative temperature-growth relationships in Beaupre and 
Gibecq, coupled with the positive associations between rainfall, wind speed, and PAIC 
observed in Trichobel, indicates clear differences in climatic sensitivity among hybrids. 
The positive responses of Trichobel to several climatic variables suggest greater 
physiological flexibility and resilience to environmental variability. In contrast, Beaupre 
and Gibecq appeared more vulnerable to conditions associated with heat and moisture 
[PDF PAGE 90]
 
70 
 
stress. These findings highlight the importance of genotype selection for maintaining 
productivity and C sequestration under changing climatic conditions and suggest that 
Trichobel may possess greater long-term potential for biomass production, and therefore 
C accumulation, under future climatic conditions than Beaupre and Gibecq. 
2.5.  Conclusions  
This study provides one of the first long-term assessments of AGB C accumulation, 
MAIC, and PAIC in a mature poplar-based silvoarable systems. The findings 
demonstrate that C sequestration dynamics vary substantially among poplar hybrids and 
are influenced by both climatic conditions and the configuration of agroforestry 
systems. Together, these factors shaped both the magnitude and temporal patterns of 
AGB C accumulation over the 30-year study period. 
Among the four hybrids evaluated, Trichobel exhibited the greatest long-term C 
sequestration potential, achieving the highest AGB C stock (65.8 Mg C ha⁻¹), almost 
double that of the lowest-performing hybrid, Gibecq (33.03 Mg C ha⁻¹). Although 
Beaupre exhibited greater C accumulation during the first decade, Trichobel 
outperformed all other hybrids over the full 30-year study period through sustained 
productivity during later stages of stand development. These contrasting trajectories 
demonstrate that early growth performance does not necessarily predict long-term C 
sequestration potential and highlight the importance of long-duration assessments when 
evaluating the climate change mitigation potential of agroforestry systems. 
The study also demonstrated the value of incorporating PAIC alongside MAIC when 
evaluating AGB C sequestration performance. While MAIC provided a useful measure 
of long-term C accumulation, PAIC revealed considerable year-to-year variation in 
productivity and C uptake that would not have been captured by MAIC alone.  
Comparison of PAIC and MAIC trajectories revealed clear differences in growth 
dynamics among hybrids, with Trichobel maintaining productivity for longer and 
reaching maturity later than Beaupre, Gibecq, and Robusta. These findings emphasise 
that short-term monitoring may not adequately capture long-term trends in AGB C 
sequestration potential. 
[PDF PAGE 91]
 
71 
 
The results further revealed distinct responses of poplar hybrids to climatic variability. 
Summer climatic conditions exerted the strongest influence on PAIC, particularly for 
Trichobel and Beaupre, highlighting the importance of weather during the primary 
growing season in regulating annual AGB C accumulation. Climatic responses were 
strongly hybrid-specific. Beaupre and Gibecq showed reduced growth under higher 
summer soil temperatures, whereas Trichobel responded positively to summer rainfall 
and seasonal wind conditions. These results indicate substantial variation in climatic 
sensitivity among poplar hybrids and suggest that hybrid selection may play an 
important role in maintaining productivity under changing environmental conditions. 
Management practices also influenced AGB C sequestration, particularly during the 
active silvoarable phase of the experiment (1993-2024). During this period, the crop-
free treatment exhibited the highest MAIC and PAIC values, indicating that the absence 
of crop competition promoted greater tree growth and C uptake.  In contrast, trees 
subjected to greater crop competition accumulated less biomass and accumulated less C. 
Following the cessation of cropping, treatment differences diminished suggesting that 
competitive effects were largely restricted to periods of active crop-tree interaction and 
that previously suppressed trees were able to partially compensate for earlier reductions 
in growth. Overall, these findings highlight the importance of minimising crop-tree 
competition when maximising AGB C sequestration is a primary management 
objective. 
Overall, the findings show that mature poplar-based silvoarable systems can make a 
meaningful contribution to climate change mitigation by accumulating C in their AGB. 
However, the magnitude of this contribution is strongly influenced by hybrid selection, 
climatic conditions, and management practices. Trichobel consistently exhibited the 
strongest C accumulation performance and the greatest resilience to climatic variability, 
highlighting the importance of selecting appropriate genotypes when designing 
agroforestry systems for C sequestration objectives. More broadly, the study 
demonstrates the value of long-term monitoring for accurately quantifying agroforestry 
AGB C stocks and sequestration rates, and provides evidence that can help improve C 
accounting, inform agroforestry policy, and guide the development of climate-smart 
land-use systems. 
[PDF PAGE 92]
 
72 
 
Chapter 3.  Soil carbon sequestration in a poplar silvoarable system 
and the potential contribution of agroforestry expansion to UK net-
zero targets 
Abstract 
Agroforestry systems are increasingly promoted as a nature-based solution for climate 
change mitigation, yet their potential contribution to national ‘net-zero’ targets remains 
uncertain, particularly for temperate countries. This study quantified soil organic carbon 
(SOC) stocks and sequestration within a mature poplar silvoarable system and evaluated 
the potential carbon (C) sequestration of large-scale silvoarable expansion across UK 
croppable and arable land, with a focus on 2050. Soil organic carbon stocks (0–30 cm) 
averaged 71.70 Mg C ha⁻¹ in tree rows and 75.71 Mg C ha⁻¹ in alleys, compared with 
58.92 Mg C ha⁻¹ in the adjacent arable field. Consequently, the silvoarable system 
stored 27% more SOC than the conventional arable system. The average net SOC 
sequestration rate attributable to silvoarable agroforestry was 0.55 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹, with 
rates of 0.43 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ and 0.56 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ in tree rows and alleys, 
respectively. Three large-scale planting scenarios were modelled to assess the 
contribution of poplar silvoarable agroforestry to national C sequestration. Across all 
scenarios, aboveground biomass (AGB) represented the largest C pool, accounting for 
66% of total C uptake, while belowground biomass (BGB) and SOC both contributed 
17%. Carbon sequestration varied substantially among poplar hybrids, with Trichobel 
consistently outperforming the other hybrids and sequestering approximately 52% more 
C than the least productive hybrid, Gibecq, across all scenarios and land-use types. 
Estimated annual CO₂ removals ranged from 0.87 to 7.16 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹, highlighting the 
strong influence of agroforestry adoption rates on mitigation potential. Overall, mature 
poplar silvoarable systems provided substantial long-term C sequestration, with 83% 
stored in biomass and 17% in SOC. However, hybrid selection, planting density, and 
deployment scale strongly influenced sequestration outcomes. As modern silvoarable 
systems typically employ wider tree spacing and lower tree densities than the system 
examined here, future C sequestration rates are likely to be lower than those reported in 
this study. Nevertheless, strategically deployed poplar silvoarable agroforestry could 
[PDF PAGE 93]
 
73 
 
make a valuable contribution to UK agricultural decarbonisation and net-zero objectives 
when integrated within a broader landscape-scale climate mitigation strategy.    
3.1.  Introduction 
In recent years, agroforestry has gained increasing attention as a climate change 
mitigation strategy due to the integration of woody species into agricultural landscapes. 
This approach enhances C sequestration and contributes to the achievement of national 
net zero targets while supporting the goals of the Paris Agreement (IPCC, 2002; Zomer 
et al., 2016; Cardinael et al., 2018a; Mayer et al., 2022). Consequently, agroforestry is 
increasingly recognised within national climate policy frameworks and is often included 
in Nationally Determined Contributions under the Paris Agreement (Westaway et al., 
2023).  
Within the European Union (EU), agroforestry was formally incorporated into the 
Common Agricultural Policy (CAP) in 2005 under both Pillars I and II, marking an 
important policy milestone (Mosquera-Losada et al., 2018). Since then, it has received 
increasing attention as a multifunctional land-use system that supports climate 
mitigation, biodiversity conservation, and sustainable agricultural production (Augère-
Granier, 2020). Agroforestry currently covers approximately 6.5% of utilised 
agricultural land in the EU, with most systems located in the Mediterranean region (den 
Herder et al., 2017). More recently, the EU Forestry Strategy set a target of planting an 
additional 3 billion trees across Europe by 2030, explicitly supporting the promotion of 
agroforestry as a multifunctional land-use system (European Commission, 2021). 
Furthermore, Gabourel-Landaverde et al. (2025) estimated that adopting agroforestry on 
30.4% of European agricultural land would be required to achieve significant climate 
change mitigation and environmental benefits.  
In the UK, and in particular England, policy frameworks in the 21st century have 
increasingly focused on tree planting as part of its climate mitigation strategy. As of 
2020, approximately 1,310,000 ha, or 10% of the UK's total land area, was covered by 
woodland, making it one of the least forested countries in Europe (Image et al., 2023). 
This low woodland cover highlights the need to increase tree presence across the 
landscape, including integrating trees within productive agricultural systems. Agri-
[PDF PAGE 94]
 
74 
 
environment schemes such as Countryside Stewardship and the Sustainable Farming 
Initiative (SFI) have therefore been used to encourage farmers to plant trees on 
agricultural land, by providing grants and ongoing management payments to 
landowners (Defra, 2022 and Forestry Commission & Natural England, 2024). 
The UK has set a target to achieve net-zero greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 2050 
under the Climate Change Act (UK Government, 2019). Agriculture currently accounts 
for approximately 12% of total UK GHG emissions, a proportion that is expected to 
increase as emissions from other sectors continue to decrease. Consequently, both the 
government’s Net Zero Strategy and the Carbon Budget Delivery Plan emphasise that 
land-use change, particularly the expansion of tree and woodland cover, has significant 
mitigation potential. Consequently, there is an ambition to increase tree cover by 
30,000–70,000 ha annually, including greater integration of trees within agricultural 
systems through agroforestry (Image et al., 2023). More specifically, the UK Climate 
Change Committee has proposed planting trees on 10% of agricultural land by 2050 as 
part of its pathway towards net-zero emissions (CCC, 2021). Despite this policy 
support, progress toward these agroforestry targets in the UK remains limited, and 
silvoarable agroforestry is still rare in practice. It currently occupies only 0.05% of 
arable land in the UK, compared to 0.41% across the rest of Europe (den Herder et al., 
2017).  
Alongside policy development, C offsetting and insetting have become increasingly 
important mechanisms for organisations seeking to achieve net-zero emissions targets 
(e.g., Black et al., 2022; Phelan et al., 2024). Carbon offsetting involves compensating 
for emissions through investments in external C sequestration or avoidance projects, 
whereas insetting focuses on reducing emissions within an organisation's own supply 
chain. Both approaches have contributed to increased interest in large-scale tree planting 
on agricultural land as a strategy for mitigating climate change (Awazi et al., 2025a). 
However, it remains unclear to what extent the UK’s Climate Change Committee target 
of a 10% increase in farmland tree cover will contribute to achieving net-zero emissions 
by 2050. In addition, despite growing awareness of the benefits of silvoarable 
agroforestry and increasing policy support, uptake of this practice in the UK remains 
very limited (Felton et al., 2023). Robust monitoring and verification of agroforestry C 
[PDF PAGE 95]
 
75 
 
sequestration potential are needed to support climate incentive mechanisms, including C 
markets and government-funded schemes (Feliciano et al., 2018).  
There is a significant knowledge gap regarding the amount of C stored and sequestered 
in different agroforestry systems, as well as the rates at which C is sequestered in both 
the biomass and soil over time following establishment. Previous studies have 
demonstrated that silvoarable agroforestry systems possess considerable C sequestration 
potential. For example, Graves et al. (2005) and Kay et al. (2019) estimated that 
silvoarable systems can sequester approximately 2-8 tonnes of CO2 ha-1 yr-1. However, 
empirical data, particularly for temperate regions, remain limited. Kay et al. (2019) 
reported that the AGB C accumulation in European silvoarable systems ranged from 
0.16 to 2.80 Mg C ha-1 yr-1. Few studies have simultaneously quantified C stocks in 
both soil and biomass in temperate silvoarable systems (Dollinger and Jose, 2018 and 
Bateni et al., 2021). Thus, improving our understanding of the C sequestration potential 
of temperate agroforestry systems is urgently needed. for improving the accuracy of 
climate change mitigation models and facilitating the wider promotion and adoption of 
agroforestry as a practice for atmospheric CO2 capture and long-term storage.   
As a fast-growing tree species, poplar is particularly well suited to C farming and serves 
as an effective nature-based solution for climate change mitigation through its high C 
sequestration potential (Zhang et al., 2020; Joshi et al., 2024). However, there is limited 
information on the total C sequestration potential and CO₂ uptake of the most 
representative poplar hybrids grown under UK conditions. Consequently, substantial 
uncertainty remains regarding the contribution that poplar-based silvoarable systems 
could make towards national net-zero targets. 
The present research aims to quantify soil C sequestration in a temperate poplar-based 
silvoarable system and combine it with biomass C sequestration presented in Chapter 2 
to assess its potential contribution to achieving the UK Climate Change Committee's 
net-zero target. Specifically, the study will assess the extent to which agroforestry 
adoption could contribute to C sequestration by 2050. This will be achieved by the 
following objectives: 
[PDF PAGE 96]
 
76 
 
1. Quantify SOC stocks within different components of a poplar-based silvoarable 
system (tree rows and alleys) and compare these with the SOC stocks in an adjacent 
conventional arable field. 
2. Determine the mean annual SOC sequestration rate of a 30-year old poplar 
silvoarable system in northern England.  
3. Integrate SOC sequestration rates with above- and belowground biomass C 
sequestration estimates to model the potential contribution of silvoarable expansion to 
UK net-zero targets by projecting total C and CO2 sequestration under a range of 
adoption scenarios designed to achieve either 5% or 10% agroforestry coverage on 
croppable and arable land by 2050. 
3.2.  Methods  
3.2.1.  Study site  
This research was carried out at the commercially-run University of Leeds farm located 
near Tadcaster, northern England. The long-term (1993-2023) mean annual temperature 
was 10.9°C, and mean annual rainfall was 767 mm (Bramham station 534, Met Office, 
2023). The soil is dominated by well-drained calcaric brown earths, which correspond 
to Calcaric Endoleptic Cambisols of the Aberford Series. These soil types are widely 
associated with Permian and Jurassic limestone in England (Pun et al., 2024).  
The 2.3 ha poplar silvoarable system was established in 1992 in one of the arable field 
on the farm, as outlined by Burgess et al. (2003). Briefly, it comprises four poplar 
hybrids: Trichobel (T), Gibecq (G), Beaupre (B), and Robusta (R) and was initially 
combined with three cropping treatments: continuously fallow (CF; i.e. no crops), 
continuously cropped (CC), and alternately cropped (AC; i.e. cropping on one side of 
the tree row only) (1993 -2004). All crops were managed according to standard 
commercial practices, receiving appropriate applications of fertiliser and pesticides. The 
combinations of the four hybrids and three cropping treatments were arranged in three 
replicated blocks (Fig 3.1).   
[PDF PAGE 97]
 
77 
 
 
Figure 3.1 Soil sampling locations within each of the three locations. Base image 
source: Esri (2025). World Imagery [Basemap]. © Esri, Maxar, Earthstar 
Geographics, and the GIS User Community 
Nineteen north–south-oriented tree rows were established, with 6.4 m spacing between 
trees within rows and 10 m spacing between adjacent tree rows. Each tree row consisted 
of groups of five consecutive trees of each hybrid, with a guard tree planted at each end. 
This design resulted in a planting density of 166 trees per ha. The entire experimental 
site covers 2.3 ha, with tree rows occupying 0.49 ha and alleys covering 1.84 ha (see 
Chapter 2 for more site details). Cropping within alleys ceased after the 2004 harvest 
due to increasing competition between trees and crops for resources. Since then, 
unmanaged natural vegetation has replaced the original cropping treatments in the 
alleys.  
3.2.2.  Sampling strategy 
Soil sampling was conducted in January 2024 using a modified all-terrain vehicle 
equipped with a hydraulic core extractor to collect intact 36-mm-diameter soil cores. 

[PDF PAGE 98]
 
78 
 
Soil cores were sampled to bedrock. The mean (n=72) total core depth was 50 (±16) cm. 
The vehicle was equipped with GPS trackers that recorded the sampling location of 
each soil core. Soil cores were kept intact in air and water-sealed tubes and refrigerated 
until processing. Before soil analysis, soil cores were subdivided into three depths 
corresponding to 0-15 cm, 15-30 cm, and 30+ cm. However, we report total SOC stock 
only for the 0–30 cm layer because soil depth below 30 cm was inconsistent between 
locations due to shallow soils overlying limestone, particularly in the arable field. In 
addition, many studies recommend sampling topsoil to a depth of 0–30 cm, as it 
represents the most active zone for plant growth. 
Eight soil cores were collected from tree rows 6, 10, and 14, which meant samples were 
collected from each of the three blocks and each of the poplar hybrids. Eight soil cores 
were taken from the alleys 5 m to the centre-right of the cores collected in each of the 
tree rows (6, 10, and 14, looking north). For each selected tree row, two soil cores were 
sampled from each of the four hybrids, resulting in eight cores per tree row (24 cores in 
total, with 6 cores per hybrid across all rows). By sampling the alleys to the right of the 
tree rows, eight cores from each of the different cropping management histories: CF, 
CC, and AC were included. In addition, eight soil cores were sampled out from the tree 
rows and alleys into the adjacent arable field (Fig. 3.1), resulting in the collection of 48 
soil samples from the arable field. 
We assume that the arable field is representative of the soil conditions prior to the 
establishment of the silvoarable system. It is a space-for-time substitution approach, 
where existing arable land is considered a record of the pre-silvoarable situation. As the 
agroforestry plot was established on land that had previously been managed as arable, 
the surrounding arable field is considered an appropriate reference for baseline soil 
conditions in the absence of pre-conversion measurements of SOC. 
3.2.3.  Soil analysis 
3.2.3.1.  Bulk density, SOC content and SOC stock  
Once soil cores were divided into three samples, individual soil samples were dried at 
75°C for a minimum of 1.5 hours and sieved to 2 mm to determine bulk density (g cm-
[PDF PAGE 99]
 
79 
 
3), and SOC content (SOCcon, g kg-1). Bulk density was calculated as the mass of the 
dry sample divided by its volume. Gravel (>2 mm) was removed from each sample, and 
its mass was recorded (Masscoarse, g).  By using a standard constant coarse fraction 
density (ρcoarse) of 2.65 g cm-3, the fine soil fraction bulk density (also known as fine 
soil stock), which will be referred to as bulk density from here onwards, was calculated 
following Poeplau et al. (2017):  
𝐵𝑢𝑙𝑘 𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦 =  
dry sample mass − Masscoarse  
sample volume − (𝑀𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑐𝑜𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒
𝜌𝑐𝑜𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒 )
    (Equation 3.1)  
SOCcon of the fine earth fraction was determined for a subsample of dried soil (< 2 
mm) ground to a fine powder. Inorganic C was removed from soil samples by reaction 
with 10% (v/v) hydrochloric acid, and SOCcon was measured by dry combustion using 
an automated C: N analyser. Results were reported to two decimal points (e.g. 0.01 g 
kg-1). Finally, SOC stock (Mg C ha-1) was calculated as: 
𝑆𝑂𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘=  𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑐𝑜𝑛 ∗  𝐵𝐷 ∗  𝑑𝑒𝑝𝑡ℎ 𝑖𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑣𝑎𝑙       (Equation 3.2)  
3.2.4.  Data and statistical analysis  
The normality of bulk density, soil organic carbon concentration (SOCcon), and SOC 
stock data was assessed using the Shapiro-Wilk test. As the data were not normally 
distributed, non-parametric statistical analyses were used. The Kruskal-Wallis test was 
applied to evaluate differences in bulk density, SOCcon, and SOC stock among soil 
depth intervals (0-15 and 15-30 cm) and sampling locations (alleys, tree rows, and 
arable field). In addition, differences among cropping treatments were assessed using a 
block-based experimental design, in which each block represented one of the three 
cropping treatments (CF, CC, or AC). Where significant differences were detected, 
Dunn's post hoc multiple comparison test was performed to identify pairwise 
differences between groups. Statistical significance was accepted at P < 0.05. All 
statistical analyses were conducted in RStudio (R version 4.3.3). 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 100]
 
80 
 
3.2.5.  SOC sequestration rate 
The annual soil sequestration (Mg C ha−1 yr−1) rate for the tree and alley location was 
determined as: 
𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑠𝑒𝑞𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑒𝑦𝑠 =  𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑒𝑦𝑠− 𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑎𝑟𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒
𝑦𝑒𝑎𝑟𝑠 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝑐𝑒 𝑝𝑙𝑎𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑔    (Equation 3.3) 
 
𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑠𝑒𝑞𝑡𝑟𝑒𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑤𝑠 =    𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑡𝑟𝑒𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑤𝑠 − 𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑎𝑟𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒
𝑦𝑒𝑎𝑟𝑠 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝑐𝑒 𝑝𝑙𝑎𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑔  (Equation 3.4) 
To determine the overall annual average SOC sequestration rate for the whole 
silvoarable site (2.3 ha and 166 trees ha-1), the total SOC stored in the silvoarable site 
(SOCsilvoarable) to 30 cm depth was determined by adding the amount of SOC stored in 
the area occupied by tree rows with the amount of SOC stored in the area occupied by 
alleys. The mean (X̄ ) SOC storage of the tree rows and alleys was calculated as:  
𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑒𝑦𝑠 =  X̄ 𝑆𝑂𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑒𝑦𝑠∗  𝐴𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑒𝑦𝑠              (Equation 3.5) 
Then the average SOC stock (Mg C ha-1) of the silvoarable site was calculated by using:  
 𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘𝑠𝑖𝑙𝑣𝑜𝑎𝑟𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒
𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙𝑎𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑖𝑙𝑣𝑜𝑎𝑟𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒              (Equation 3.6) 
The change in SOC stock that has occurred over 30 years since establishment of the 
silvoarable system was determined by calculating the difference between the SOC stock 
in silvoarble and arable field: 
𝛥𝑆𝑂𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘 =  𝑆𝑂𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘𝑠𝑖𝑙𝑣𝑜𝑎𝑟𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒 −  𝑆𝑂𝐶 𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘𝑎𝑟𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒 𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑙𝑑     (Equation 3.7)           
Finally, the mean annual SOC sequestration rate (Mg C ha-1 yr-1) was calculated as: 
𝑆𝑂𝐶 𝑠𝑒𝑞𝑢𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒=
 𝛥𝑆𝑂𝐶𝑠𝑡𝑜𝑐𝑘
𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟 𝑜𝑓 𝑦𝑒𝑎𝑟𝑠 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝑐𝑒 𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑖𝑠ℎ𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡       (Equation 3.8) 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 101]
 
81 
 
3.2.6.  Upscaling of results to estimate the climate change mitigation 
potential of silvoarable system 
We followed the UK Climate Change Committee's target of converting 10% of 
croppable/arable land to agroforestry by 2050 (CCC, 2020). In the UK, the total 
croppable area, defined as the land utilised for agriculture, including crop production 
and temporary grass, is approximately 6,000,000 ha, of which 4,100,000 ha is dedicated 
to annual arable crops (Defra, 2025). Applying the CCC target to all croppable land 
corresponds to the establishment of 600,000 ha of silvoarable agroforestry, whereas 
applying the target only to land currently under annual arable crops corresponds to 
410,000 ha. Given the relatively low uptake of silvoarable in the UK, an additional 
adoption target of 5% of croppable and arable land was also evaluated.   
Three planting scenarios were evaluated:  
Scenario 1: all 600,000 or 410,000 ha (representing 10% of total croppable or arable 
land) are converted to silvoarable systems in a single year (2025). 
Scenario 2: a total of 600,000 or 410,000 ha of silvoarable agroforestry is established 
over the period 2025-2050, at annual rates of 24,000 or 16,400 ha yr⁻¹, respectively.    
Scenario 3: a 5% adoption target is achieved over the period 2025-2050, corresponding 
to the establishment of 300,000 ha of croppable land or 205,000 ha of arable land as 
silvoarable systems. This is equivalent to an annual planting rate of 12,000 and 8200 
ha yr⁻¹, respectively. 
For each scenario, total C and CO2 sequestered by AGB, belowground biomass (BGB), 
and SOC were estimated to 2050 for each poplar hybrid, representing 25 years from the 
start of the scenarios. AGB sequestration rates were based on the periodic annual 
increment in carbon (PAIC) derived in Chapter 2. BGB sequestration was calculated 
using a shoot-to-root ratio of 0.26 (FAO, 2004), equivalent to 26% of the AGB PAIC 
(Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹). Soil organic carbon sequestration was assumed to occur at a constant 
rate of 0.55 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹. Total C sequestration for each hybrid was obtained by 
summing the contributions from AGB, BGB, and SOC. 
[PDF PAGE 102]
 
82 
 
In Scenario 1, the entire target area (600,000 or 410,000 ha) is converted to silvoarable 
systems in a single year (2025). Consequently, the full area contributes to C 
sequestration from 2026 onwards. Annual AGB PAIC for each hybrid was multiplied 
by the total converted area for each year to 2050, and the cumulative AGB was 
calculated as the sum of annual sequestration values over the 25-year period. BGB and 
SOC sequestration were calculated using the same approach. 
In Scenario 2, silvoarable targets are reached incrementally from 2025 to 2050 at annual 
establishment rates of 24,000 or 16,400 ha yr⁻¹. Carbon sequestration was therefore 
calculated using a cohort-based approach. In the first year, only one cohort contributes 
to sequestration, resulting in annual AGB sequestration of AGB PAIC × 24,000 or 
16,400 ha. In the second year, two cohorts contribute; in the third year, three cohorts, 
and so on. BGB and SOC sequestration increments were calculated using the same 
cumulative cohort method.  
Scenario 3 follows the same cohort-based approach as Scenario 2 but assumes 
incremental adoption on only 5% of croppable or arable land over 25 years. Annual and 
cumulative AGB, BGB, and SOC sequestration were calculated using annual 
establishment rates of 12,000 and 8,200 ha yr⁻¹ for croppable and arable land, 
respectively. 
For all scenarios, C uptake was converted to CO2 using a factor of 3.67 (IPCC, 2006). 
Total CO2 uptake for each hybrid was calculated by summing the CO2 equivalents 
derived from AGB, BGB, and SOC sequestration.  
3.3.  Results 
3.3.1.  Bulk density 
Bulk density was significantly affected by both soil depth and location within the 
silvoarable system (p < 0.001; Table 3.1). Across all locations, bulk density increased 
with depth, with values at 15–30 cm significantly higher than those at 0–15 cm. Tree 
rows exhibited the lowest bulk density at both depths (1.01 g cm⁻³ at 0–15 cm; 
1.16 g cm⁻³ at 15–30 cm). At 0-15 cm soil depth, bulk density showed highly significant 
differences between tree rows and the arable field (p < 0.001) and significant 
[PDF PAGE 103]
 
83 
 
differences between alleys and the arable field (p < 0.05), while tree rows and alleys 
were not statistically significant. At 15-30 cm depth, significant differences were 
observed between tree rows and alleys (p < 0.01) and tree rows and the arable field (p < 
0.05), whereas higher values were observed in the alleys and the arable field (Table 3.1) 
did not differ significantly (p > 0.05). 
Table 3.1 Mean and (median) bulk density, SOC%, and SOC stock by location and

measurements.

given soil depth (p < 0.05). Values sharing at least one common letter are not significantly 
different. An asterisk (*) indicates a significant difference between soil depths within the same 
location. 
3.3.2.  SOC concentration  
SOCcon was highest in the tree rows, averaging 2.86% and having a median of 2.83% 
in the 0–15 cm layer. At the 0-15 cm soil depth, SOCcon was significantly greater under 
tree rows and alleys compared with the arable field (p < 0.001), whereas tree rows and 
alleys did not differ. At 15-30 cm depth, SOCcon was comparable in the alleys and 
arable field (Table 3.1), while it was higher under tree rows than in the arable field (p < 
0.001). Across all locations, SOCcon was greater in the surface layer than at 15-30 cm 
depth, with a significant effect of depth on SOCcon (p<0.001).  
3.3.3.  SOC stock 
At depths of 0–15 cm, SOC stock varied significantly among locations. The arable field 
exhibited significantly lower values than both the alleys (p < 0.001) and tree rows (p < 
[PDF PAGE 104]
 
84 
 
0.001, Fig 3.2), while no significant difference was detected between the tree rows and 
alleys (p = 0.725). In contrast, at a depth of 15–30 cm, SOC stock did not differ 
significantly among locations (p = 0.87) (Fig. 3.2, Table 3.1). When the SOC stock for 
the entire soil profile (0-30 cm) is considered, the tree rows and alleys contained 21.7% 
and 28.5% more C, respectively, than the arable field. In addition, the arable field had 
significantly lower SOC stocks than in both the alleys and tree rows (p<0.001, while no 
significant difference was observed between the tree rows and alleys (p = 0.21, Fig. 
3.2). Moreover, at 0-30 cm, SOC stock differed significantly between the AC and CC 
treatments (p = 0.02), with the treatment effect being consistent in Blocks 2 and 3.  
 
Figure 3.2 Distribution of SOC stock at 0–15 and 15-30 cm depths across three 
treatments: (tree rows (n=24), alleys (n=24), and arable field (n=48)), illustrated using 
a box-and-whisker plot. The box plot shows the median and first and third quartiles (the 
25th and 75th percentiles). Different letters indicate statistically significant differences 
according to post hoc Dunn's tests within the same soil depth (p < 0.05). 
 
 
 

[PDF PAGE 105]
 
85 
 
3.3.4.  SOC storage 
Table 3.2 shows that within the silvoarable system, SOC was distributed between tree 
rows (35 Mg C) and alleys (140 Mg C). This represented a mean SOC stock of 75 Mg C 
ha⁻¹ within the 0–30 cm soil layer, representing an increase of 16.58 Mg C ha⁻¹ (27%) 
compared with the adjacent arable field (59 Mg C ha⁻¹). Estimated SOC sequestration 
rates were 0.46 and 0.58 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ for the tree rows and alleys, respectively. 
Relative to the arable field, the additional SOC stock associated with the whole 
silvoarable system corresponded to a net SOC sequestration rate of 0.55 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ 
(Table 3.2).

agroforestry system at 0–30 cm soil depth 
 
 
 
 
 
 
3.3.5.  Climate change mitigation potential of establishing silvoarable 
systems 
The cumulative C uptake in AGB, BGB and SOC for each poplar hybrid under the three 
scenarios is presented in Fig. 3.3 for croppable land and Fig. 3.4 for arable land. 
Although the temporal trends were similar across hybrids and scenarios, the magnitude 
of C uptake differed, as shown by the differing y-axis scales in Figs 3.3 and 3.4. This 
indicates that while the pattern of response is consistent, the magnitude of the 
measurements differs between scenarios. Across all scenarios, total C uptake 
consistently ranked Trichobel> Beaupre > Robusta > Gibecq.  
Locations at 0-30 cm  
SOC stored in the tree rows (Mg C) 35

Additional stock (Mg C ha-1) in silvoarable system 16.84 
SOC sequestration in tree rows (Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹) 0.43 
SOC sequestration in alleys (Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹) 0.56 
SOC sequestration in silvoarable system (Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹) 0.55 
[PDF PAGE 106]
 
86 
 
Under Scenario 1 for croppable land, Trichobel recorded the greatest cumulative C 
uptake over the 25-year period, totalling 48.77 Tg C. Of this, AGB accounted for 65.9% 
(32.16 Tg C) BGB for 17.1% (8.36 Tg C) and SOC for 16.9% (8.25 Tg C) (Table 3.2; 
Fig. 3.3). Beaupre and Robusta followed similar trends but achieved lower cumulative 
C uptakes of 34.34 and 32.59, respectively. Gibecq recorded the lowest total C uptake 
of 29.19 Tg C. Consequently, the total C sequestration potential of Gibecq was 
approximately 40% lower than that of Trichobel over the 25-year period. 
 
Figure 3.3 Croppable land: Cumulative C uptake by AGB, BGB and SOC for four 
poplar hybrids across three silvoarable establishment scenarios on UK croppable land: 
(1) establishment of all 600,000 ha in a single year, (2) an annual establishment rate of 
24,000 ha-1yr-1 over 25 years and (3) an annual establishment rate of 12,000 ha-1yr-1 
over 25 years.  
Under Scenario 1 for arable land, Trichobel still had the highest cumulative C uptake, 
reaching 33.33 Tg C after 25 years (Table 3.3). However, this was 15.44 Tg C (or 
31.7%) lower than the value estimated for croppable land. The remaining hybrids 
retained the same ranking, with cumulative C values of 23.47 Tg C for Beaupre, 22.27 
Tg C for Robusta, and 19.95 Tg C for Gibecq. As a result, Trichobel sequestered 

[PDF PAGE 107]
 
87 
 
between 9.86 and 13.38 Tg C more C than the other hybrids under arable land 
conversion to silvoarable.  
Scenario 3 assumes the establishment of silvoarable agroforestry on only half the area 
converted under Scenario 2; total C uptake by all hybrids was correspondingly 50% 
lower (Figs 3.3 and 3.4; Table 3.3). For example, in Scenario 2, Trichobel sequestered a 
total of 2.67 and 1.61 Tg C over 25 years when established on croppable and arable 
land, respectively. In Scenario 3, cumulative C uptake declined to 1.18 Tg C on 
croppable land and 0.8 Tg C on arable land. These reductions reflect the smaller area 
available for agroforestry establishment under Scenario 3 than Scenario 2.    
 
Figure 3.4 Arable land: Cumulative C uptake by AGB, BGB and SOC for four poplar 
hybrids across three silvoarable establishment scenarios on UK arable land (1) 
establishment of all 410,000 ha in one year, (2) an establishment of 16,400 ha yr -1 over 
25 years, and (3) an establishment rate of 8,200 ha yr-1 over 25 years.  
Furthermore, the rates of C uptake under Scenario 2 were approximately half those 
achieved under Scenario 1. This highlights the importance of the rate and extent of 
silvoarable agroforestry adoption in determining its overall contribution to C 
sequestration and net-zero targets. Although all scenarios demonstrated positive C 
sequestration benefits, the cumulative C uptake achieved over the 25-year period was 

[PDF PAGE 108]
 
88 
 
strongly dependent on the hybrids and area converted to agroforestry and the pace of 
establishment. 
Table 3.3 illustrates the variation in total C and CO2 uptake among scenarios and poplar 
hybrids. For all hybrids, total CO2 uptake declined substantially from Scenario 1 to 
Scenario 3 as the area and rate of silvoarable agroforestry establishment decreased. 
Under Scenario 1, an optimal but unrealistic scenario in which all new silvoarable is 
established within a single year across either 600,000 ha of croppable land or 410,000 
ha of arable land, Trichobel was projected to sequester 178.99 and 122.32 Tg of CO2, 
respectively, by 2050. Under Scenario 2, where silvoarable establishment occurs 
steadily over 25 years at rates of 24,000 and 16,400 ha yr⁻¹ on croppable and arable 
land, respectively, CO2 sequestration by Trichobel was reduced by 51.44 and 51.49% 
less relative to Scenario 1 by 2050. Scenario 3 represented the most realistic (but still 
ambitious) scenario, given the very low area of silvoarable across the UK, where 
silvoarable is established on 5% of croppable/arable land. Under this scenario, 
Trichobel, the most productive hybrid, was projected to sequester 43.45 Tg of CO2 on 
croppable land and 29.73 Tg of CO2 on arable land by 2050. In contrast, Gibecq, the 
least productive hybrid, was projected to sequester 32.26 and 21.72 Tg of CO2 on 
croppable and arable land, respectively, by 2050. For Trichobel, these values represent a 
75.7% reduction in CO2 sequestration potential relative to Scenario 1 for both land use 
categories.  
Across all scenarios and land-use types, Trichobel consistently exhibited the greatest 
CO2 sequestration potential, achieving approximately 52% greater sequestration than 
Gibecq (Table 3.3). These findings demonstrate that both the extent of agroforestry 
adoption and hybrid selection strongly influence the contribution of silvoarable systems 
towards achieving net-zero targets.

3.4.  Discussion  
3.4.1.  Bulk density and SOC  
Bulk density was significantly lower in the tree rows than in both the alleys and the 
adjacent arable field at the 0-15 cm depth, and bulk density also differed significantly 
between the alleys and arable field (Table 3.1). At 15-30 cm depth, bulk density 
remained significantly lower in the tree rows, whereas values in the alleys and arable 
field were not significantly different. This pattern may reflect both historical machinery-
induced compaction in the alleys and arable field during the early years of the 
silvoarable phase (Upson and Burgess, 2013; Correa et al., 2019) and the gradual 
recovery of soil structure within the tree rows where machineries cannot access. The 
absence of differences between the alleys and adjacent arable field after approximately 
20 years suggests either persistent legacy effects of past management or similar rates of 
soil structural recovery. In addition, canopy-derived litter inputs may extend into the 
alleys, potentially reducing the magnitude of expected differences between these 
locations.  Moreover, the lower bulk density observed within tree rows may result from 
greater organic matter inputs from litterfall, root turnover, and understorey vegetation, 
which can improve soil structure and reduce soil density. This interpretation is 
supported by the well-established negative relationship between soil organic matter and 
bulk density (Chaudhari et al., 2013; Robinson et al., 2025). Further SOC concentration 
results support these findings, as the greatest difference was observed between tree rows 
and arable field at the 15-30 cm soil depth, suggesting that greater accumulation of 
organic matter under tree rows may have contributed to the observed variation in soil 
physical properties. 
SOC stocks within the silvoarable system (72.5–76.2 Mg C ha⁻¹, 0–30 cm) were similar 
to those reported from other temperate poplar agroforestry systems (Table 3.4), 
including the 78.5 Mg C ha⁻¹ reported for a 13-year-old poplar system in Canada 
(Peichl et al., 2006). Furthermore, the SOC stocks measured in the present study (72.5-
76.2 Mg C ha⁻¹, 0-30 cm) are comparable to those reported for mature poplar-based 
silvoarable agroforestry systems elsewhere, including the 78.5 Mg C ha⁻¹ reported by 
Peichl et al. (2006) in Canada. 
[PDF PAGE 111]
 
91 
 
The relatively high SOC stock observed in this study may reflect the long-term 
development of the system (30 years old) and the cessation of cropping in 2004, which 
could have favoured continued organic matter inputs from tree litter and naturally 
established ground vegetation. However, these mechanisms were not directly assessed 
and therefore remain speculative. Thus, the land-use change context in this study needs 
to be noted.  Direct comparisons between studies should also be interpreted with caution 
because SOC stocks are strongly influenced by initial land use, sampling depth, soil 
types, stand age, tree species, climate and management history (Cardinael et al., 2018b; 
Upson and Burgess, 2013). The studies summarised in Table 3.4 sampled soils at depths 
ranging from 15 to 100 cm, limiting direct quantitative comparisons. Compared with the 
conventional arable field, the silvoarable system exhibited significantly higher SOC 
stocks in the surface layer (0-15 cm; Table 3.1 and Fig. 3.2). 
This suggests that enhanced litter inputs, root turnover, and microbial activity promoted 
higher C accumulation. These effects are most likely related to the development of the 
poplar canopy, which increased litter deposition not only in tree rows but also into 
adjacent alleys. Following termination of intercropping in 2004, the establishment of 
understorey vegetation may have provided additional sources of organic matter. 
Consequently, no significant difference in SOC stocks was observed between tree rows 
and alleys, suggesting that tree-derived C inputs were distributed across both locations 
rather than being confined to the tree rows. This is maybe because consistent with 
canopy litterfall extending into the alleys and may help explain the limited spatial 
variation in SOC stocks despite the lower bulk density observed in the tree rows 
(Cardinael et al., 2015; Cardinael et al., 2018ab; Ivezić et al., 2022). Because at the 0- 
30 cm depth, SOC stock differed significantly between the AC and CC treatments (p = 
0.02), whereas SOC concentration and bulk density showed no significant treatment or 
block effects. This suggests that SOC stock still reflects the legacy of historical 
cropping practices, while more than two decades of natural vegetation growth have 
likely reduced treatment-related differences in SOC concentration and bulk density 
through continued litter inputs and root turnover. 
In contrast, SOC stocks did not differ among locations at 15–30 cm depth, suggesting 
that agroforestry-induced increases in SOC were mainly restricted to surface soils. This 
finding is consistent with previous studies, showing that deeper soil layers respond more 
[PDF PAGE 112]
 
92 
 
slowly to changes in vegetation and C inputs (Lal, 2004; Germon et al., 2016; Mayer et 
al., 2022).  
Table 3.4 SOC stock and sequestration rates reported for temperate silvoarable systems 
 
The 27% increase in SOC storage observed in the silvoarable system, when compared 
to the adjacent arable field, aligns with the typical global range of SOC stock increases 
(26-40%) reported following the conversion of conventional agricultural land to 
agroforestry systems, although responses vary according to soil depth and system type 
(De Stefano & Jacobson, 2018). The SOC sequestration rate recorded in this study (0.55 
Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹) exceeds the average rates reported for temperate alley-cropping systems 
in a recent global meta-analysis, which identified mean sequestration rates of 0.26 ± 
1.15 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ in topsoils (0-20 cm) and 0.23 ± 0.25 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ in subsoils 
(20-40 cm) (Mayer et al., 2022). Although higher than the global averages, the 
sequestration rate is within the range reported for other temperate silvoarable systems 
(0.19-1.04 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹; Table 3.4).  
Unlike most previous studies that reported a system-level average SOC sequestration 
rate, sequestration was quantified separately within tree rows and alleys in this study. 
This revealed pronounced spatial heterogeneity in the silvoarable system. While SOC

stocks were greater in the alleys, sequestration rates were highest in the tree rows 
(Tables 3.1 and 3.2), suggesting that active C accumulation is concentrated near trees 
where litterfall and root-derived inputs are greatest (Cardinael et al., 2018b). This 
within-system variability, together with differences in stand age, management and 
planting density, likely contributes to the wide range of sequestration rates reported 
across agroforestry systems (Table 3.4).  Such complexity may also help explain why 
SOC sequestration is often omitted in broader assessments of agroforestry contributions 
to net-zero targets, despite its potential importance as a long-term C sink and 
increasingly promoted by governments as a nature-based climate solution and a means 
of achieving Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) (Defra, 2022).  
The role of agroforestry system design should therefore be considered when evaluating 
future C sequestration potential on arable soils. Recent silvoarable systems increasingly 
adopt wider alley widths (>24m) to maximise crop production and improve operational 
efficiency (Staton et al., 2018; Judson et al., 2025; Reuse & Langhof, 2025). While 
wider alleys may improve agricultural productivity, they reduce tree density and may 
consequently lower system-level SOC stocks and long-term sequestration potential. 
Overall, these findings demonstrate that mature poplar based agroforesty systems can 
deliver measurable gains in SOC storage and sequestration relative to conventional 
arable land, while highlighting the importance of spatially explicit assessment and 
system design when maximising climate mitigation benefits. 
3.4.2.  Carbon sequestration potential of poplar silvoarable expansion under 
alternative adoption Scenarios 
This study provides one of the first data-driven assessments of the national-scale C 
sequestration potential of poplar silvoarable systems, incorporating C stored in AGB, 
BGB, and SOC (Fig. 3.3 and 3.4). The results demonstrated that both the extent and rate 
of silvoarable establishment strongly influence total C sequestration potential. The UK 
CCC identifies agroforestry as a key component of the pathway towards net-zero, with a 
target of establishing agroforestry on 10% of cropland by 2050 alongside a 40% 
increase in hedgerow extent (AHDB, 2025; CCC, 2020). The upscaling results 
demonstrate that poplar-based silvoarable agroforestry could make a substantial 
contribution to mitigating agricultural CO₂ emissions, with estimated annual CO₂ 
[PDF PAGE 114]
 
94 
 
removals ranged from 0.87 to 7.16 Mt CO₂eyr⁻¹ across the different adoption scenarios 
and hybrids evaluated (Fig. 3.3 and 3.4). Variation in sequestration potential was mainly 
driven by differences in the area allocated to agroforestry in each scenario, highlighting 
that the scale of adoption is more important determinant of national mitigation 
outcomes than differences in sequestration rates among hybrids alone. 
Relative to projected UK agricultural emissions of approximately 41 Mt CO₂e yr⁻¹ in 
2050, the highest-performing hybrid (Trichobel under Scenario 1) was estimated to 
offset approximately 17.3% of annual agricultural emissions, whereas the lowest-
performing hybrid (Gibecq under Scenario 3) still offset around 3.1%. Under Scenario 
1, hybrid poplar systems offset between 10.3 and 17.3% of projected agricultural 
emissions, while under Scenario 2, which reflects the CCC land-use change pathway, 
offsets ranged from 6.2 to 8.4%. Under Scenario 3, representing a more realistic, 
although still ambitious, level of agroforestry adoption, offsets ranged from 3.1 to 4.2% 
of projected agricultural emissions. These findings highlight that even under the lowest 
level of adoption, agroforestry expansion could contribute meaningfully to agricultural 
decarbonisation, although the level of mitigation achieved remained highly dependent 
on deployment scale (Department for Energy Security and Net Zero, 2022). 
The projected C trajectories should be interpreted in the context of tree growth 
dynamics. Biomass accumulation represents the largest proportion of total C 
sequestration (83%), and therefore overall C removal rates were strongly linked to 
patterns of tree growth. However, biomass accumulation is unlikely to increase 
indefinitely, because tree growth rates generally decline with increasing stand age, 
resulting in progressively slower rates of C accumulation over time (Truax et al., 2014). 
Consequently, the values presented here should be interpreted as plausible C 
sequestration pathways under current conditions, rather than predictions that 
sequestration rates will continue indefinitely into the future. Even so, the results 
demonstrate that mature silvoarable agroforestry can provide substantial long-term C 
storage and make a meaningful contribution to climate change mitigation. 
The contrast between scenarios highlights an important trade-off between theoretical 
mitigation potential and practical implementation. Although Scenario 1 generated the 
greatest C sequestration, achieving widespread establishment at this scale may provide 
[PDF PAGE 115]
 
95 
 
challenging due to competing demands for food production, biodiversity conservation, 
and broader landscape multifunctionality. This challenge is reflected in the UK's 
historical difficulties in meeting national tree-planting targets (Westaway et al., 2023). 
Lower adoption rates, such as those represented by Scenario 3, may be easier to 
integrate within existing farming systems and therefore more achievable in practice. 
However, this came at the cost of substantially reduced mitigation potential. Across all 
hybrids, total sequestration under Scenario 3 was approximately 70-76% lower than 
under Scenario 1. For example, annual sequestration by Trichobel decreased from 7.09 
Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹ under Scenario 1 to 1.72 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹ under Scenario 3, a reduction of 
75.7%. Consequently, Scenario 3 offset only 3.1-4.2% of projected UK agricultural 
emissions in 2050, compared with 10.3-17.3% under Scenario 1 (Table 3.3 and 3.4). 
These findings suggest that although agroforestry can provide a significant C sink, it is 
unlikely to deliver net-zero targets in isolation and should be implemented alongside 
other agricultural mitigation measures (Westaway et al., 2023). 
Comparison with Burgess et al. (2022) further emphasises the importance of hybrid 
selection, system design, and implementation scale. Annual soil and tree C 
sequestration rates reported for the 19- and 30-year-old silvoarable systems in Burgess 
et al. (2022) were converted to Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹ and scaled according to the land area 
available under each scenario (Table 3.5). Under Scenario 1, Trichobel exhibited the 
highest annual C sequestration rate (4.84 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹), representing a 38.7% increase 
relative to the 19-year-old Burgess system (3.49 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹) and a 27.4% increase 
relative to the 30-year-old system (3.80 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹). This difference was primarily 
attributable to greater AGB C accumulation, whereas SOC sequestration rates were 
relatively similar among systems. Under Scenario 2, all poplar hybrids outperformed the 
Burgess reference systems, with annual sequestration ranging from 1.74 to 2.35 Tg CO₂ 
yr⁻¹ compared with 0.77 to 1.11 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹ for the Burgess systems. Although similar 
trends were observed under Scenario 3, the large differences among systems were 
influenced not only by biological productivity but also by the assumptions used when 
scaling sequestration rates to national land areas. Consequently, these comparisons 
demonstrate that national mitigation potential is determined not only by per-hectare 
sequestration rates but also by the extent of agroforestry adoption. Together, the 
[PDF PAGE 116]
 
96 
 
findings identify hybrid selection and deployment scale as the principal factors 
controlling the overall C sequestration potential of silvoarable agroforestry.
