Comparison with hedgerow systems further highlights the substantial biomass 
accumulation potential of poplar-based agroforestry. Biffi et al. (2023) reported the 
accumulation of 0.67 Tg C in hedgerow AGB over a 27-year period, whereas estimated 
AGB C accumulation in the poplar-based systems ranged from 5.26 to 21.98 Tg C 
[PDF PAGE 117]
 
97 
 
across the scenarios evaluated. This represents approximately 8 to 33 times higher C 
uptake than hedgerows alone, likely due to the greater tree density (166 trees ha⁻¹) and 
biomass production of poplar systems. Even if tree density were halved, estimated C 
uptake would still be about 4 to 16 times higher than in hedgerows. While hedgerows 
provide numerous environmental benefits, including habitat provision, landscape 
connectivity, and erosion control, these results suggest that silvoarable agroforestry has 
considerably greater potential for biomass C sequestration at the landscape scale. 
From a policy perspective, these findings suggest that predicting the impact of expanding 
agroforestry to net-zero targets should not rely on generic C sequestration estimates or a 
one-size-fits-all approach. The substantial variation in C uptake among pop lar hybrids 
and implementation Scenarios demonstrates the importance of matching suitable tree 
genotypes to specific sites and management objectives. Policies should therefore support 
not only the establishment of agroforestry systems, but also their long-term management 
to maximise C sequestration benefits over time.  In addition, robust monitoring 
frameworks are needed to capture variation in C outcomes among systems and ensure 
that reported climate benefits are evidence -based. This approach aligns closely with 
current UK agroforestry policy, which seeks to increase agricultural C sequestration while 
simultaneously delivering wider ecosystem services, including soil erosion control, water 
regulation, biodiversity enhancement, and the maintenance of food production (Areal et 
al., 2026). Ultimately, the results highlight that the climate mitigation potential of 
agroforestry depends not only on adoption rates, but also on system design, species 
selection and long-term management.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 118]
 
98 
 
3.5. Conclusion  
This study highlights three key findings: 
1. Agroforestry can contribute meaningfully to net-zero targets but the magnitude of 
mitigation depends on how and where it is implemented. Upscaling estimates indicate 
that poplar-based silvoarable systems could remove between 0.87 and 7.16 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹, 
equivalent to offsetting between 2.13% and 17.5% of projected UK agricultural 
CO2emissions by 2050. However, the potential climate mitigation benefit varied 
substantially among adoption scenarios and hybrids, demonstrating that sequestration 
outcomes are highly dependent on system design, species selection, and the extent of 
agroforestry adoption. Consequently, agroforestry policies should move beyond generic 
sequestration estimates and instead promote evidence-base, site-specific approaches that 
prioritise high-performing hybrids, locally adapted tree varieties and long-term system 
management. Such targeted deployment would maximise climate mitigation benefits 
while simultaneously delivering wider environmental co-benefits, including improved 
soil health, biodiversity enhancement, and greater resilience of agricultural systems. 
2. Silvoarable agroforestry enhances long-term soil carbon storage.  Mature poplar-
based silvoarable agroforestry systems accumulated significantly greater SOC stocks 
than an adjacent conventional arable field, indicating their capacity to increase long-
term soil C storage. Although SOC accumulation was concentrated within the surface 
soil layer (0-15 cm), the average net sequestration rate of 0.55 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹, with rates 
of 0.43 and 0.56 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹ in tree rows and alleys, respectively, indicates a 
sustained capacity for C storage over time. Furthermore, separating sequestration 
estimates between tree rows and alleys revealed substantial within-system 
heterogeneity, highlighting the importance of spatially explicit assessments when 
evaluating agroforestry soil C dynamics. Carbon sequestration within the system was 
not limited to soils; AGB accounted for the largest proportion of total C uptake (66%), 
while BGB (17%) and SOC (17%) provided additional long-term storage. Together, 
these findings demonstrate that poplar silvoarable systems can simultaneously increase 
C storage, improve soil quality and maintain productive agricultural land use. 
[PDF PAGE 119]
 
99 
 
3. Hybrid selection is a critical determinant of carbon sequestration potential. Carbon 
sequestration differed substantially among poplar hybrids, with Trichobel consistently 
achieved the highest C uptake. Across all expansion scenarios, Trichobel sequestering 
approximately 52% more C than the least productive hybrid, Gibecq, highlights the 
importance of selecting productive and site-adapted tree varieties when designing 
silvoarable systems. Although Scenario 1 delivered the greatest cumulative C 
sequestration over 25 years, its assumption of immediate large-scale agroforestry 
adoption makes it unlikely to be achieved in practice. In contrast, Scenarios 2 and 3 
represent more realistic pathways for UK agroforestry expansion and demonstrate that 
both the rate of expansion and the choice of hybrid strongly influence long-term climate 
mitigation outcomes. These findings emphasise that successful agroforestry policy 
should focus not only on increasing adoption, but also on promoting the most effective 
tree varieties capable of maximising C sequestration for local environmental conditions. 
Overall, this study demonstrates that mature poplar-based silvoarable agroforestry 
systems can provide substantial long-term C sequestration through the combined 
accumulation of C in soil, AGB, and BGB. However, the magnitude of climate change 
mitigation achieved depends on interactions among hybrid performance, system design, 
and the scale of agroforestry adoption. The results therefore highlight the need for 
strategically targeted agroforestry expansion supported by robust C accounting, long-
term monitoring, and appropriate hybrid selection to maximise the contribution of 
agroforestry to UK net-zero ambitions. 
[PDF PAGE 120]
 
100 
 
Chapter 4.  Spatial variability in soil nutrients, fine root traits and 
belowground carbon dynamics in a mature temperate poplar 
silvoarable system 
Abstract  
This study investigated spatial variability in soil nutrients, root traits, and soil physical 
properties within a mature poplar-based silvoarable system in northern England. Soil 
and root measurements were compared across three management zones: crop alleys 
(Alley), tree rows (Row), and an adjacent conventional arable field (Arable). Available 
nitrogen (AN) and Olsen phosphorus (P) declined significantly with soil depth and 
exhibited distinct spatial patterns, with AN generally higher in agroforestry zones and P 
highest in Arable. Both nutrients were positively associated with live and dead root 
length density, highlighting the linkage between nutrient availability and root 
distribution. In contrast, bulk density was negatively correlated with key root traits, 
including live and dead root length, biomass density and root C stocks, and was 
generally lower in Row and Alley, suggesting improved soil structure in biologically 
active zones. Root traits varied significantly with depth and location, with the highest 
(80-90%) live root length density, biomass density, and root C stocks occurring within 
agroforestry zones. Soil organic carbon (SOC) concentrations increased significantly 
with depth, being 29.9% greater at 45 cm than at 15 cm in Alley and 14.4% greater in 
Row. Although SOC stocks did not differ significantly among locations, they were 
strongly associated with root biomass and root C stocks. Dead roots dominated topsoil 
C inputs in Row, accounting for 89.2% of root biomass density and 97.1% of root C 
stocks, Similar patterns were observed for live root biomass density and root C stocks, 
with 86-99% occurring in the topsoil whereas live roots extended into the subsoil in 
Alley and Row (9.6% and 11.1% of root length density, respectively) but were absent in 
the subsoil of Arable. These patterns suggest complementary C pathways, with live 
roots supporting nutrient cycling and rhizosphere processes and dead roots contributing 
to necromass accumulation and longer-term soil C storage in agroforestry. Overall, 
mature silvoarable agroforestry enhanced belowground spatial heterogeneity, promoted 
deeper rooting, and increased the potential for soil C sequestration. 
[PDF PAGE 121]
 
101 
 
 
4.1.  Introduction  
In terrestrial ecosystems, including woodlands, fine and coarse roots, together with their 
interactions with soil microorganisms, play a fundamental role in biomass production 
and the regulation of nutrient and carbon (C) cycling (Clark et al., 2001; Raich et al., 
2014; Smyth et al., 2013). It is estimated that approximately 22-63% of organic C fixed 
through total gross primary productivity in forests is allocated to below ground (Litton 
et al., 2007; Prescott, 2010). Recognising these functions, both the IPCC (Ngo et al., 
2019) and UNFCCC (Rosenstock et al., 2019) have identified agroforestry as a 
multifunctional land-use approach that enhances agroecological processes, contributes 
to climate change mitigation through C sequestration, and supports a wide range of 
interconnected ecosystem services. Consequently, agroforestry systems have been 
identified as a key component of Europe's C farming scheme (COWI, Ecologic Institute 
& IEEP, 2021). However, despite increasing recognition of their potential, considerable 
uncertainty remains regarding the soil C sequestration potential of complex, 
biodiversity-rich systems such as agroforestry, particularly in comparison with 
conventional agricultural and forestry systems. 
Silvoarable agroforestry systems consist of three principal vegetation zones: (1) trees 
planted in rows, (2) an understory strip, typically a few metres wide, located within the 
tree rows, and (3) arable crops cultivated in the inter-row alleys, which are typically 10s 
of metres wide. While these systems can enhance resource use efficiency and ecosystem 
functioning, competition for light, water, and nutrients can reduce crop yields due to 
interspecific competition, particularly where tree root systems extend laterally into 
adjacent crop alleys (Livesley et al., 2004; Zhang et al., 2019; Guillot et al., 2021). The 
root systems of these vegetation types differ in their chemical characteristics, including 
C, N, and C/N ratio, as well as in key functional traits such as root length density, root 
biomass density, root diameter, and root turnover (Prieto et al., 2015). These differences 
influence interactions with soil physical and chemical properties (Freschet et al., 2021), 
thereby regulating below-ground processes such as nutrient acquisition, organic matter 
decomposition and nutrient mineralisation - immobilisation dynamics (Smith et al., 
2013).  
[PDF PAGE 122]
 
102 
 
Silvoarable systems are characterised by spatially heterogeneous root distributions, 
which can enhance soil C storage across the system, although these effects often vary 
among vegetation zones and soil depth (Bonanomi et al., 2021a; Villarino et al., 2021). 
In these systems, both tree and crop roots may adapt their functional traits such as root 
diameter and root length density to optimise nutrient uptake and soil exploration under 
varying levels of competition and resource availability (Freschet et al., 2021; Houde et 
al., 2020). In addition to this spatial heterogeneity, root-derived C inputs display 
pronounced temporal dynamics. Annual crops contribute substantial belowground C 
inputs during crop maturation and harvest, whereas perennial trees provide more 
continuous inputs through root growth, exudation, and turnover processes (Abbas et al., 
2020). Consequently, the diversity and temporal variability of root systems in 
silvoarable agroforestry play a critical role in regulating soil C inputs and influencing 
long-term soil C sequestration. 
Root development is strongly influenced by the availability of both C and nitrogen (N). 
C provides the structural resources required for root construction and expansion, 
particularly under nutrient-limited conditions, whereas N availability regulates root 
growth and nutrient acquisition strategies. In silvoarable systems, nutrient availability is 
spatially heterogeneous, with fertilised crop alleys typically containing greater nutrient 
inputs than tree rows, creating varying conditions for root growth (Sharp et al., 1990). 
Consequently, both trees and crops may adjust their root morphology and foraging 
strategies in response to these spatial gradients. 
Interactions among living roots, dead roots, and soil microbial communities underpin 
SOC storage through mechanisms such as rhizodeposition, root turnover, and 
rhizosphere-mediated microbial activity (Dijkstra et al., 2021; Villarino et al., 2021). 
Root-derived C inputs (exudates and decomposing root material) play an important role 
in SOC stabilisation because they can be incorporated into stable soil C pools more than 
aboveground litter plant inputs. Living root inputs have been reported to be 2–13 times 
more effective than litter inputs in forming SOC (Sokol et al., 2019), while root-derived 
C typically has a longer residence time in soil than shoot-derived C (Rasse et al., 2005).     
Mature agroforestry systems offer a valuable opportunity to investigate the long-term 
dynamics of soil resource acquisition and its interaction with root traits, particularly in 
[PDF PAGE 123]
 
103 
 
systems containing fast growing tree species. Despite growing recognition of the 
importance of root-driven processes for ecosystem functioning and soil C sequestration, 
very few studies have examined the relationships between root functional traits and soil 
functionality including C storage and nutrient cycling in mature agroforestry alley-
cropping systems (Prescott, 2010; Raich et al., 2014). Among these systems, mature 
poplar-based agroforestry systems are of particular interest because of their high 
productivity, extensive rooting networks, and potential resilience to environmental 
variability (Enescu et al., 2026). Although numerous studies have demonstrated the 
capacity of agroforestry systems to enhance soil C sequestration (Kaur et al., 2000; 
Sharrow & Ismail, 2004; Cardinael et al., 2018a; Cardinael et al., 2018b), no studies, to 
date, have comprehensively examined horizontal soil gradients in conjunction with root 
functional traits in mature poplar agroforestry systems in temperate regions. 
Furthermore, it remains unclear the extent to which root-trait nutrient acquisition 
strategies influence C sequestration (Prieto et al., 2015; Pausch & Kuzyakov, 2018). 
Hence, this study aims to fill this knowledge gap by investigating root–soil interactions 
and their implications for nutrient cycling and C sequestration in a mature silvoarable 
system. 
This study aims to investigate the spatial variation in plant-available soil nutrients, 
including available nitrogen (AN), phosphorus (P), total nitrogen (N%), SOC%, SOC 
stock, and bulk density, as well as root traits such as live and dead root length density, 
root biomass density, root C stock, and root C and N content. These parameters will be 
assessed across three vegetation zones: Alley, Row, and Arable and at different soil 
depths within the soil profile. We hypothesise that (i) soil nutrient availability would 
differ among locations and soil depths, (ii) root traits would vary in response to these 
nutrient gradients, and (iii) interactions between soil nutrient availability and root 
dynamics would influence belowground C inputs, turnover, and storage, resulting in 
spatially distinct soil environments within the agroforestry system. 
 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 124]
 
104 
 
4.2.  Methods  
4.2.1.  Study site  
The research was conducted at the University of Leeds farm located near Tadcaster (53° 
51′ 44.45″ N, −1° 19′ 58.14″ W), northern England, a mixed arable and pasture system 
(Guest et al., 2022; Judson et al., 2024). The farm comprises both conventional arable 
fields and some mature agroforestry plots established in the 1990s. The region has a 
temperate oceanic climate under the Köppen system, characteristic of much of Western 
Europe (Beck et al., 2018). It has a mean annual temperature of 10.9°C and a mean 
annual rainfall of 767 mm (1993-2023 data for Bramham station, UK Met Office, 
2023). Soils at the farm are classified as calcaric endoleptic cambisol from the Aberford 
series, well-drained calcareous brown earth developed on Permian and Jurassic 
limestone in central and northern England (British Geological Survey, 2018). 
The poplar silvoarable experimental system was established in 1992 and had four poplar 
hybrids, Trichobel, Gibecq, Beaupre, and Robusta, which represented the major groups 
of poplars being grown in Britain at the time (Burgess et al., 2005). Three arable 
cropping systems were implemented: continuously fallow (CF), continuously cropped 
(CC), and alternatively cropped (AC). Each combination of poplar hybrid and cropping 
system was arranged in three replicated blocks in a randomised block design to account 
for spatial variability in the field and edge effects throughout the experiment.  
The site consisted of 19 rows of trees oriented approximately NNE-SSW. Trees were 
planted at 6.4 m intervals within rows, with 10 m spacing between rows. Within each 
row, hybrids were planted in groups of five consecutive trees, with a guard tree 
positioned at each end of the row. The planting design resulted in an overall tree 
planting density of 166 trees per ha. Further information on the establishment and 
management of the trees at the site is provided by Burgess et al. (2005). 
Between 1992 and 2003, the 10-metre-wide alleys were ploughed annually in autumn in 
8-metre strips. This resulted in tree root damage to a depth of approximately 20-30 cm 
in the cultivated alleys, while leaving a 2 m-wide uncultivated strip along the tree rows 
(Burgess et al., 2005). In the CF treatment, two adjacent alleys were regularly 
[PDF PAGE 125]
 
105 
 
cultivated. In the CC treatment, a rotation of cereals and non-cereal crops was cultivated 
on both sides of the measurement tree row. The AC treatment involved continuous 
cropping on one side of the measurement tree row and the fallow treatment on the other 
side (Burgess et al., 2003).  
All crops were managed following standard commercial practices, including the 
application of fertilisers and pesticides as appropriate. However, crop cultivation in the 
alleys ceased after the 2004 harvest due to declining yields, largely resulting from 
competition for light, nutrients, and water. Since then, the alleys have been unmanaged, 
allowing natural vegetation to establish and develop.  
 
Figure 4.1 Map of the experimental site, highlighting the poplar silvoarable system, and 
adjacent arable field and the red dots indicate the sampling locations where soil pits 
were excavated. (Base image source: Esri (2025). World Imagery [Basemap]. © Esri, 
Maxar, Earthstar Geographics, and the GIS User Community). 
One soil pit per location measuring approximately 2.5 m × 1 m, was excavated to a 
depth of 50 cm in each of Row, Alley, and Arable using a small mechanical excavator. 
The pits in Alley and Row were positioned between evenly spaced rows, 10-12 of the 
19 total rows (Fig. 4.1). In the Row location, the pit was located in the centre of the 
Row, at the mid-point between two adjacent rows. From this location, the pit in Alley 
was established 2 m away, also positioned in the centre of the alley. The Arable pit was 

[PDF PAGE 126]
 
106 
 
located in a field adjacent to the silvoarable system (Fig. 4.1). The vertical faces of each 
pit were cleaned using a shovel and measured with a tape to facilitate soil sampling. 
This preparation also allowed precise observation of soil colour and identification of 
soil horizons (British Society of Soil Science, 2025).  
As shown in Fig. 4.2a and b, soil layers varied irregularly between Row and Alley. In 
Alley, limestone-rich soil was more prominent than in Row. In both locations, shallower 
soil horizons were associated with a more complex root network. Observations from the 
soil pits, measured using a tape, indicated that root diameters generally did not exceed 
3 cm in Alley and 5 cm in Row. In contrast, Arable (Fig. 4.2c) exhibited shallower soil 
compared with the silvoarable locations. Limestone was encountered at a depth of 
~10 cm, and the soil appeared more compact than in the silvoarable systems, as 
indicated by bulk density.  
 
Figure 4.2 Soil profile for a. Row, b. Alley, c. Arable 
4.2.2.  Sampling strategy  
Soil sampling was conducted in January 2024 from each pit. Samples were collected 
using a grid system within each pit, aligned with predefined soil sampling locations on 
the pit face. Soil cores were extracted horizontally from depths of 5-45 cm at 10 cm 
intervals using a cylindrical core sampler (5 cm diameter, 5.1 cm length; volume = 100 
cm3). This resulted in five nominal sampling depths per pit face (2.5–7.5, 12.5–17.5, 

[PDF PAGE 127]
 
107 
 
22.5–27.5, 32.5–37.5, and 42.5–47.5 cm) hereafter referred to as 5, 15, 25, 35, and 
45 cm depths based on their central positions. To minimise soil compaction, which can 
significantly affect bulk density measurements, cores were taken diagonally across face 
of the soil pit to avoid repeated disturbance at the same location. Additional 100 cm3 
soil cores were collected from the same depth interval for root analysis. For the analysis 
of soil nutrients, including AN, P, SOC, and N%, loose soil was taken from the same 
locations and depth intervals.  
A total of 60 soil samples for each determinant (5 depths × 4 pit faces × 3 locations) 
was initially targeted. However, sampling depth was constrained by the presence of 
limestone bedrock, which occurred between approximately 40 and 70 cm in the Alley 
and Row locations, and at shallower depths (less than 30 cm) in the Arable location. As 
a result, several samples could not be sampled: one from Alley (face 4, 35 and 45 cm 
depth), eleven from Arable (all faces, 25, 35 and 45 cm depths), and one from Row 
(face 4, 35 and 45 cm depth). Hence, a total of 47 soil samples were collected. 
Consequently, subsequent analysis considered a depth range of 5-45 cm for Alley and 
Row location and 5-25 cm for the Arable location. All soil samples were stored at 4 °C 
and analysed in the laboratory within one week of collection.  
4.2.3.  Soil analysis  
Bulk density was calculated as the oven-dried mass of soil divided by the volume of the 
soil core, following the method described by Poeplau et al. (2017). Available nitrogen 
was measured by extracting fresh, moist soil with 1 M KCl at a 1:5 soil-to-solution 
ratio. The resulting extracts were analysed for nitrate (NO₃⁻–N) and ammonium (NH₄⁺–
N) using a SKALAR SAN++ auto-analyser, following standard procedures outlined by 
(Carter and Gregorich,2008; Motsara and Roy, 2008). For the determination of 
available P (Olsen P), SOC and N%. Soil samples were oven-dried at 40 °C for 24 
hours and subsequently passed through a 2 mm sieve. Available phosphorus was 
extracted using Olsen’s extraction method (Olsen, 1954) and the phosphate (PO4-P) 
content determined using a Skalar San ++ (Skalar Analytical B.V., Netherlands) 
continuous flow auto-analyser. Total SOC and N contents were quantified using a dry 
combustion elemental analyser (EuroEA 3000). To account for the presence of 
inorganic C associated with calcareous soils, SOC samples were pre-treated by sieving 
[PDF PAGE 128]
 
108 
 
to <100 μm, acidified with 30 μL of 15% HCl to remove inorganic C, and dried at 80 °C 
for 2 hours prior to analysis. Additional HCl was added where necessary due to a high 
carbonate content from underlying limestone.   
SOC stock (Mg ha-1) was calculated using the following equation: 
SOC stock = SOC (%) * BD (bulk density) * depth    (Equation 4.1) 
where BD is expressed in g cm⁻³, and depth in cm (10 cm intervals). 
4.2.4.  Root washing  
Within three days of sample collection, roots were separated from soil using a washing 
and hand-sieving procedure (Suppl. Fig. B.1). Approximately 100–200 g of field-moist 
soil was placed in a tray and submerged in water. The soil–water mixture was gently 
agitated to disperse the soil and release root material. Floating roots and suspended 
organic matter were decanted onto a series of sieves with mesh sizes of 500 µm and 250 
µm, followed by filtration through a 53 µm sieve. The washing and sieving process was 
repeated until no visible roots remained in the soil sample. Each sample was 
subsequently rinsed four times under running tap water to remove adhering mineral 
particles and organic debris. The selected sieve combination was based on preliminary 
trials, which demonstrated its effectiveness in removing soil particles while retaining 
intact roots. After washing, roots retained on the sieves, along with those floating in the 
water, were manually collected with tweezers (Frasier et al., 2016; Ristova and Barbez, 
2018). Roots were subsequently separated into live and dead fractions based on 
morphological characteristics, such as colour and elasticity, following established 
criteria (Berhongaray et al., 2013; Brassard et al., 2013; Fiala et al., 2017; Fortier et al., 
2019; Yang et al., 2010). All separated root samples were placed in tins, sealed in 
plastic bags and stored at 4 °C prior to root image scanning and subsequent C and N 
analysis. 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 129]
 
109 
 
4.2.5.  Root image acquisition and interpretation  
For root trait analysis, all root samples were scanned within 48 hours of washing using 
an EPSON scanner at a resolution of 400 dpi. Prior to scanning, samples were carefully 
spread out in a transparent tray filled with clear water and placed on the scanner glass to 
ensure proper separation and high-quality image acquisition (Slota et al., 2016). For 
large root samples, multiple images were taken, with roots evenly spread across the 
scanning area to avoid overlapping and maintain an appropriate root density (Ristova 
and Barbez, 2018).  
Root diameters were measured using WinRHIZO 2020 software (Regent Instruments 
Inc., Canada) with the Regent method (WinRHIZO, 2020). However, due to the 
presence of heterogeneous root material, including broken and fine absorptive roots, 
WinRHIZO was unable to measure root length accurately. Therefore, ImageJ software 
was used to quantify root length (Pierret et al., 2013). Root length density was 
calculated as root length (cm) per unit soil volume (cm³). 
4.2.6.  Root biomass and nutrients  
Immediately after scanning, live and dead roots were air-dried for 2 days, then oven-
dried at 80 °C for 24 hours. The dry weight of each sample was recorded to calculate 
root biomass. The oven-dried roots were then ground using hand homogenisers to a 
particle size of approximately 100 µm for C and N analysis via combustion using 
elemental analyser (EuroEA 3000).  
For longer roots, samples were weighed directly into 5 × 5.5 mm tin capsules, using 
tweezers and scissors for handling and cutting. Ground root samples were similarly 
weighed into tin capsules before analysis. Root biomass density was calculated as oven-
dry mass (mg) per unit soil volume (cm³). Root C stock (g ha-1) was determined by 
multiplying the root C concentration (g kg-1) by the oven-dry mass (g) and scaling by 
the area represented by the soil core (ha) (Drexler and Don, 2024). 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 130]
 
110 
 
4.2.7. Statistical analysis  
The Shapiro-Wilk test was performed to assess the normality of the soil and root 
datasets. The SOC stock and bulk density were found to be normally distributed. For 
variables that did not meet the assumption of normality, data transformations were 
applied to both soil (AN, P, SOC%, N%, SOC/N ratio) and root datasets. Log 
transformation successfully normalised several variables, including soil AN and SOC%, 
as well as root traits such as live root length density, live root biomass density, live root 
C stock, dead root length density, dead root nitrogen content, and dead root C/N ratio. 
However, other soil variables, such as P, N%, and C/N ratio, along with additional root 
traits, did not achieve normality despite applying different transformations.  
As distributions of soil nutrient and root morphological trait data were approximately 
symmetrical around the mean, a two-way ANOVA was conducted to assess the effects 
of soil depth and location, followed by post hoc Tukey’s tests or pairwise comparisons. 
Relationships between soil nutrient variables and root traits were assessed using 
Spearman’s rank correlation analysis. Statistical significance was determined based on 
p-values obtained via the rcorr() function from the Hmisc package in R. Principal 
Component Analysis (PCA) was subsequently applied to identify patterns of variation 
and associations between soil nutrients and root morphological traits across soil depths. 
Prior to PCA, soil data were standardised using the scale() function in RStudio. RStudio 
version 4.3.3 was used to undertake all statistical analyses. All statistical analyses were 
conducted using RStudio (version 4.3.3). Hereafter, topsoil is considered 5-25 cm and 
subsoil 35-45 cm.  
4.3.  Results 
4.3.1.  Soil nutrients  
Available nitrogen was highest in the topsoil layers (5-25 cm, 90.25%) and declined 
with increasing depth across all locations (Fig. 4.3a). Available nitrogen was similar at 
depths of 5 cm and 15 cm, showing a slight increase of 4.4%, but declined sharply by 
80.9% between 15 cm and 25 cm. These results indicate that most of the AN is retained 
[PDF PAGE 131]
 
111 
 
within the upper 15 cm of the soil profile, with substantially lower concentrations at 
greater depth. A two-way ANOVA revealed a significant effect of soil depth on AN 
concentration (F = 19.76, p < 0.001), indicating substantial variation across soil layers 
(Suppl. Table B.1). In contrast, location had no significant effect (F = 0.32, p = 0.728), 
nor was there a significant depth × location interaction (F = 0.81, p = 0.568). Similarly, 
P concentrations were highest in the topsoil (90.02%) and decreased with depth. Two-
way ANOVA indicated significant effects of both depth (F = 28.28, p < 0.001) and 
location (F = 10.01, p < 0.001), while the interaction effect was not significant (F = 
1.39, p =0.240) (Suppl. Table B.1). Mean P concentrations decreased by 55.9% from 5 
to 15 cm and by 22.0% from 15 to 25 cm depth, with topsoil concentrations 
significantly greater than those at deeper depths (Tukey's test, p < 0.001; Suppl. Table 
B.1, Fig. 4.3b). At 5 cm depth, median P concentration followed the trend 
Arable>Row>Alleys (median = 41 mg kg-1, 27 mg kg-1 and 24 mg kg-1, respectively) 
(Suppl. Table B.1, Fig. 4.3b), while no significant differences were observed among 
locations in the deeper soil layers (Suppl. Table B.1). 
Bulk density was significantly affected by location (F = 5.33, p = 0.010), but not by 
depth (F = 1.80, p = 0.151) or the interaction between factors (F = 0.48, p = 0.818) 
(Suppl. Table B.1, Fig. 4.3d). Tukey’s test showed that bulk density was significantly 
higher in Arable compared with both Alley and Row.   
Soil organic carbon concentration varied significantly with soil depth (F = 2.70, 
p = 0.047), whereas neither location (F = 0.51, p = 0.607) nor the depth × location 
interaction (F = 0.55, p = 0.768) had a significant effect. Although the difference was 
only marginally significant (p = 0.090), post hoc Tukey comparisons showed higher 
SOC concentrations at 45 cm than at 15 cm (Fig. 4.3e), with increases of 29.9% in the 
Alley and 14.4% in the Row. In contrast, N% and the C/N ratio showed no significant 
effects of depth, locations, or their interaction (Fig. 4.3f, g). 
Soil organic carbon stock was not significantly affected by location (F = 1.36, p = 
0.271) or the depth*location interaction (F = 0.67, p = 0.678) but showed a marginal 
effect of depth (F = 1.03, p = 0.070) (Suppl. Table B.1; Fig. 4.3c). Although the post 
hoc Tukey test revealed only a marginally significant depth effect (15 vs. 45 cm), SOC 
[PDF PAGE 132]
 
112 
 
stocks at 45 cm were 67.8% and 48.4% greater than those at 15 cm in the Alley and 
Row locations, respectively.  
 
Figure 4.3 Variation in soil properties with depth and location: (a) Available nitrogen 
(AN), (b) Olsen phosphorus (P), (c) SOC stock, (d) bulk density, (e) SOC%, (f) N%, (g) 
C/N ratio, at five soil depth intervals under three locations. Bar show the median (n=4), 
error bars indicate the interquartile range (25th and 75th percentiles) 
4.3.2.  Root traits 
Both soil depth and location had significant effects on live root length density, live root 
biomass density, and live root C stock (Fig. 4.4 b, c, and f), whereas their interaction 
was not significant. This indicates that location effects were consistent across depths. 
Tukey’s HSD test revealed significant pairwise differences among depth intervals (5, 
15, 25, and 35 cm; p < 0.001) and between locations, specifically Arable–Alley and 
Row–Arable (p < 0.001) for both live root length density and live root biomass density 
(Suppl. Table B.2). Across locations, mean live root length density and mean live root 

[PDF PAGE 133]
 
113 
 
biomass density were highest in the Alley, followed by the Row and lowest in the 
Arable, especially at 5 cm depth.  
For live root C stock, significant differences were primarily observed between the 5 cm 
layer and the deeper soil layers (35 cm and 45 cm), with location contrasts similar to 
those observed for live root length density and live root biomass density (Suppl. Table 
B.2). As shown in Fig. 4.4f, the highest live root C stock occurred in Alley (2.62 Mg ha-
1), followed by Row (1.29 Mg ha-1), while Arable had the lowest value (0.12 Mg ha-1) at 
5 cm depth. Relative to the 5 cm depth, only 9.92% and 5.43% of the live root C stock 
remained at 45 cm in Alley and Row, corresponding to declines of 90.08% and 94.57%, 
respectively.  
For live root N, soil depth (F = 3.28, p = 0.024), locations (F = 7.56, p = 0.002), and 
their interaction (F = 3.78, p = 0.006) all had statistically significant effects (Fig. 4.4d). 
Tukey’s post-hoc test revealed significant location differences at specific depths, 
particularly between Arable and Alley (p < 0.01) and between Row and Arable (p < 
0.001) within the 5 and 15 cm soil layer. Additional significance contrasts were 
observed among depth-location combinations (e.g., 15 cm: Arable vs. 5cm: Alley, 
45cm: Rows vs. 15cm: Arable; p < 0.001) (Fig. 4.4d, Suppl. Table B.2).  Live root N 
concentrations were highest in Arable within the upper soil layers (1.84% at 5 cm and 
3.33% at 15 cm) compared with Alley and Rows. However, vertical patterns varied 
among locations, with live root N showing an irregular distribution across the soil 
profile and a slight increase with depth in the Row location.  
In contrast, live root C concentration was not significantly influenced by soil depth (F = 
2.43, p = 0.070) or location (F = 1.89, p = 0.168) (Fig. 4.4e). However, live root C/N 
ratio was significantly affected by location (F = 9.60, p < 0.001), whereas neither depth 
(F = 1.94, p = 0.130) nor the interaction between factors (F = 1.52, p = 0.206) had a 
significant effect (Fig. 4.4g). Tukey’s post hoc test confirmed significant differences 
between locations, particularly between Arable and Alley, and between Row and Arable 
(p < 0.001). The live C/N ratio exhibited location-specific patterns, with the highest 
values observed in the Row at 15 cm depth and in the Alley at 5 cm depth, followed by 
a general decline with increasing soil depth. 
[PDF PAGE 134]
 
114 
 
 
Figure 4.4 Variation in live root traits with soil depth and location: (a) live root length 
diameter (cm), (b) live root length density (LRLD), (c) live root biomass density 
(LRBD), (d) live root N (LRN), (e) live root C (LRC), (f) live root C stock (LRCS), (g) 
live root C/N ratio (LRCN) at five soil depth intervals and at three locations. Bars 
represent median values and error bars indicate the interquartile range (25th to 75th 
percentiles) 
Finally, ANOVA revealed no significant effects of soil depth, location, or their 
interaction on either live or dead root diameter (Fig. 4.4 and 4.5a). While Tukey’s post 
hoc test indicated significant pairwise differences between specific location 
combinations, most notably between Arable and Alley and between Row and Arable (p 
< 0.001), these differences should be interpreted with caution given the absence of 
significant main effects or depth × location interactions in the overall ANOVA.  
Soil depth (F = 6.48, p < 0.001) and location (F = 32.90, p < 0.001) had significant 
effects on dead root length density, whereas the interaction between these factors was 
not significant (F = 0.66, p= 0.683) (Fig. 4.5b; Suppl. Table B.3). The highest dead root 
length density was observed in Row (0.36 cm cm-3), followed by Alley (0.14 cm/³), 
whilst the lowest occurred in Arable within the 5 and 15 cm soil layer (Fig. 4.5b; Suppl. 

[PDF PAGE 135]
 
115 
 
Table B.3). Tukey’s post hoc test showed significant differences between deeper soil 
layers (25–35 cm and 35- 45 cm), and among locations, particularly between Alley and 
Arable and between Row and Arable (p < 0.001, Fig. 4.5b, Suppl. Table B.3). The 
interaction between depth and locations was not significant (F = 0.66, p = 0.683).   
 
Figure 4.5 Variation in dead root traits with soil depth and location: (a) dead root 
diameter (DRD), (b) dead root legnth density (DRLD), (c) dead root biomass density, 
(d) dead root N (DRN), (e) dead root C (DRC), (f) dead root C stock (DRCS), (e) dead 
root C/N ratio (DRCN) measured at five soil depth intervals and at three locations. Bar
