Similarly, dead root biomass density was significantly influenced by both soil depth (F= 
6.11, p = 0.001) and locations (F = 15.94, p < 0.001), with a significant depth x location 
interaction (F = 3.89, p = 0.005) (Fig. 4.5c, Suppl. Table B.3), indicating that location 
effects varied across the soil profile (Fig. 4.5c; Suppl. Table B.3). Relative to the 
surface layer (5 cm), only 17.03% and 5.68% of dead root biomass remained at 45 cm 
in Row and Alley, representing declines of 82.97% and 94.32%, respectively. This 
indicates a steeper reduction of dead root biomass with depth in Alley than in Row (p < 
0.001, Fig. 4.5c). Significant differences were observed among all locations; 
comparisons were significant, with the largest differences between Rows at 5 cm and 

[PDF PAGE 136]
 
116 
 
Arable at 25 cm depth.  Several depth-location contrasts were also significant (e.g., Row 
at 5 cm depth and Alley at 15 cm depth, and Row at 15 cm depth and Alley at 5 cm 
depth (Suppl. Table B.3). 
Dead root C concentration was not significantly affected by soil depth (F = 2.02, p = 
0.122), locations (F = 0.81, p = 0.456), or their interaction (F = 1.10, p = 0.388) (Fig. 
4.5e; Suppl. Table B.3). In contrast, dead root C stock was significantly influenced by 
soil depth (F = 5.53, p = 0.002), location (F = 16.67, p < 0.001), and the depth x 
location interaction (F = 4.63, p = 0.003) (Fig. 4.5f). Relative to a depth of 5 cm, dead 
root C stock in Alley declined by 23.1%, 78.2%, and 93.5% at 15, 25, and 45 cm, 
respectively, while in Row it declined by 80.0%, 74.9%, and 90.0%. Tukey's post hoc 
test showed that dead root C stock was significantly higher at 5 cm than at 15, 25, and 
45 cm (p < 0.001). particularly evident in the Alley and Row locations (Suppl. Table 
B.3).  Significant differences were observed among all locations, following the trend: 
Arable < Alley < Row, with the strongest positive effect in the Row (Fig. 4.5f). 
Furthermore, several significant depth × location interactions were observed, 
particularly within the Row soil profile (e.g., 5 cm: Rows vs. 25 cm: Alleys, 35 cm: 
Alley, and 5 cm: Arable; p < 0.001) (Fig. 4.5f), highlighting pronounced location effects 
at specific depths. 
Soil depth also had a significant influence on dead root N concentration (F = 3.75, p = 
0.016), whereas location (F = 1.00, p = 0.383) and the depth × location interaction (F = 
1.54, p = 0.205) were not significant (Fig. 4.5d; Suppl. Table B.3). Tukey’s post hoc test 
revealed significant differences between 45 and 15 cm, and between 25 and 45 cm (p < 
0.001) soil depths. Dead root N generally increased with depth, with the highest values 
observed at 45 cm in Alley and Row, and at 25 cm for Arable. At 45 cm, it was 72.7% 
higher in Alley and 40.1% higher in Row than at 15 cm depth (Fig. 4.5d). 
Similarly, the dead root C: N ratio decreased with soil depth across all locations, 
reflecting a significant main effect of depth (F = 3.32, p = 0.026). In contrast, neither 
location (F = 0.98, p = 0.391) nor the depth x location interaction (F = 0.76, p = 0.608) 
significantly affected dead root C:N ratio (Fig. 4.5g, Suppl. Table B.3). These results 
indicate that variation in dead root stoichiometry was primarily driven by vertical 
changes within the soil profile with differences among locations. 
[PDF PAGE 137]
 
117 
 
4.3.3.   Relationships between soil properties and root traits  
Live and dead root traits exhibited significant relationships with several soil properties 
(Fig. 4.6). Overall, the correlation matrix identified 11 significant associations between 
soil characteristics and root morphological and biochemical traits, highlighting the 
important role of soil nutrient availability and physical conditions in shaping 
belowground plant development (Fig. 4.6c). The scatterplots further illustrate that AN 
and BD were the primary factors influencing root abundance-related traits (Fig. 4.6a, b). 
Among the live root traits, live root length density, live root biomass density, and live 
root C stock showed strong responses to both AN and BD. Live root length density was 
positively correlated with AN (ρ = 0.552, p < 0.001; Fig. 4.6b) and P (ρ = 0.352, p < 
0.001) but negatively correlated with BD (ρ = –0.296, p = 0.048; Fig. 4.6a). Similarly, 
live root biomass density exhibited a positive correlation with AN (ρ = 0.498, p < 0.001; 
Fig. 4.6b) and a negative correlation with BD (ρ = –0.373, p = 0.010; Fig. 4.6a). 
Likewise, live root C stock increased with increasing AN (ρ = 0.466, p < 0.001; Fig. 
4.6b) and decreased with increasing BD (ρ = –0.331, p = 0.030; Fig. 4.6a).  
In contrast, dead root traits displayed weaker and more variable relationships with soil 
properties (Fig. 4.6a–c). Although some negative trends with BD and positive trends 
with AN were evident, these relationships were generally less pronounced than those 
observed for live roots. In addition to the relationships among soil properties and 
nutrients, a strong correlation was observed between dead root N and dead root C/N 
ratio (ρ = –0.92, p <0.001). This suggests that the distribution of dead roots is 
influenced not only by current soil conditions but also by root turnover, mortality, and 
decomposition processes (Fig. 4.6). 
[PDF PAGE 138]
 
118 
 
 
Figure 4.6 Spearman correlation analysis of soil properties and root traits: (a) BD 
versus live and dead root traits. (b) AN versus live and dead root traits, showing the 
most influential and abundant traits based on the correlation matrix. (c) Spearman’s 
rank correlation matrix between soil properties and root traits. Colours indicate 
correlation coefficients from −1 (blue) to +1 (red), and black stars denote significant 
correlations (p < 0.05) 
Principal component analysis (PCA) was used to explore the multivariate relationships 
among soil nutrients and root traits, and to summarise the overall variability in the 
dataset. For soil properties (Fig. 4.7a), the first two principal components explained 
66.5% of the total variance, with PC1 accounting for 38.0% and PC2 for 28.5%. SOC% 
and SOC stock were strongly aligned and contributed primarily to PC1, indicating that 
C-related variables were major drivers of variation in soil characteristics. Variables such 
as C/N ratio, AN, and P contributed to both principal components, reflecting their 
influence across multiple environmental gradients. In contrast, soil depth and BD were 
negatively associated with SOC%, SOC stock, N%, and soil C/N ratio, suggesting an 
inverse relationship between soil compaction and increasing depth on one hand, and C 
and nutrient-rich conditions on the other. 

[PDF PAGE 139]
 
119 
 
 
Figure 4.7 PCA biplot showing the distribution of (a) soil variables: SOC, SOC stock, 
N, AN, P, bulk density, and soil depth along the first two principal components (Dim1: 
38%, Dim2: 23.5%) and (b) live and dead root variables: live root length density, live 
root biomass density, live root C stock, live root N, live root C, live root C/N, live root 
diameter, dead root length density, dead root biomass density, dead root C stock, dead 
root N, dead root C, dead root C/N, dead root diameter with soil depths (Dim1: 26.3%, 
Dim2: 15.4%). Red vectors represent the direction and magnitude of each variable's 
contribution, with blue points indicating individual observations; variables pointing in 
the same direction are positively correlated.  
For root traits (Fig. 4.7b), PC1 accounted for 26.3% of the total variance, and PC2 
explained 15.4%, together accounting for 41.7% of the overall variability. Several live 
root traits, including live root C, live root C/N, live root biomass density, live root 

[PDF PAGE 140]
 
120 
 
length density, and live root C stock, were strongly aligned along the principal axes, 
indicating a strong positive association among these variables. Dead root traits such as 
dead root biomass density, dead root length density, and dead root C concentration also 
clustered with this group, together with live root N concentration, suggesting 
coordinated patterns of variation in root morphology and chemistry. In contrast, live and 
dead root diameter, dead root C/N ratio, and soil depth were negatively associated with 
the main cluster of root traits, indicating contrasting functional and structural patterns 
with the root system (Fig. 4.7b). Overall, the PCA highlights clear gradients in both soil 
and root trait distributions. The scree plots (Suppl. Fig. B.2ab) supported the retention 
and interpretation of the first two principal components, showing that explained 
variance declined substantially after PC2, with an evident elbow around PC2-PC3. 
4.4.  Discussion 
This study demonstrates that silvoarable agroforestry systems generate pronounced 
spatial heterogeneity in soil properties and root functional traits, with important 
implications for nutrient dynamics, belowground resource dynamics and C storage. 
Across the Row, Alley, and Arable locations, clear patterns emerged in nutrient and C 
distribution, root biomass allocation, and root–soil interactions, highlighting the 
capacity of silvoarable systems to modify biogeochemical processes relative to 
conventional arable management.  
4.4.1.  Soil nutrient stratification and management effects 
Soil properties exhibited clear vertical stratification across all locations. Soil organic 
carbon, AN, and P were most abundant in the surface soil (5 cm) and generally declined 
with depth, whereas BD increased with depth, reflecting higher compaction in deeper 
soil layers (Fig. 4.3a-g). Although these depth-related trends were consistent across 
locations, their magnitude varied according to land use and management practices. PC1, 
which explained 33.2% of the total variance, was primarily driven by SOC (-2.74), SOC 
stock (-2.54), N (-2.13), and C/N ratio (-1.94) (Fig. 4.7). This indicates that differences 
in soil C and nutrient dynamics played a key role in shaping the vertical and spatial 
distribution of soil resources (Judson et al., 2025). Available nitrogen declined 
significantly with increasing soil depth across all locations (Fig. 4.3a). While 
[PDF PAGE 141]
 
121 
 
concentrations were generally similar in the Row and Alley and were lower in the 
Arable (Suppl. Table B.1), overall depth effects were substantially larger than location 
effects. The decrease in AN with depth may support results from previous investigations 
suggesting that N availability is preferential at surface soils, where nutrient cycling, 
organic matter input, litter decomposition, microbial activity and microbial composition 
are most significant (Kim and Isaac, 2022; Tonucci et al., 2023; Judson et al., 2025). 
The presence of litter and root biomass, along with organic matter accumulation in the 
upper soil layers of silvoarable systems, can promote biological activity and N cycling, 
thereby ensuring higher N availability near the soil surface (Kim and Issac, 2022). Thus, 
the vertical distribution of AN reflects the extent of organic matter inputs and nutrient 
turnover within the soil profile, with the highest concentrations occurring near the 
surface and declining with increasing depth. 
In contrast to AN, N% did not vary significantly with either depth or location (Fig. 4.3a 
and f), indicating a relatively stable N pool throughout the soil profile and across 
locations. This divergence between AN and N% highlights the distinction between 
labile and more stable N fractions, suggesting that management practices primarily 
influence N availability rather than the total amount of N stored in the soil (Kaye et al., 
2003). These findings indicate that N cycling processes are more dynamic in surface 
soil layers, where biological activity is greatest, while deeper layers maintain a more 
conserved N pool. 
Phosphorus availability exhibited a clear spatial gradient across the study locations, 
with concentrations decreasing in the order Arable > Alley > Row. Significant 
differences were observed between the Arable location and both silvoarble locations 
(Alley and Row) (Figure 4.3b; Suppl. Table B.1). This variation probably reflects the 
legacy effects of historical fertilisation at the Arable location, where P inputs can persist 
in soils for extended periods because of the relatively low mobility of P compared with 
other nutrients. Previous studies have shown that fertiliser-derived P applied can 
accumulate within soil and remain available for many years, contributing to elevated P 
concentrations in arable soils (Johnston et al., 2014; Solangi et al., 2023). However, the 
relatively low P availability observed in the silvoarable system might indicate a reduced 
supply of fertiliser-derived P, especially in tree-based (Row) systems for increased plant 
uptake. But this finding is contrary to some of the results of Judson et al. (2025), who 
[PDF PAGE 142]
 
122 
 
reported higher P concentrations in both Alley and Row positions than in their 
corresponding control location. These contrasting findings between studies suggest that 
P dynamics in agroforestry systems are highly site-specific and influenced by factors 
such as management history, fertiliser legacy, tree species composition, vegetation 
cover, and nutrient redistribution processes. In the present study, the long-term 
reduction in cropping and fertiliser inputs in Alley, together with sustained nutrient 
uptake by trees and associated vegetation, may have ultimately contributed to lower P 
availability compared to Arable.  
Alley recorded the highest SOC% at 5 cm depth, followed by Row and Arable, with 
relatively small variation across soil depths (Fig. 4.3c, Suppl. Table B.1). These patterns 
likely reflect differences in root activity, organic matter inputs, and microbial processes, 
which are influenced by vegetation structure and management history (Coleman et al., 
2000; Berhongaray et al., 2013). While no significant differences in SOC% and SOC 
stock were observed among locations in the present study (Suppl. Table B.1), the spatial 
distribution of SOC stock suggests that the silvoarable system may play an important 
role in soil C cycling, particularly when compared with the adjacent arable system (Fig. 
4.3c). This interpretation is consistent with the findings reported in Chapter 3, where 
SOC concentration and stock differed significantly between the silvoarable and arable 
systems, although no significant differences were detected between the tree row and 
alley positions within the silvoarable system. In established silvoarable systems, similar 
spatial patterns have been reported, with SOC stock often concentrated in tree-
influenced areas due to greater litter inputs, enhanced root turnover, and reduced soil 
disturbance (Cardinael et al., 2015; Lorenz and Lal, 2014; Udawatta and Jose, 2011). 
Together, these findings suggest that trees enhance localised above- and belowground C 
inputs, contributing to spatial variation in soil C retention and nutrient cycling (Jose et 
al., 2004; Nair et al., 2009a). Furthermore, cropping in Alley ceased in 2004, allowing 
natural vegetation to establish. This transition in vegetation composition is likely to 
have altered both the quantity and distribution of below-ground C inputs, while changes 
in rooting distribution may have affected nutrient cycling and soil organic matter 
turnover. Consequently, contemporary differences in SOC and nutrient dynamics within 
the Alley and Row may reflect not only tree effects but also the legacy influences of 
vegetation succession and changes in land management.  
[PDF PAGE 143]
 
123 
 
4.4.2.  Root trait variation and belowground resource acquisition through 
root soil interactions 
Root distribution with soil depth is largely controlled by nutrient availability, water 
accessibility, and physical constraints imposed by the soil environment (Bardgett et al., 
2014). In the present study, root length density and biomass density declined with 
increasing depth across all locations, corresponding with reductions in AN, P, and SOC 
(Fig. 4.3-4.6). This pattern suggests that roots were concentrated in nutrient-rich surface 
horizons where resource acquisition is most efficient. The co-occurrence of higher root 
densities and elevated nutrient concentrations in surface soils indicates that nutrient 
availability may have been an important driver of rooting distribution. Similar vertical 
rooting patterns have been widely reported in agroforestry and agricultural systems, 
where fine roots are concentrated in upper soil layers to maximise nutrient uptake and 
exploit organic matter-rich layers (Pilon et al., 2013; Chen et al., 2013ab; Drexler et al., 
2023).  
By contrast, P appeared to influence the spatial distribution of roots more strongly than 
overall root abundance. Although live root length density was positively correlated with 
available P, the relationship was weaker than that observed for AN (Fig. 4.6c), 
suggesting that P was not the primary driver of root biomass accumulation across 
locations. Instead, plants may have responded to P limitation through changes in root 
architecture and nutrient foraging strategies (Lu et al., 2024). The greater root 
proliferation observed between the Row and Alley indicates that roots explored larger 
soil volumes to compensate for localised nutrient limitations, a response consistent with 
the findings of Forde and Lorenzo (2001), who demonstrated that root architecture is 
highly responsive to nutrient heterogeneity. Furthermore, P concentrations declined 
markedly with soil depth, reinforcing its stratification within surface soil horizons. 
Given the low mobility of P in soils, efficient uptake depends on the development of 
dense surface-root networks capable of exploiting nutrient-rich topsoil layers 
(Schachtman et al., 1998). Together, these findings suggest that nutrient stratification 
contributed to the high root densities observed in surface soils. 
Significant variation in root N concentrations among locations and depths further 
highlights the influence of soil nutrient dynamics on root functioning (Fig. 4.4 and 4.5). 
[PDF PAGE 144]
 
124 
 
Live root N was greatest at 15 cm in Arable, whereas dead root N peaked at 25 cm 
depth (Suppl. Table B.2, B.3). These patterns likely reflect differences in historical 
fertiliser inputs, decomposition processes, and microbial activity, all of which can 
influence root nutrient content and turnover rates (van Noordwijk et al., 1995; Huang et 
al., 2021). Fertiliser-derived N can modify root mortality, microbial community 
structure, nutrient cycling processes, and competition for belowground resources, 
resulting in heterogeneous patterns of root nutrient distribution through the soil profile 
(Nadelhoffer, 2000; Treseder, 2008). 
In contrast, root diameter remained relatively stable or increased slightly with depth 
(Fig. 4.4 and 4.5), suggesting a shift in root function from nutrient acquisition in surface 
soils to resource transport and structural support in deeper horizons (McCormack et al., 
2012). Deeper soil layers are typically characterised by lower nutrient availability, 
greater mechanical resistance, and reduced biological activity, favouring the 
development of thicker, longer-lived roots that facilitate water uptake and resource 
transport (Geng & Jin, 2022; Gao et al., 2023). The persistence of larger-diameter roots 
at depth may therefore represent an adaptive strategy that enables plants to access 
subsoil resources while reducing the costs associated with high rates of root turnover 
(Pregitzer et al., 2002; McCormack et al., 2012; Lynch, 2019). 
Spatial variation in root distribution was evident across the silvoarable system. Live 
roots were strongly concentrated within the topsoil (5-25 cm) across all locations, 
accounting for 90.4%, 88.9%, and 100% of live root length density in the Alley, Row, 
and Arable, respectively (Fig. 4.4, Suppl. Table B.2). Similar patterns were observed for 
live root biomass density and root C stocks, with 86-99% occurring in the topsoil (5-25 
cm, Table B.2). These values exceed the 50-80% range commonly reported for fine 
roots within the upper 30 cm of soil and are greater than the 51% fine-root biomass C 
reported by Drexler et al. (2024) for hedgerows in Germany, indicating a particularly 
strong concentration of roots in surface soil layers. Surface-root distribution likely 
reflects greater nutrient availability and biological activity in the topsoil (Keiblinger et 
al., 2023).  
Dead roots were also predominantly concentrated in the topsoil; however, in the Row, 
dead root biomass density (89.16%) and dead root C stocks (97.07%) exceeded those of 
[PDF PAGE 145]
 
125 
 
live roots (86.34% and 88.29%, respectively). This pattern suggests greater root 
turnover and accumulation of root-derived residues, which may enhance nutrient 
recycling and contribute to the formation and stabilisation of soil organic C through 
microbial processing and mineral associations (Riley et al., 2009; Rasse et al., 2005). 
Despite the predominance of roots in surface soils, the Alley and Row maintained live 
roots in the subsoil, accounting for 9.6% and 11.1% of root length density, respectively, 
whereas no live or dead roots were detected below the topsoil in the Arable. This 
finding suggests that tree integration promoted deeper rooting and greater utilisation of 
subsoil resources, a characteristic commonly associated with silvoarable agroforestry 
(Upson and Burgess, 2013; Thorup-Kristensen et al., 2020). 
No significant relationship was observed between root distribution and soil C stocks. 
This finding supports previous findings that root abundance alone is a poor predictor of 
vertical SOC distribution, which is also regulated by microbial transformations, mineral 
associations and C stabilisation mechanisms (Rasse et al., 2005; Lorenz and Lal, 2014). 
Nevertheless, the presence of both live and dead roots at depth may indicate a greater 
potential for long-term C sequestration potential in the silvoarable system than in the 
conventionally managed Arable location, as deeper C inputs are generally subject to 
slower turnover and may contribute disproportionately to persistent soil C pools. 
Correlation analysis further demonstrated that live root length density and biomass 
density were strongly and positively associated with AN (Fig. 4.6 c), indicating that N 
availability was a key driver of belowground growth. These root traits were also 
negatively associated with BD, suggesting that both nutrient supply and soil physical 
structure influenced rooting patterns. Similar relationships have been widely reported, 
whereby higher AN has been shown to stimulate root proliferation, increase biomass 
allocation, and enhance nutrient acquisition efficiency (Poorter et al., 2012; Giehl and 
von Wirén, 2014). The strong positive correlations observed between live root traits and 
AN (Fig. 4.6 a and c), together with their negative relationships with BD (Fig. 4.6b and 
c), therefore suggest that both nutrient availability and soil physical structure played 
important roles in determining root distribution patterns. Although, low N availability in 
poplar-based silvoarable systems has been shown to induce various adaptive responses, 
including increased fine root growth and enhanced N uptake efficiency (Coleman & 
[PDF PAGE 146]
 
126 
 
Aubrey, 2018), responses are often context dependent and influenced by species 
composition, and resource availability (Poorter et al., 2012). 
Overall, these findings demonstrate that mature silvoarable agroforestry system create a 
more heterogeneous root distribution both vertically and horizontally compared with 
conventional Arable location. Greater root presence in subsoil layers and distinct root 
dynamics within tree-influenced zones indicate that trees substantially modify 
belowground resource acquisition and allocation patterns. These changes highlight the 
important role of agroforestry in influencing soil nutrient cycling and C dynamics in 
temperate agricultural landscapes. 
4.3.3.  Root–soil interactions and implications for C storage  
The relationships between root traits and soil properties indicate that roots both respond 
to and influence soil conditions. Significant negative correlations between BD and live 
and dead root length density, biomass density, and root C stocks (Figs. 4.6 and 4.7) 
suggest that greater root abundance contributes to improved soil structure through 
enhanced porosity, aggregation, and the incorporation of root-derived organic matter. In 
the silvoarable system, these effects are likely reinforced by reduced soil disturbance 
and continuous organic matter inputs. Similar relationships between root abundance, 
improved soil structure, and reduced compaction have been reported in other 
agroforestry systems (Tracy et al., 2013; Correa et al., 2019; He et al., 2022). 
Root traits were strongly interrelated, particularly live and dead root length density, 
biomass density, and associated C stocks, indicating coordinated belowground resource 
allocation and C cycling (Fig. 4.7). Live roots were most abundant in the Alley, 
suggesting greater inputs of C through root growth, turnover, and rhizodeposition, 
whereas the greater abundance of dead roots in the Row indicates a larger contribution 
of root-derived organic matter through decomposition. These contrasting distributions 
suggest that Alley and Row contribute to belowground C cycling through 
complementary pathways. 
Although SOC stocks declined with depth, both live and dead roots were present 
throughout the soil profile, demonstrating continued inputs of root-derived C 
[PDF PAGE 147]
 
127 
 
belowground. Relationships between root traits and SOC stocks further suggest that root 
abundance and turnover contribute to soil C dynamics within the system. However, 
Chapter 3 found no significant effect of location on SOC stocks at 15-30 cm, despite the 
clear spatial differences in root distribution observed between Row and Alley in present 
study. This indicates that while root-derived C inputs are associated with SOC storage, 
spatial variation in root abundance has not yet resulted in distinct SOC stocks among 
locations. The absence of a location effect may reflect the relatively slow accumulation 
and stabilisation of soil C compared with the more dynamic processes of root growth, 
turnover, and decomposition. 
The accumulation of dead roots within the upper 5 cm of the Row may provide an 
important source of root necromass for SOC formation, whereas the greater abundance 
of live roots in the Alley suggests more active rhizosphere processes and continuous C 
inputs through root turnover and exudation (Liang et al., 2017; Sokol et al., 2019). 
These findings indicate that different zones within the agroforestry system contribute to 
soil C cycling through distinct but complementary mechanisms, with dead roots acting 
as a source of organic matter inputs and live roots sustaining ongoing C fluxes 
belowground. 
Live and dead roots perform complementary functions in regulating soil processes. Live 
roots facilitate nutrient and water uptake while stimulating microbial activity through 
root exudation, whereas dead roots contribute to nutrient recycling, organic matter 
formation, and C storage through decomposition and incorporation into soil organic 
matter (Ehrenfeld et al., 1997). Overall, the combined findings from Chapters 3 and 4 
suggest that mature silvoarable agroforestry systems influence belowground C 
dynamics. While significant differences in SOC stocks among locations were not 
detected, relationships between root traits and SOC highlight the important role of both 
live and dead roots in shaping soil structure, nutrient cycling, and C dynamics within 
the system. 
 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 148]
 
128 
 
4.4.  Implications for agroforestry systems 
The findings of this study highlight the important role of mature silvoarable 
agroforestry systems in regulating belowground C dynamics, nutrient cycling, and soil 
physical properties. The greater abundance of both live and dead roots in agroforestry 
zones indicates enhanced C inputs to the soil through root growth, turnover, and 
decomposition. In particular, the accumulation of dead root biomass within Row, 
suggests increased inputs of root-derived organic matter, which may contribute to 
necromass formation and the stabilisation of SOC. At the same time, greater live root 
abundance in the Alley supports active nutrient uptake, rhizosphere processes, and 
continuous C inputs through root exudation. These biological processes are further 
facilitated by lower BD in agroforestry zones, which enhances soil porosity, facilitates 
root growth, and promotes the movement of water, air, and nutrients through the soil. 
The presence of live and dead roots below the topsoil in both the Row and Alley, 
combined with the absence of roots in deeper soil layers under the Arable location, 
suggests that tree integration extended rooting depth and increased utilisation of subsoil 
resources. This deeper rooting may also increase the delivery of C to deeper soil 
horizons, where turnover rates are typically slower and the potential for long-term C 
stabilisation is greater. Consequently, silvoarable systems may enhance not only the 
quantity of belowground C inputs but also their potential persistence within the soil 
profile. 
Collectively, these findings suggest that mature silvoarable agroforestry systems 
develop multifunctional rooting networks that combined nutrient-foraging in surface 
soils with nutrient acquisition and transport functions at depth. The spatial heterogeneity 
generated by tree–crop interactions appear to promote complementary resource use, 
improve nutrient cycling, and enhance soil structural conditions. s These processes may 
contribute to greater ecosystem resilience and increased C sequestration potential 
relative to conventional arable management. This highlights the value of silvoarable 
systems as a promising sustainable land-use strategy for maintaining soil health, 
improving resource-use efficiency, and contributing to climate change mitigation 
through increased belowground C storage.  
[PDF PAGE 149]
 
129 
 
4.5.  Limitations and future research directions 
A key limitation of this study is the limited spatial replication, with only one soil pit 
sampled per location. Furthermore, it was not possible to distinguish between tree and 
grass roots; therefore, the results represent the combined root system within each 
sampling location. Similar difficulties in separating tree and understory roots have been 
reported in temperate silvoarable agroforestry systems (Upson and Burgess, 2013). 
Consequently, caution should be exercised when interpreting species-specific root 
contributions and extrapolating the findings beyond the sampled locations. 
A further limitation was the presence of shallow limestone bedrock, which restricted the 
maximum sampling depth, particularly in the Arable location. This constrained the 
comparability of soil profiles among locations and may have limited the assessment of 
deeper root distributions and subsoil C storage. Given the importance of deep roots in 
belowground C sequestration, a greater sampling depth would provide a more 
comprehensive understanding of C dynamics throughout the soil profile.  
Coarse roots were not investigated in the present study because very few were 
recovered from soil samples collected at the 5 and 45 cm depth in the Alley and Row. 
This constitutes a limitation of the study, as coarse roots can contribute to belowground 
C inputs through root turnover, decomposition, and rhizodeposition, thereby potentially 
influencing the formation and stabilisation of mineral-associated organic carbon 
(MAOC). Given that MAOC represents a relatively stable soil C pool, the contribution 
of coarse roots to MAOC dynamics warrants further investigation in future studies.  
Future research should incorporate greater spatial replication across locations to better 
quantify within- and between-locations variability in soil properties and root functional 
traits. Additional sampling locations and the inclusion of multiple agroforestry systems 
would improve the robustness and general applicability of the findings. Integrating 
measurements of microbial communities, soil enzyme activity, and isotopic tracing 
techniques would also help clarify the mechanisms through which root-derived C is 
transferred, transformed, and stabilised within soil organic C pools. 
[PDF PAGE 150]
 
130 
 
Further research should focus on the role of root exudates from both live and 
decomposing roots, and on their interactions with root architecture, soil properties, and 
microbial processes. Such investigations would improve understanding of rhizosphere 
dynamics and their contribution to nutrient cycling and C sequestration. Evaluating 
these relationships across different tree species, crop combinations, and climatic 
conditions would provide valuable insights into species-specific effects on belowground 
ecosystem functioning. This knowledge could support the design of more effective 
agroforestry systems by identifying crop–tree combinations that maximise rhizosphere 
activity, enhance soil C storage, improve nutrient-use efficiency, and contribute to low-
emission, climate-resilient agricultural production systems. 
4.6.  Conclusion 
The findings of this study demonstrate that soil nutrients, root traits, and soil physical 
properties are closely linked within a mature poplar agroforestry system. Although 
originally established as a silvoarable system with active cropping between 1993 and 
2004, crop production subsequently ceased and the alleys became dominated by grasses 
and herbaceous vegetation. Within this system, AN and P were most strongly associated 
with root traits in the topsoil, where approximately 80-90% of root biomass was 
concentrated. Compared with the Arable location, the agroforestry zones had lower BD, 
greater root abundance, and higher root-derived C stocks. Clear differences were also 
observed between the Row and Alley, indicating greater spatial heterogeneity in 
belowground conditions and root distribution. 
Both live and dead roots contributed to belowground C dynamics as live roots were 
more abundant in the Alley, whereas dead roots and associated C stocks were greater in 
the Row. These contrasting patterns suggest spatial variation in root growth, turnover, 
and decomposition across the agroforestry system. Although SOC stocks did not differ 
significantly among the Arable, Row, and Alley locations, significant relationships 
between root traits and SOC indicate that root distribution is closely linked to soil C 
dynamics. Overall, this study shows that the mature agroforestry system promoted 
greater belowground heterogeneity than conventional arable management, particularly 
within the topsoil. These findings highlight the importance of root systems in shaping 
soil structure, nutrient availability, and C cycling within temperate agroforestry systems. 
[PDF PAGE 151]
 
131 
 
Chapter 5.  Leaf litter decomposition and nutrient dynamics beneath 
hedgerows in a temperate agricultural landscape 
Abstract  
Leaf litter decomposition is a vital process that regulates nutrient cycling, carbon (C) 
turnover and ecosystem function. Despite the widespread presence of hedgerows in 
temperate agri-ecosystems, their contribution to litter decomposition and nutrient 
dynamics remains poorly studied. This study investigates leaf litterfall, decomposition 
and nutrient release of two common UK hedgerow species, hawthorn (Crataegus 
monogyna) and hazel (Corylus avellana), using a 366-day litterbag experiment 
conducted in northern England. Hazel produced significantly greater annual litterfall 
than hawthorn, indicating higher litter production at the species level. Decomposition 
followed a biphasic pattern, characterised by rapid mass loss (40-60%) during the initial 
phase (0-223 days) and slower decomposition during the second phase (224-366 days), 
reflecting the progressive depletion of readily decomposable material. Litterbag position 
significantly influenced decomposition dynamics (p<0.001), with buried litter generally 
decomposing more rapidly than surface litter. Decomposition was strongly influenced 
by litter placement (litterbag position), with buried litter decomposing more rapidly than 
surface litter. Species effects on mass loss and decay constants were limited, suggesting 
that environmental conditions associated with litterbag placement were the primary 
drivers of decomposition. Initial litter quality differed between species, with hazel 
exhibiting higher nitrogen (N) concentrations and lower C: N ratios than hawthorn. 
However, nutrient dynamics were influenced by both litter chemistry and litterbag 
placement. Nitrogen concentrations and C: N ratios fluctuated throughout 
decomposition, and litter mass loss was positively correlated with N concentration and 
negatively correlated with C:N ratio. Carbon and N release were strongly associated 
with litter mass loss, demonstrating a close link between decomposition and nutrient 
turnover. Hence, the results show that litter decomposition in temperate hedgerows is 
regulated by the interaction among litter quality, litter placement, and the associated 
decomposition environment. The substantial mass loss and nutrient release observed 
over the study period highlight the important role of hedgerows in supporting nutrient 
turnover and C cycling within agricultural landscapes.  
[PDF PAGE 152]
 
132 
 
5.1.  Introduction 
Leaf litter decomposition is a fundamental ecological process that regulates energy flow 
and nutrient cycling in terrestrial ecosystems (Liao et al., 2022; Zhao et al., 2022). 
Globally, terrestrial vegetation produces nearly 100 billion tonnes of organic matter 
annually, of which approximately 90% enters the detrital pool through senescence and 
litterfall. Through decomposition, this organic matter is processed by complex detritus 
food webs, facilitating the cycling of carbon (C) and essential micro- and 
macronutrients (Didion et al., 2016; Petraglia et al., 2019). Approximately 70% of leaf 
litter contributes to nutrient and energy flows via decomposition, playing a key role in 
terrestrial biogeochemical cycles (Bradford et al., 2016; Cárdenas et al., 2017; Zan et 
al., 2021). During this process, C is released into the atmosphere while a proportion is 
stabilised in soils, and nutrients such as nitrogen (N) and phosphorus (P) are mineralised 
and made available for plant uptake (Freschet et al., 2012). Consequently, litter 
decomposition plays a critical role in maintaining soil fertility, nutrient availability, and 
C dynamics.   
In addition, litterfall and decomposition processes offer valuable insights into soil-tree 
interactions, which underpin the functioning and management of tree-based systems 
(Gray & Schlesinger, 1981; Andivia et al., 2010). Leaf litter serves as a major source of 
organic matter and nutrients to both topsoil and subsoil layers, linking primary 
production to nutrient cycling and ultimately influencing vegetation productivity 
(Giweta, 2020). The rate of leaf litter decomposition is primarily influenced by the 
interaction of three key factors: litter quality (Djukic et al., 2018; Sun et al., 2002), 
environmental factors (Qu et al., 2021; Vitousek et al., 1994), and soil biota (Riutta et 
al., 2012; García-Palacios et al., 2013; Cotrufo et al., 2015). Litter quality, commonly 
characterised by its nutrient content and C:N ratio, strongly influences decomposition 
rates and nutrient release dynamics (Vitousek et al., 1994; Dawoe et al., 2018; Vigulu et 
al., 2019). These properties determine the balance between N mineralisation and 
immobilisation processes, thereby regulating nutrient availability in soils (Seneviratne, 
2000). Climate factors, such as temperature, moisture, and daylight hours, further 
modulate decomposition by influencing microbial activity and litter-processing 
organisms (García-Palacios et al., 2013; Bradford et al., 2016). 
[PDF PAGE 153]
 
133 
 
Hedgerows are a common feature of agricultural landscapes in temperate regions, 
particularly across western and northwestern Europe (Image et al., 2023). They provide 
multiple ecosystem services, including soil erosion control, reduction of runoff, 
enhancement of biodiversity and regulation of local microclimatic conditions (Biffi et 
al., 2023). In the UK, hedgerows are increasingly recognised for their ecological 
importance, with policy initiatives aiming to restore and expand the national hedgerow 
network by 30,000 miles (48,280 km) by 2037 and 45,000 miles (72,420 km) by 2050 
(Defra, 2023a; CPRE, 2024). Species composition within hedgerows can significantly 
influence soil processes and broader ecosystem functioning (Holden et al., 2019). For 
example, hawthorn (Crataegus monogyna), a dominant native species in the UK, 
contributes to C sequestration and habitat provision (Axe et al., 2017), while hazel 
(Corylus avellana) is valued for its ecological adaptability, biodiversity benefits and 
economic importance (Woodland Trust, 2025). As two of the most common native 
hedgerow shrubs in the UK, these species provide an ideal model for investigating 
species-specific influences on litter decomposition and nutrient cycling. 
Despite growing recognition of hedgerows as important components of sustainable 
agricultural landscapes, relatively little is known about their nutrient cycling through 
leaf litter decomposition and nutrient release. Existing research, to date, has largely 
focused on broad functional comparisons, such as deciduous versus evergreen, or on 
forest and agroforestry systems (Cornwall et al., 2008; Zeng et al., 2010; Prescott, 2010; 
Hossain et al., 2011; Hasanuzzaman & Hossain, 2015; Akinyele & Donald-Amaeshi, 
2021). While these studies provide valuable general insights into decomposition 
processes, they may not adequately represent the environmental conditions and species 
assemblages found in temperate hedgerow ecosystems. Empirical data on litterfall 
production, decomposition rates, and nutrient release within hedgerows remain limited, 
especially under UK conditions.  
Differences in litter quantity, chemical composition, and decomposition environment 
(e.g., surface versus subsurface litter placement) may significantly affect nutrient 
turnover and soil fertility, yet these interactions remain underexplored for hedgerow 
systems. Seasonal variation and microenvironmental heterogeneity within hedgerows 
may further influence decomposition processes, contributing to variability in C and N 
dynamics over time. This knowledge gap constrains our ability to quantify the 
[PDF PAGE 154]
 
134 
 
contribution of hedgerows to nutrient cycling, soil fertility, and C sequestration, and 
consequently limits the development of evidence-based management strategies aimed at 
maximising ecosystem service provision. 
Given the increasing emphasis on hedgerow restoration and sustainable land 
management, there is a clear need to quantify decomposition processes and nutrient 
dynamics in these systems. Therefore, this study aims to determine the rate of leaf litter 
decomposition for two common native shrub species, hawthorn and hazel, over a one-
year period in a temperate environment. Specifically, the study sought to: (i) evaluate 
how litter placement (litterbag position) influences decomposition; litter was placed 
either on the soil surface or buried within the soil. This distinction is important because 
different agroecosystems vary in the extent to which litter is incorporated into the soil 
through management practices (e.g., tillage) and biological activity (e.g., earthworms). 
Consequently, surface and buried litter represent two common decomposition pathways. 
Surface litter decomposes under greater exposure to aboveground environmental 
conditions and surface-dwelling decomposers, whereas buried litter has closer contact 
with the soil matrix, higher moisture availability, and greater interaction with soil biota, 
all of which may influence decomposition rates. (ii) determine changes in C and N 
content throughout the decomposition process. By addressing these objectives, this 
research contributes to a better understanding of species-specific nutrient cycling 
dynamics and supports the development of evidence-based approaches to hedgerow 
management and restoration. 
5.2.   Methods  
5.2.1.  Study site  
The study was conducted at the University of Leeds research farm (53.8667° N, -
1.2833° W) in northern England, which covers an area of 317 ha. Approximately 75% 
of the farm is managed for arable production, while 7.7 hectares are dedicated to 
agroforestry systems (University of Leeds, 2019). The farm lies at an elevation of 40 to 
70 metres above sea level and experiences a temperate climate. During the study year 
(2024), the mean annual temperature was 10.7 °C and total rainfall was 786.8 mm (Fig. 
5.1), indicating climate conditions very similar to the long-term average (see Chapter 2).  
[PDF PAGE 155]
 
135 
 
The soils at the site are shallow (30–70 cm depth) and classified as Calcaric Endoleptic 
Cambisols of the Aberford series, overlying a dolostone aquifer of the Cadeby 
Formation (LandIS, 2025). These soils are well-drained and calcareous, typical of 
brown earths associated with gently sloping Permian and Jurassic limestone landscapes 
in central and northern England (Holden et al., 2019; Austen et al., 2022).  
To characterise local environmental conditions, soil temperature and moisture were 
monitored using two Decagon 5TM sensors installed at a depth of 5 cm. Sensors were 
positioned midway along two of the four hedgerows included in the study. However, 
due to a sensor malfunction, data were available for only four months of the study. 
Mean soil temperature was 16.8°C in June, 18.8°C in July, 17.3°C in October, and 
11.4°C in November. Corresponding mean soil moisture values were 11.2%, 8.7%, 
16.4%, and 9.4%, respectively.  These measurements indicate warmer, drier soil 
conditions during summer, followed by cooler, wetter conditions during autumn. 
Figure 5.1 Mean monthly air temperature (red line) and total monthly precipitation 
(blue bars) at the study site in 2024 (Spen Farm, COSMOS-UK monitoring network, UK 
Centre for Ecology and Hydrology, 2026). Month 1 corresponds to January and month 
12 to December. 
5.2.2.  Hedgerow characteristics  
Four hedgerows were selected for the study, ranging from 1.0–2.5 m in width and 1.8–
4.8 m in height. The hedgerows bordered a single arable field managed under a four-

[PDF PAGE 156]
 
136 
 
year crop rotation system consisting of winter wheat, spring or winter barley, and 
oilseed rape. The hedgerows were continuous along all four boundaries of the field, 
forming a well-established linear network within the agricultural landscape. Species 
composition within the hedgerows was diverse, comprising 12 woody species. 
Crataegus monogyna (hawthorn) was the dominant species, accounting for an average 
of 60% of the composition (range: 0-100%). Other commonly occurring species 
included Sambucus nigra (elder) and Ilex aquifolium (holly), each contributing 
approximately 10% of the composition (range: 0-20%), while Corylus avellana (hazel) 
typically accounted for <10%. Additional species present in varying proportions (0-
20%) included Cornus sanguinea (common dogwood) and Rosa canina (dog rose), 
along with less frequent species such as Prunus spinosa (blackthorn), Acer campestre 
(field maple), Fraxinus excelsior (ash), Euonymus europaeus (European spindle), and 
Rhamnus cathartica (buckthorn) (Holden et al., 2019). The dominance of hawthorn, 
combined with the presence of hazel and a range of other native woody species, is 
characteristic of established agricultural hedgerows in the UK. Consequently, the 
selected hedgerows provide a representative system for investigating litter 
decomposition and nutrient cycling processes within temperate agricultural landscapes. 
5.2.3.  Collection of leaf litter  
Leaf litter was collected by hand from four hedgerows in late September to early 
October 2023, prior to natural leaf fall. The location of each hedgerow was recorded 
using a handheld GPS device, and a single coordinate was assigned to represent the 
approximate centre of each sampling area (Hedge 1 = 53.86994 N, 1.33232 W; Hedge 2 
= 53.86990 N, 1.33223 W; Hedge 3 = 53.87077 N, 1.33256 W; Hedge 4 = 53.87069 N, 
1.33262 W). Leaves of hawthorn (HW) and hazel (HZ) were randomly collected across 
all four hedgerows to capture natural variability in litter characteristics and 
decomposition potential between species.  
Following collection, leaves were taken to the laboratory and manually sorted to remove 
non-target material and other organic debris. Samples were then rinsed with water to 
remove adhering soil particles, air-dried for 48 hours and subsequently oven-dried at 
65°C until a constant weight was reached.  This procedure ensured a uniform moisture 
content prior to chemical analysis and the decomposition experiment. 
[PDF PAGE 157]
 
137 
 
To determine initial litter chemistry, subsamples (10-15 g) of oven-dried material were 
randomly selected and finely ground using a mixer miller (Retsch MM400 ball mill; 
RETSCH GmbH, Germany). Total C and N concentrations were determined by 
weighing approximately 100 μg of the homogenised fine powder into aluminium tins 
and analysing them using an elemental combustion analyser (Elementar 
Analysensysteme GmbH, Germany). Four replicate samples were analysed for each 
species, and mean C and N contents were calculated. The C:N ratio was subsequently 
derived and used as an indicator of initial litter quality prior to the decomposition 
experiment.    
5.2.4.  Preparation of litterbags 
Litterbags measuring 20 cm × 20 cm were constructed using 2 mm nylon mesh and 
sealed along all edges. Nylon was chosen for its durability and resistance to UV 
degradation, making it well suited for field-based decomposition studies (Krishna & 
Mohan, 2017). The mesh size (2 mm) was selected to limit access of larger soil 
macrofauna (>2 mm), thereby allowing decomposition to be driven primarily by 
microbial communities and smaller soil fauna (Sulkava and Huhta, 1998; Njoroge et al., 
2022).  
A total of 112 litterbags were prepared, comprising 56 bags for HZ and 56 for HW. The 
amount of litter added to each bag reflected differences in the quantity and morphology 
of leaf material collected for the two species.  Hazel litterbags were filled with between 
1.5 and 2.0 g of dried leaf material, whereas each HW litterbag contained 1.0 g.  Litter 
mass per bag was determined using the total available material, differences in leaf size 
among species, and litterbag dimensions, following established litterbag methodologies 
(Rahman & Tsukamoto, 2013).  
Litterbags were deployed during the first week of January 2024 beneath each of the four 
study hedgerows to assess decomposition under contrasting microenvironmental 
conditions. At each hedgerow, seven HW and seven HZ litterbags were placed on the 
soil surface at 50 cm intervals. An additional seven HW and seven HZ litterbags were 
buried at a depth of 5 cm directly beneath the corresponding surface positions. This 
design resulted in seven paired surface and subsurface litterbags for each species at each 
[PDF PAGE 158]
 
138 
 
hedgerow, allowing direct comparison of decomposition rates between litter placement 
treatments and species. 
Subsurface litterbags were installed following the method of Harman et al. (1999). I 
inserted a spade at an angle of approximately 45° and gently lifted the soil to create a 
cavity. The litterbag was then placed within the opening beneath the lifted soil, after 
which the spade was carefully removed, and the soil pressed firmly back into place to 
ensure good contact between the litterbag and the surrounding soil.  
Surface litterbags were placed directly above their buried counterpart to maintain 
consistency in location and environmental exposure. This paired placement design 
enabled a controlled comparison of litter placement effects on decomposition dynamics, 
particularly with respect to soil contact, moisture, and microclimatic conditions. Each 
litterbag was fitted with a durable plastic tag indicating species identity, hedgerow 
location (Hedge 1–4), and a unique bag number. This ensured reliable identification and 
tracking of individual samples throughout the decomposition experiment. 
5.2.5.  Litterbag retrieval and laboratory analysis 
Litterbags were retrieved on seven occasions during the decomposition experiment: 
April, June, August, October, November, and December 2024, and January 2025. On 
each sampling occasion, two paired surface and subsurface litterbags of each species 
were collected from each of the four hedgerows.   
In some cases, leaf litter could not be fully recovered from the retrieved litterbags.   
Extensive decomposition and mass loss had transformed the litter into fine organic 
residues indistinguishable from soil organic matter, making complete recovery 
impossible. Consequently, the amount of remaining material in some samples was 
insufficient for subsequent mass-loss and nutrient analyses. However, during August, 
rapid hedgerow growth obscured some litterbags, preventing their recovery during the 
scheduled sampling event. A follow-up collection was therefore conducted five days 
later to retrieve the remaining litterbags, resulting in a short sampling interval between 
Days 218 and 223. 
[PDF PAGE 159]
 
139 
 
Immediately following collection, litterbags were placed in sealed plastic bags and 
transported to the laboratory. There, each litterbag was carefully opened using scissors, 
and any adhering soil or other extraneous material was gently removed using a soft 
brush. Any macro- and mesofauna (e.g., snails, earthworms, spiders) present within the 
litterbags were manually removed prior to processing.  
The remaining leaf litter was then rinsed 2–3 times with water to remove residual soil 
particles and organic debris, and then oven-dried at 65°C to a constant mass. The oven-
dried mass was then recorded and used to calculate litter mass remaining throughout the 
decomposition period. After drying, litter samples were ground to a fine powder (~100 
µm) using a mixer mill (Retsch MM400). Subsamples were subsequently analysed for C 
and N content using an elemental CN analyser (as described previously), thereby 
enabling assessment of temporal changes in litter chemistry during decomposition.  
5.2.6.  Estimation of leaf fall 
To quantify mean leaf litterfall rates of HW and HZ, litter was collected from each of 
the four hedgerows during the autumn defoliation period between October and 
December 2024. At each hedgerow, five replicate hessian litter traps (1 m × 1 m) were 
placed centrally on the ground beneath the hedge canopy to capture falling litter. 
Collections were undertaken once in October and twice in each of November and 
December, until leaf fall had ceased.  
Following collection, litter samples were sorted by species to isolate HW and HZ leaf 
litter. The leaves were thoroughly washed with tap water to remove adhering soil 
particles and debris, air-dried, and then oven-dried at 65 °C to a constant mass. Dry 
weights were recorded to calculate litterfall production for each species. 
Litterfall for each species was standardised to a hedge length of 1.0 m and subsequently 
extrapolated to a per-hectare basis. This enabled comparison of annual litter inputs 
between HW and HZ within the study system and provided an estimate of their relative 
contributions to organic matter and nutrient inputs. 
 
[PDF PAGE 160]
 
140

Higher k values indicate faster decomposition rates and, therefore, shorter times

Importantly, k, t₅₀, and t₉₅ represent model-derived measures of decomposition rather 
[PDF PAGE 161]
 
141 
 
than simple empirical estimates of mass loss (%) or decomposition rate (mg day-1). The 
decomposition constant (k) is estimated from the exponential relationship between 
remaining litter mass and time, which reflects the rate of decay. Importantly, t₅₀ and t₉₅ 
represent model-derived measures of decomposition rather than simple empirical 
estimates of mass loss (%) or decomposition rate (mg day⁻¹).  The decomposition 
constant is estimated from the exponential relationship between remaining litter mass 
and time, which reflects the rate of decay. The values of t₅₀ (time required for 50% mass 
loss) and t₉₅ (time required for 95% mass loss) are calculated directly from k and 
provide standardised measures of decomposition that are independent of initial litter 
mass. However, because t₅₀ and t₉₅ are derived from k, they remain dependent on the 
dataset's quality and duration used to estimate the decay constant (Adair et al., 2010; 
Wieder & Lang, 1982).

5.2.8.  Statistical analysis  
Data normality was assessed using the Shapiro-Wilk test. All measured variables, 
including leaf litter mass loss (%), k (day⁻¹), residual C and N concentrations, and C:N 
ratio, remained non-normally distributed even after data transformation. Consequently, 
non-parametric statistical tests were applied for subsequent analyses. The Kruskal-
Wallis test was used to assess the effect of species, sampling time, and litter placement 
(surface vs. buried) on leaf litter mass loss, k and residual nutrient concentrations. 
Spearman’s rank correlation analysis was conducted using the Spearman function in 
RStudio to examine relationships between leaf litter mass loss and litter nutrient 
dynamics (residual and released C, N and C:N ratio) during the decomposition process. 
[PDF PAGE 162]
 
142 
 
Differences in initial litter quality (C, N and C:N ratio) between HW and HZ were 
evaluated using Welch’s two-sample t-test, using the t.test() function in RStudio, as 
initial litter C and N concentrations and C:N ratios were normally distributed. Welch's t-
test was selected because it is robust to unequal variances and suitable for small sample 
sizes. Results were reported as t-statistics, degrees of freedom, p-values, 95% 
confidence intervals, and treatment mean, with statistical significance evaluated at α = 
0.05. All statistical analyses were performed in RStudio (version 4.3.3; R Core Team, 
2024). 
5.3. Results  
5.3.1. Phases of leaf litter decomposition rate  
Litter decomposition was monitored through successive retrieval intervals (0–110, 111–
169, 170–217, 218–223, and 224-273, 274-316, 317-338, 339-366 days). However, 
examination of cumulative litter mass loss revealed two broad phases of decomposition. 
An initial phase (0–223 days) was characterised by rapid litter mass loss, whereas a later 
phase (223–366 days) exhibited slower and more gradual decay. These phases describe 
the overall decomposition dynamics and do not replace the interval-specific 
decomposition rate calculations. 
Across both species, leaf litter mass loss was greatest during the first phase of 
decomposition (0–223 days) and subsequently decreased during the later phase (Fig. 
5.2). Litterbag position and time since deployment had significant effects on mass loss 
(p < 0.001). During the initial decomposition phase, litter mass loss was consistently 
greater in buried than in surface-placed litterbags (Fig. 5.2). Mean mass loss in buried 
HZ litterbags reached 64.68 ± 63.33%, compared with 36.58 ± 37.52% in surface 
litterbags. Similarly, buried HW litterbags exhibited a mean mass loss of 81.52 ± 
75.31%, substantially higher than the 41.75 ± 36.73% observed in surface litterbags 
(Fig. 5.2). 
Decomposition proceeded rapidly for both species during days 0-223. Buried HZ 
litterbags had lost more than 90% of their initial mass by approximately day 217. By the 
beginning of the later decomposition phase (days 224-273), mass loss in surface HZ 
[PDF PAGE 163]
 
143 
 
litterbags had reached approximately 85% (Fig. 5.2). Although HZ generally exhibited 
higher mass-loss values than HW during much of the decomposition period, species 
identity had no significant overall effect on litter mass loss (p = 0.51). 
By the end of the experiment (day 366), decomposition was nearly complete across all 
treatments, with litter mass losses exceeding 98% in every case. Mean mass loss was 
lowest in surface HZ litterbags (98.24%), followed by buried HW litterbags (91.16%), 
and buried HZ litterbags (99.45%), while surface HW litterbags exhibited the greatest 
mass loss (99.73%) (Fig. 5.2). Despite these small differences, treatment values 
converged towards the end of the study, indicating that most decomposition occurred 
during the initial phase and that decomposition rates declined as the remaining litter 
became increasingly recalcitrant. 
 
Figure 5.2 Leaf litter mass loss (%) for HW and HZ leaves, at the soil surface or buried 
at 5 cm depth. The shaded areas around the lines represent the 95% confidence 
interval. The vertical dashed (or dotted) lines indicate the transition point between the 
two decomposition phases identified in the analysis: an initial phase characterised by 
rapid mass loss and a later phase characterised by a slower decomposition rate. The 
position of each dashed line marks the boundary between these phases for the 
respective species and position of leaf litter bags 

[PDF PAGE 164]
 
144 
 
Based on leaf litter mass loss, decomposition rates (mg day⁻¹) were calculated for each 
sampling interval (Table 5.1). Decomposition rate differed significantly between species 
throughout the study period (p < 0.001), whereas litterbag position had no significant 
effect on decomposition rate (p = 0.550). Although decomposition rates did not differ 
statistically between surface and buried litterbags, temporal variation was observed 
during the decomposition process (p = 0.041).  
During the early stages of decomposition (0–223 days), both HW and HZ generally 
exhibited higher decomposition rates in buried litterbags than in surface litterbags. 
However, decomposition rates declined over time for all treatments. By days 218- 223, 
surface HW litter exhibited a decomposition rate of 3.57 mg day⁻¹, exceeding that of 
buried HW litter. This pattern likely reflects the fact that much of the buried litter had 
already decomposed during earlier stages of the experiment, resulting in lower rates 
later in the decomposition process. A similar trend was evident at day 274-316, when 
surface HW litter exhibited a decomposition rate of 2.04 mg day⁻¹, compared with 1.52 
mg day⁻¹ for buried HW litter (Table 5.1). By the final sampling date (366 days), 
decomposition rates in surface and buried HW litter had converged, indicating that 
differences associated with litter placement became less pronounced as decomposition 
progressed.

(p < 0.001; Fig. 5.2). In contrast, litterbag position did not significantly influence 
decomposition rate, despite some variation in decomposition patterns through time. 
The k presented in Table 5.2 represents the Phase 1 and Phase 2 values reported for 223 
and 366 days of decomposition, respectively. During Phase 1, higher k values were 
observed for buried HW and buried HZ litter compared to surface litter. These higher 
decomposition rate constants were associated with lower estimated times required to 
achieve 50% (t₅₀) and 95% (t₉₅) mass loss. This indicates that buried HW and HZ litter 
decomposed more rapidly during the initial stage of decomposition, resulting in greater 
litter turnover than surface litter.

In contrast, during Phase 2, lower k values were observed for buried HW and surface 
HZ litter. These lower decay constants corresponded to longer estimated times required 
t₅₀ and t₉₅, indicating slower decomposition rates and reduced litter turnover. 
Conversely, surface HW and buried HZ litter exhibited higher k values and shorter t₅₀ 
and t₉₅ values, reflecting more rapid decomposition and faster litter turnover (Table 5.2). 
Overall, litterbag placement had a significant effect on decay dynamics (p < 0.01), 
whereas no significant differences in k were observed between species (p = 0.19). 
Furthermore, the decomposition phase (Phase 1 versus Phase 2), identified from the 
decomposition pattern, did not significantly influence k-derived parameters (p = 0.17). 
[PDF PAGE 166]
 
146 
 
Mean annual leaf litterfall was higher for HZ (235.21 kg ha-1) compared to HW (216.4 
kg ha-1), indicating a greater potential contribution of HZ to organic matter inputs 
within the hedgerow system. 
5.3.2. Nutrient dynamics of litter 
Significant inter-species differences were observed in the initial N content and litter C:N 
ratio, whereas initial C content was similar between species (Table 5.3). Hawthorn 
contained significantly less N than HZ (mean: 15.04 g kg-1 vs. 20.55 g kg-1; t = -5.115, 
df = 4.60, p < 0.001). In contrast, initial C contents were nearly identical between the 
two species (370.37 g kg-1vs. 370.57 g kg-1t = -0.062, df = 4.57, p = 0.953), indicating 
no significant difference. Consequently, the initial C: N ratio was markedly higher in 
HW (24.63) than in HZ (18.08), reflecting the lower N concentration relative to C in 
HW litter (t = 5.701, df = 5.97, p < 0.001; 95% CI: 3.75 to 9.40) (Table 5.3). 
Carbon concentration fluctuated throughout the decomposition period and differed 
among litterbag positions, indicating non-linear changes relative to the initial C 
concentration (Table 5.3, Fig. 5.4). Nitrogen concentrations generally increased during 
the initial stages of decomposition in both species compared to their initial values, 
before fluctuating throughout the remainder of the study. Similar temporal patterns were 
observed in the C:N ratio, although responses differed among species and litterbag 
placements. Notably, the C:N ratio of surface-placed HZ litter increased substantially 
over the 366-day decomposition period and remained consistently higher than that of 
buried HZ litter (Table 5.3; Fig. 5.4). Overall, changes in C:N ratio exhibited 
pronounced species- and treatment-specific patterns, with no consistent trend relative to 
the initial litter values.

By Day 366, clear differences in residual litter chemistry were evident between species 
and litterbag positions. In HW, buried litter retained a higher C concentration than 
surface-placed litter, which contained 392.20 g kg-1. In contrast, surface HZ litter 
retained a higher C concentration (392.50 g kg-1) than buried HZ litter (364.97 g kg-1) 
(Table 5.3; Fig. 5.4).  
Nitrogen concentrations also varied among treatments at the final sampling days. 
Surface HW litter contained a higher N concentration (21.08 g kg-1) than buried HW 
litter (11.19 g kg-1). In contrast, buried HZ litter had a higher N concentration (27.50 g 
kg-1) than surface HZ litter (21.16 g kg-1) (Table 5.3; Fig. 5.4). Consequently, the 
highest C:N ratio was in buried HW litter (36.75) and the lowest in buried HZ litter 
[PDF PAGE 169]
 
149 
 
(14.27). Surface HW and HZ litter showed intermediate values of 18.61 and 17.25, 
respectively (Table 5.3; Fig. 5.4).  
Overall, litterbag position and decomposition time significantly influenced C, N, and 
the C: N ratio (p < 0.001). Changes in the C:N ratio reflected concurrent fluctuations in 
both C and N concentrations throughout decomposition. Despite differences in residual 
litter chemistry among treatments, cumulative nutrient losses were substantial, with 
both HW and HZ releasing approximately 99% of their initial C and N by day 366 
regardless of litterbag placement (Table 5.4).

5.3.3. Relationship between nutrients and leaf litter mass loss 
Leaf litter mass loss was significantly correlated with residual N and the C: N ratio in 
both species, but not with residual C, highlighting the contrasting roles of these 
elements in regulating litter breakdown. Spearman's rank correlation analysis showed a 
significant negative correlation between litter mass loss and C: N ratio (ρ = −0.329, p = 
0.01), indicating that greater decomposition was associated with lower C: N ratios (Fig. 
5.4). In contrast, litter mass loss was also significantly positively correlated with N 
concentration (ρ = 0.34, p = 0.01), suggesting that litter with higher N concentrations 
decomposed more extensively.  No significant relationship was found between litter 
mass loss and C concentration (ρ = −0.17, p = 0.21) (Fig. 5.4).  
[PDF PAGE 170]
 
150 
 
 
Figure 5.4 Relationship between leaf litter mass loss (%) for both species and their 
residual nutrient content - (a) C (g/kg), (b) N (g/kg) and (c) C:N ratio   

[PDF PAGE 171]
 
151 
 
 
Figure 5.5 Relationship between leaf litter mass loss (%) of both species and their 
nutrient release (%). 
Carbon and N release were both strongly and positively correlated with leaf litter mass 
loss (C: ρ = 0.89, p < 0.001; N: ρ = 0.80, p < 0.001; Fig. 5.5). These relationships 
indicate that nutrient release increased as decomposition progressed and litter mass 
declined. The stronger correlation observed for C suggests that C release closely tracked 
the loss of litter mass throughout decomposition. In contrast, the weaker relationship 
between N release and litter mass throughout the decomposition indicated greater 
variability in N dynamics, likely reflecting concurrent processes of mineralisation, 
immobilisation, and microbial uptake. 
Overall, these results demonstrate that litter quality, particularly N concentration and 
C:N ratio, played an important role in influencing decomposition dynamics, whereas 
residual C concentration showed little direct association with litter mass loss. The strong 
relationships between nutrient release and litter decomposition further highlight the 
close coupling between organic matter turnover and nutrient cycling within the 
hedgerow ecosystem. 

[PDF PAGE 172]
 
152 
 
5.4.  Discussion  
5.4.1.  Factors controlling leaf litter decomposition and carbon cycling 
Leaf litter decomposition showed a characteristic biphasic pattern of rapid initial mass 
loss followed by a slower decomposition phase (Fig. 5.2; Table 5.1 and 5.2). This trend 
is commonly attributed to the loss of labile compounds during the initial phase of decay, 
followed by the persistence of more recalcitrant materials such as lignin and other 
structural components (Berg & McClaugherty, 2020; Suseela et al., 2013). Generally, 
buried litterbags exhibited greater mass loss during the initial phase of decomposition, 
suggesting that increased litter–soil contact and more favourable moisture conditions 
enhanced degradation between 0-223 days (Fig 5.2; Chen et al., 2021; Su et al., 2021).  
In the later phase, by day 366, both species and litterbag positions had achieved very 
high mass loss (98.19–99.73%) (Fig. 5.2; Table 5.5). These values were higher than 
those in other temperate European studies summarised in Table 5.5. For example, 
Cornelissen (1996) reported a 15.2–48.4% reduction in litter mass by 244 days, while 
Remy et al. (2018) observed a 52.7–92.7% mass loss after 548 days. Although climatic 
conditions at the present study site were broadly comparable to those reported by Remy 
et al. (2018), litter decomposition beneath hedgerows was substantially greater.   
This enhanced mass loss may reflect the influence of increased soil biotic activity, more 
complex habitat structure, and favourable microclimatic conditions associated with 
hedgerow environments, all of which can promote litter degradation (Cornelissen, 1996; 
Holden et al., 2019). These findings were consistent with Remy et al. (2018), who 
observed differences in litter decomposition between forest edges and forest-interior 
environments.  
Differences in decomposition rates between species may also be linked to variation in 
litter quality (Table 5.1). Previous studies have shown that litter from oak and conifer 
species often contain higher concentrations of lignin, tannins, and other structural 
compounds that can alter decomposition, whereas HW and HZ litter may provide more 
readily available substrates for decomposer communities (Table 5.5).  Furthermore, 
while small differences in decay constants were observed between HW and HZ litter, 
[PDF PAGE 173]
 
153 
 
these differences were not statistically significant, suggesting that species identity was 
not a major determinant of decomposition rate. Rather, litterbag position and the 
resulting microenvironmental conditions were the primary factors influencing 
decomposition dynamics. Consequently, any apparent differences between species 
should be interpreted with caution (Tables 5.1 and 5.2; Li et al., 2026). 
Moreover, burial of litterbags appeared to modify decomposition pathways by 
increasing litter–soil contact and exposing litter to more stable moisture and temperature 
conditions, factors that generally promote microbial activity (Swift et al., 1979; Berg & 
McClaugherty, 2020). The observed effects of litter burial suggest that decomposition 
dynamics were influenced primarily by changes in the surrounding microenvironment 
rather than by species identity (Table 5.2). Burial may also have facilitated interactions 
among litter, soil microorganisms, and soil fauna, thereby enhancing litter 
fragmentation and microbial processing, and contributing to the formation of soil 
organic C (Wei et al., 2022; Sofia et al., 2023). These findings indicate that litter 
incorporation into soil, whether through earthworm activity or agricultural tillage, can 
influence decomposition processes and may play an important role in C cycling within 
hedgerow systems (García-Palacios et al., 2013). 
Burial of litterbags appeared to increase decomposition by improving litter–soil contact 
and exposing litter to more stable moisture and temperature conditions, both of which 
generally promote microbial activity (Swift et al., 1979; Berg & McClaugherty, 2020). 
However, as no significant differences in the decay constant were observed between the 
buried HW and HZ litter, the results suggest that species identity had a limited influence 
on decomposition rates under burial conditions (Table 5.2)
[PDF PAGE 174]
