[PDF PAGE 175]
 
155 
 
Nevertheless, decomposition dynamics during the later stages of decay may have been 
influenced by additional factors beyond initial litter quality. The deposition of 
surrounding organic residues and the ingress of soil fauna and microfauna into litterbags 
after placement at a depth of 5 cm may have altered microbial activity and litter 
fragmentation, thereby may affected overall decomposition and contributing to the 
formation of soil organic C (Wei et al., 2022; Sofia et al., 2023). These findings indicate 
that litter incorporation into soil, whether through earthworm activity or agricultural 
tillage, can substantially influence decomposition processes and consequently play an 
important role in C cycling within hedgerow systems (García-Palacios et al., 2013) 
The 2 mm mesh size used in this study allowed access by small soil fauna, possibly 
aiding litter fragmentation and facilitating microbial colonisation (Bradford et al., 
2002). Soil fauna including snails, earthworms, and spiders were observed within buried 
litterbags during spring-autumn and winter retrievals, indicating active faunal 
interactions with litter throughout much of the year, suggesting that decomposition 
continued under colder seasonal conditions is consistent with studies demonstrating 
microbial activity can persist beneath snow cover and contribute to litter mass loss 
(Uchida et al., 2005). These observations emphasise the critical importance of litterbag 
mesh size in determining the extent to which soil fauna contribute to decomposition 
processes. Overall, decomposition dynamics in hedgerows adjacent to agricultural 
systems varied with litter placement; however, the consistently high rates of mass loss 
observed in this study suggest that UK hedgerows provide favourable conditions for 
litter decomposition and play a potentially important role in local C cycling (Carreiro et 
al., 2000).  
5.4.2.  Influence of litter quality, nutrient dynamics and environmental 
conditions on leaf litter decomposition  
The decomposition of leaf litter is strongly affected by litter chemistry, particularly N 
availability and C: N stoichiometry, which regulate microbial activity and nutrient 
cycling (Aerts, 1997; Berg & McClaugherty, 2020; Manzoni et al., 2021; Yu et al., 
2025). In the present study, HZ litter had a higher initial N concentration and a lower 
C:N ratio than HW litter, indicating higher litter quality (Table 5.3; Fig. 5.3). These 
features are traditionally associated with faster decomposition because they reduce 
[PDF PAGE 176]
 
156 
 
nutrient limitation for decomposer organisms (Cornelissen et al., 1999). However, litter 
chemistry alone could not fully account for the decomposition patterns (Dent et al., 
2006). Despite its lower initial N concentrations and a higher C: N ratio, HW 
decomposed more quickly than HZ in surface litterbags and also showed more rapid 
decomposition when buried (Table 5.3; Fig. 5.3). This implies that decomposition rates 
were affected not only by litter quality but also by environmental conditions associated 
with litterbag position, as discussed in section 5.1. 
Nitrogen content of the litter varied considerably throughout the decomposition period 
and did not diminish consistently over time (Table 5.3). This variability was probably 
driven by microbial immobilisation and mineralisation processes, together with nutrient 
exchange between the litter and the surrounding hedgerow environment (Prescott & 
Vesterdal, 2021). The positive relationship between litter mass loss and N 
concentration, and the negative correlation between litter mass loss and C:N ratio, 
indicate that the availability of N in the litter is important in regulating decomposition 
(Fig. 5.4; Zheng et al., 2022). In contrast. residual C content was not significantly 
associated with litter mass loss. This finding suggests that decomposition was 
influenced more strongly by litter quality traits and species composition than by total C 
content alone. Such results are consistent with previous studies showing that plant 
functional traits and litter chemistry are primary drivers of decomposition processes 
(Cornwell et al., 2008; Cornelissen et al., 1999).  
Although N concentration and C: N ratio contributed to decomposition, their effects 
depended on environmental conditions, suggesting that N alone did not determine 
decomposition rates in hedgerow litter. Enhanced microbial activity under hedgerows 
likely promoted nutrient mineralisation and nutrient exchange between litter and soil, 
thereby influencing the nutrient dynamics observed throughout the experiment 
(Akinyele & Donald-Amaeshi, 2021; Froufe et al., 2020).  
Litter position influenced decomposition patterns, with differences observed between 
buried and surface litterbags. These differences may reflect variation in environmental 
conditions experienced by the litter, although factors such as soil moisture and 
microbial activity were not directly measured in this study. Seasonal variation may also 
have contributed to the observed patterns, with warmer periods generally associated 
[PDF PAGE 177]
 
157 
 
with greater nutrient release and colder winter conditions with reduced nutrient turnover 
(Aerts, 1997). In addition to microbial processes, decomposer fauna might have 
influenced patterns of nutrient release and retention. Surface litter was more accessible 
to detritivorous invertebrate species (e.g., earthworms, woodlice, mites and springtails) 
whose feeding and fragmentation behaviour increase litter surface area and facilitate 
microbial colonisation (Hättenschwiler et al., 2005). Seasonal variation in faunal 
abundance and activity may thus have accounted for the differences observed between 
surface and buried litterbags.  
Moreover, HZ produced significantly more litter than HW, potentially increasing 
organic matter inputs to the soil and stimulating microbial activity, which may partly 
explain the relatively faster decomposition of buried HZ litter (Pedersen et al., 2017; 
Erdem and Karavin, 2016). In contrast, the rapid decomposition of HW despite its lower 
litter production suggests that litter quality, rather than quantity alone, also played an 
important role in decomposition dynamics. This finding is consistent with previous 
studies demonstrating species-specific litter effects of litter traits on decomposition 
processes (Bellingham et al., 2013). 
Overall, the results suggest that nutrient dynamics during decomposition were governed 
by a combination of litter quality, litter position, seasonal environmental conditions and 
decomposer activities. Although the N and C: N ratio were important predictors of 
decomposition, their influence was moderated by environmental conditions and biotic 
interactions. Consequently, N availability alone was insufficient to explain 
decomposition patterns in hedgerow litter, highlighting the importance of considering 
both litter traits and environmental context when evaluating decomposition processes in 
hedgerow ecosystems.   
 
 
 
 
[PDF PAGE 178]
 
158 
 
5.5.  Conclusion 
1. Leaf litter decomposition. Leaf litter decomposition in temperate hedgerows followed 
a distinct two-phase pattern, characterised by rapid mass loss during the initial stages (0-
223 days) of decomposition followed by a slower rate of breakdown thereafter (223-366 
days). Hazel produced higher litterfall than HW on a species basis, suggesting a greater 
potential for the return of organic matter and nutrients. However, because HW is the 
dominant hedgerow species within the study area, its overall contribution to litter inp uts 
and soil C and nutrient cycling is likely substantial and may exceed that of HZ at the 
hedgerow scale. Therefore, both species play important roles in regulating organic matter 
dynamics and ecosystem functioning within hedgerow systems. Decomposition was 
strongly influence by decomposition environment, with buried litter decomposing more 
rapidly than surface litter regardless of species. Although leaf litter mass loss and decay 
constants did not differ significantly among species, the consistently higher 
decomposition rates observed in buried litterbags suggests that environmental conditions 
associated with litter placement was a stronger determinant of decomposition than species 
identity. These findings highlight the importance of the decomposition environment in 
regulating litter turnover and C cycling within temperate hedgerow systems. 
2. Litter quality and nutrient dynamics. Initial litter quality differed significantly between 
species, with HW exhibiting higher N concentrations and lower C:  N ratios than HW. 
However, decomposition patterns were not determined solely by initial litter chemistry, 
indicating that litter quality interacts with environmental conditions to regulate 
decomposition processes. Temporal changes in N concentration and C:  N ratio reflected 
active nutrient cycling throughout the decomposition period, with litter mass loss 
positively associated with N concentration and negatively associated with the C: N ratio. 
These relationships highlight the importance of N availability in promoting litter 
breakdown and nutrient turnover. Carbon and N release remained closely linked to litter 
mass loss, highlighting the strong interplay between organic matter decomposition and 
nutrient cycling. Overall, these findings indicate that nutrient dynamics beneath 
hedgerows are regulated  by both litter quality and local microenvironment, which 
together appear to provide favourable conditions for decomposer activity and nutrient 
cycling. 
[PDF PAGE 179]
 
159 
 
In summary, the study demonstrates that environmental conditions associated with litter 
placement exert a greater influence on decomposition dynamics than species identity. The 
enhanced decomposition observed in buried litterbags was likely driven by improved soil 
contact, more stable moisture conditions, and greater access to decomposer communities. 
Litterbag mesh size and placement may also influence the accessibility of litter to soil 
fauna and microbial communities, potentially affecting decomposition and nutrient 
release. 
Given ongoing efforts to expand and restore hedgerows across the UK, further research 
examining a wider range of hedgerow species, litter qualities, and decomposer 
communities across contrasting environmental conditions. Such research would improve 
understanding of the mechanism controlling decomposition and nutrient cycling in 
hedgerow systems and support evidence -based management  strategies aimed at 
maximising both C sequestration and broader ecosystem service delivery. 
[PDF PAGE 180]
 
160 
 
Chapter 6:  Synthesis 
6.1.  Outline of chapter  
This final chapter synthesises the key findings presented in Chapters 2 to 5 and places 
them within a broader context of agroforestry as a climate mitigation strategy. Section 
6.2 summarises the major findings from each chapter. Building on these findings, 
Section 6.3 integrates the results to synthesise an understanding of carbon (C) and 
nutrient dynamics in agroforestry systems.  
The implications of the research for policy and practice are discussed in Section 6.4, 
with particular emphasis on the contribution of agroforestry to climate change 
mitigation.  Section 6.5 outlines the study's limitations and the challenges encountered 
during the research process. Section 6.6 identifies priorities for future research, 
highlighting the need to improve understanding of the mechanisms governing C and 
nutrient dynamics and to support the development of more efficient agroforestry 
systems for climate change mitigation and sustainable land management. Finally, 
Section 6.7 concludes the thesis by summarising the principal findings, their 
significance, and their contribution to advancing knowledge of C and nutrient dynamics 
in agroforestry systems. 
6.2.  Summary of key findings 
The overall aim of this thesis was to evaluate the aboveground and belowground 
interactions that lead to the quantification of C storage and accumulation in temperate 
agroforestry systems and to examine the biotic and abiotic factors influencing C and 
nutrient cycling. Specifically, this thesis evaluated the effects of agroforestry 
configuration, tree species, time, climate variability, soil nutrient availability, root 
dynamics, and litter decomposition on aboveground and belowground C pools and 
nutrient dynamics. I focused on the four research questions (RQ) to understand the C 
and nutrient dynamics in temperate agroforestry that were outlined in section 1.2.  
[PDF PAGE 181]
 
161 
 
1. How do aboveground biomass (AGB) C stocks and annual C increments vary 
among poplar hybrids over a 30-year period, and to what extent are these 
variations influenced by local climate variability? 
2. What are the effects of a 30-year-old poplar-based silvoarable system on soil 
organic carbon (SOC) storage and sequestration, and how could expansion of 
such systems contribute to UK net-zero targets through enhanced C 
sequestration? 
3. How do spatial variations in soil nutrient availability across locations and soil 
depths influence root traits, root dynamics, and belowground C inputs, turnover, 
and storage within an agroforestry system? 
4. How does leaf litter location (surface vs. buried) influence decomposition rates 
and the dynamics of C and nitrogen (N) content during decomposition? 
Chapters 2-5 address each of the RQs in turn.  
Chapter 2 evaluated the AGB C sequestration potential of four poplar hybrids in a 
mature silvoarable agroforestry system. The results highlighted the value of PAIC for 
assessing long-term C dynamics in mature agroforestry systems, where growth and C 
accumulation are influenced by changing weather and environmental conditions. 
Trichobel outperformed the other hybrids, sequestering approximately 50% more C than 
the least productive hybrid, whereas Beaupre showed high early productivity followed 
by a decline, suggesting greater sensitivity to changing environmental conditions. 
Seasonal climatic variables, particularly summer conditions (R² = 43-59%, p ≤ 0.04), 
had a stronger effect on C accumulation than annual weather conditions. Soil 
temperature negatively affected PAIC of Beaupre and Gibecq, reducing it by 22% and 
38.2%, respectively, whereas rainfall and wind speed positively influenced Trichobel. 
Cropping history also influenced C accumulation, with continuous fallow (CF) 
supporting higher AGB C stocks (53.7%) and increment rates than continuous cropping 
(CC) during the active silvoarable phase, likely reflecting reduced competition for 
resources. Overall, the findings demonstrate the combined importance of genotype, 
climate, and management in determining long-term C sequestration. 
Building on these findings, Chapter 3 quantified SOC stocks and sequestration in the 
same mature poplar silvoarable system and assessed the potential contribution of large-
[PDF PAGE 182]
 
162 
 
scale expansion to UK net-zero targets. The silvoarable systems contained 27% more 
SOC than the adjacent conventional arable field (73.7 vs. 58.9 Mg C ha⁻¹) and achieved 
an average SOC sequestration rate of 0.55 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹.  Scenario modelling showed 
that AGB was the dominant C pool, accounting for 66% of total sequestration, while 
belowground biomass (BGB) and SOC each contributed 17%. Trichobel again showed 
the greatest sequestration potential, storing approximately 52% more C than Gibecq. 
Depending on adoption scenario and hybrid selection, estimated CO2 removals ranged 
from 0.87 to 7.16 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹, highlighting the significant climate change mitigation 
potential of agroforestry expansion in the UK. 
Chapter 4 examined the interactions between root traits, soil nutrients, and SOC storage. 
Available nitrogen and P were positively associated with root growth, while bulk 
density showed negative relationships with root traits, indicating improved soil physical 
conditions under agroforestry. Although SOC stocks did not differ significantly among 
locations and depths, SOC concentrations increased at 45cm depth. Most live and dead 
root biomass (86-99%) was concentrated in the topsoil, and agroforestry also promoted 
deeper rooting than in the adjacent arable field. Tree rows and alleys supported 
substantially greater root development, with 80-90% higher live and dead root length 
density, biomass density, and root C stocks than the arable field. These findings 
emphasise the importance of root production, turnover, and necromass inputs as key 
drivers of belowground C accumulation and nutrient cycling. 
Lastly, Chapter 5 investigated leaf litter decomposition and nutrient dynamics in 
temperate hedgerows. Decomposition followed a biphasic pattern, characterised by 
rapid mass loss (40-60%) during the first 223 days, followed by slower decomposition 
thereafter. Litter placement –was the main driver of decomposition, with buried litter 
decomposing significantly faster than surface litter (p < 0.001), while species effects 
were limited. Although hazel litter had higher N concentrations and lower C: N ratios 
than hawthorn, nutrient release was more strongly influenced by decomposition 
environment than species identity. Strong relationships between mass loss, N 
concentration and C:N ratio indicated the close coupling of decomposition and nutrient 
cycling. Overall, the study highlights the important role of hedgerows in organic matter 
turnover, nutrient cycling and C dynamics within agriculture.  
[PDF PAGE 183]
 
163 
 
6.3.  Integration of key findings  
Collectively, the findings demonstrate that C sequestration in temperate agroforestry 
systems is governed by interconnected biological and environmental processes 
operating across multiple C pools and temporal scales. While tree biomass represented 
the largest C pool, long-term sequestration depended on the combined contributions of 
root-derived C inputs, SOC, and decomposition-driven nutrient cycling. 
The overall aim of the thesis is to examine the key biotic and abiotic factors controlling 
C storage and sequestration in temperate agroforestry systems by examining AGB, soil 
properties, root characteristics, and their influence on C and nutrient cycling processes. 
Overall, the thesis shows how species selection, time/age, tree density, cropping 
treatments, and climatic conditions interact to regulate C sequestration, C storage, and 
decomposition-driven nutrient cycling in agroforestry systems. The results of this thesis 
demonstrate that agroforestry contributes to C sequestration through multiple pathways 
operating both aboveground and belowground. Findings from the mature poplar-based 
silvoarable agroforestry system (Chapters 2-4) showed that C storage is distributed 
among AGB, BGB (based on root-shoot ratio), and SOC, highlighting the 
multifunctional role of trees in capturing and retaining atmospheric C. This is consistent 
with previous studies that identify agroforestry as an effective land-use strategy because 
C is stored simultaneously in vegetation and soil pools (Lorenz & Lal, 2014; Nair et al., 
2009ab). 
Fig. 6.1 summarises the major pathways contributing to C sequestration in agroforestry 
systems. In the mature silvoarable agroforestry system (Chapters 2-4), C sequestration 
was primarily driven by biomass accumulation, with AGB representing the largest C 
pool. Root growth, turnover, and necromass production contributed to BGB storage and 
SOC formation. Root-soil interactions, nutrient availability, and lower bulk density 
created favourable conditions for long-term C accumulation. In the hedgerow 
agroforestry system (Chapter 5), litter decomposition regulated C and nutrient cycling, 
with litter placement affecting decomposition rates and nutrient release. Together, these 
processes illustrate how different agroforestry configurations contribute to ecosystem C 
sequestration through biomass storage, root-mediated inputs, and organic matter 
turnover. 
[PDF PAGE 184]
 
164 
 
 
Figure 6.1 Conceptual pathways of carbon sequestration in agroforestry 
Overall, Chapters 2-4 demonstrate how mature silvoarable agroforestry systems store C 
through biomass accumulation and root-mediated soil processes, whereas Chapter 5 
highlights the contribution of litter decomposition to C and nutrient cycling within 
hedgerow agroforestry systems. Together, these findings show that both root turnover 
(Chapter 4) and litter decomposition (Chapter 5) represent pathways through which 
plant-derived C enters the soil. Although operating through different mechanisms, both 
contribute to long-term SOC formation. Hence, agroforestry enhances C sequestration 
through multiple, interconnected mechanisms operating across different agroforestry 
designs and C pools. 
6.3.1.  Role of hybrids and species 
Across all chapters, species identity influenced the quantity of C entering agroforestry 
systems, whereas environmental and management factors more strongly regulated the 
rate at which C was retained, transformed, or recycled. Species and hybrid selection 
strongly influenced C sequestration across the agroforestry systems studied, 

[PDF PAGE 185]
 
165 
 
highlighting that the climate-mitigation potential of agroforestry depends not only on 
the presence of trees but also on the characteristics of the species selected. Within the 
mature poplar silvoarable system, substantial variation in long-term C accumulation was 
observed among hybrids. Trichobel achieved the highest mean AGB C stock (65.77 Mg 
C ha⁻¹), MAIC (2.13 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹) and PAIC (2.33 Mg C ha⁻¹ yr⁻¹), and sequestered 
approximately 52% more C than Gibecq in the UK upscaling Scenarios. These findings 
demonstrate that hybrid selection can significantly influence biomass production and 
long-term C sequestration potential. 
The results further suggest that early growth performance is not necessarily a reliable 
indicator of long-term C storage. Although Beaupre exhibited significantly greater 
height growth during the first seven years following establishment, Trichobel 
maintained superior C accumulation at maturity, indicating that long-term sequestration 
outcomes depend on interactions between genotype, environmental conditions and 
management practices. Similar genotype × environment interactions have been widely 
reported in poplar systems, where hybrid performance varies according to site 
conditions, competition and resource availability (Burgess et al., 2005). 
Species selection also influenced C cycling processes. While Chapter 5 found limited 
effects of hedgerow species on litter mass loss and decomposition rates, species 
determined the quantity and quality of litter entering the soil. In contrast, environmental 
conditions and litter placement exerted a stronger influence on decomposition dynamics 
and nutrient release. These findings may suggest that species choice affects C inputs to 
soil, whereas management and environmental conditions largely regulate the rate at 
which plant-derived C is transformed and incorporated into soil organic matter (Cotrufo 
et al., 2013; Dong et al., 2026). 
The findings also highlight important trade-offs between maximising C sequestration 
and delivering broader farm benefits. Fast-growing species such as poplar are frequently 
favoured in silvoarable agroforestry because they rapidly accumulate biomass, sequester 
large quantities of C and can produce marketable timber within relatively short rotations 
(Forest Research, 2024; Woodland Trust, 2023). In contrast, productive agroforestry 
species such as apple, hazel and walnut provide opportunities for income diversification 
through fruit and nut production while maintaining agricultural output between tree 
[PDF PAGE 186]
 
166 
 
rows (Agroforestry Research Trust, accessed 2026; Whittick, 2024a, 2024b). Current 
agri-environment schemes in England, including the Sustainable Farming Incentive 
(SFI) and Countryside Stewardship Higher Tier (CSHT), increasingly support such 
systems because of their wider environmental and economic benefits. However, the 
present study suggests that these systems are unlikely to achieve the same levels of 
biomass C storage as fast-growing poplar-based systems. Consequently, species 
selection should balance C sequestration objectives against income diversification, 
resilience, nutrient cycling, crop compatibility and long-term C permanence. 
Overall, the results indicate that species and hybrid selection influence multiple 
components of agroforestry C dynamics, including biomass accumulation, litter inputs 
and nutrient cycling. Maximising the climate-change mitigation potential of 
agroforestry therefore requires selecting species that are appropriate not only for site 
conditions but also for the primary objectives of the system, whether these are C 
storage, agricultural productivity, income diversification or a combination of these 
outcomes. 
6.3.2.  Management and site design  
Beyond species choice and system age, management practices and site design strongly 
influenced C accumulation and cycling within the agroforestry systems. In the mature 
poplar silvoarable system, alley management affected AGB C sequestration, particularly 
during the establishment and active cropping phases when competition between trees 
and crops was greatest. The CF treatment maintained higher AGB C stocks and greater 
C accumulation rates than treatments where crops were present, likely because reduced 
competition for light, water, and nutrients promoted greater tree growth. In contrast, 
crop production on either side of the tree rows constrained tree growth and C 
accumulation, highlighting the importance of crop-tree interactions in determining stand 
productivity (Burgess et al., 2005; Jose, 2009). Following the cessation of cropping, 
treatment differences may progressively decrease, indicating that the effects of alley 
management were largely associated with the period of active competition. These 
findings suggest that minimising crop-tree competition may be advantageous where 
maximising long-term AGB C sequestration is a primary management objective. 
[PDF PAGE 187]
 
167 
 
Management effects were also evident belowground. The significant difference between 
CF and CC treatment effects observed in SOC stock, particularly in Blocks 2 and 3, 
suggests that historical differences in C accumulation persisted despite the cessation of 
cropping. However, the long period under unmanaged vegetation likely attenuated 
treatment contrasts, which may explain the absence of significant differences in SOC 
concentration and bulk density. Thus, the current SOC stock pattern may appear to 
reflect both the legacy of past silvoarable management and the subsequent transition 
toward a vegetation-dominated system. Furthermore, analysis of soil samples from 
multiple sampling points revealed significantly higher SOC stocks in the agroforestry 
zones than in the conventional arable field in the 0-15 cm soil layer. However, when 
SOC stocks were assessed using soil samples collected from only three representative 
pits and evaluated at a finer spatial scale (5-45 cm depth), no significant differences 
were detected among the arable field, tree rows, and alley positions. This discrepancy 
likely reflects the influence of sampling intensity and spatial variability in SOC 
distribution, whereby a larger number of sampling points provided greater statistical 
power to detect differences between land-use systems. 
Agroforestry zones supported substantially greater root length density, root biomass and 
root C stocks than conventional arable land, while lower bulk density and deeper 
rooting patterns created favourable conditions for continued C inputs to the soil profile 
(Cardinael et al., 2018). Although changes in SOC stocks were not always detectable at 
fine spatial scales, root turnover, rhizodeposition and root necromass provided a 
continuous pathway for transferring plant-derived C into the soil, contributing to long-
term soil C accumulation and stabilisation (Rasse et al., 2005; Cardinael et al., 2015; 
Kätterer et al., 2011; Pankaj et al., 2023). 
Similarly, findings from the hedgerow system demonstrated the importance of leaf litter 
decomposition as a complementary pathway linking plant biomass production to soil C 
and nutrient cycling. The faster decomposition of buried litter highlighted how 
management can influence the rate at which C and nutrients are transferred into the soil. 
Changes in C, N, and C: N ratios during decomposition indicated active immobilisation 
and mineralisation processes that regulate nutrient cycling and contribute to soil organic 
matter formation (Cotrufo et al., 2013; Dong et al., 2026). Root turnover and litter 
decomposition represent complementary pathways linking plant production to soil C 
[PDF PAGE 188]
 
168 
 
formation. Whereas root-derived C enters the soil directly through root mortality, 
exudation, and rhizodeposition, litter-derived C contributes through aboveground 
organic matter inputs and subsequent decomposition. Together, these processes form 
the principal biological pathways through which agroforestry systems transfer 
atmospheric C into longer-term soil storage. While root turnover (Chapter 4) and litter 
decomposition (Chapter 5) may work through different mechanisms, both represent 
important pathways through which plant-derived C enters the soil and contributes to 
long-term SOC formation (Rasse et al., 2005; Cotrufo et al., 2013). 
Together, the findings from Chapters 2 to 5 demonstrate that agroforestry enhances C 
sequestration through multiple interconnected mechanisms. Aboveground carbon is 
accumulated through tree biomass growth, while BGB C storage is supported by root 
production, root turnover, rhizodeposition and litter decomposition. The relative 
contribution of these processes is influenced by management decisions throughout the 
lifetime of the system, emphasising that agroforestry performance depends not only on 
tree establishment but also on how systems are managed over time (Jose, 2009; 
Cardinael et al., 2018b). 
Site design further influenced the magnitude of C storage. Wider alleys supported fewer 
trees per hectare and therefore lower AGB C at the system scale, whereas narrower 
alleys and higher tree densities increased biomass C storage by allocating a greater 
proportion of land to trees. However, increasing tree density may also intensify 
competition with crops for light, water and nutrients. Tree spacing additionally affects 
root distribution, litter inputs and rhizosphere processes, influencing both aboveground 
and belowground C dynamics. Consequently, maximising agroforestry C sequestration 
requires balancing tree density, spatial arrangement and crop productivity, highlighting 
the need for site-specific system designs rather than relying on generic C sequestration 
estimates (Ivezić et al., 2022; Muñoz-Rojas et al., 2023; Sutterlütti et al., 2023). 
6.3.3.  Tree age and system maturity 
The findings of this thesis demonstrate that system age is a fundamental determinant of 
C sequestration in agroforestry systems. Much of the current evidence base is derived 
from young agroforestry systems, whereas the present research evaluated a mature 
[PDF PAGE 189]
 
169 
 
silvoarable system approximately 30 years after establishment. The results showed that 
C in AGB continued to accumulate over multiple decades, highlighting the importance 
of long-term monitoring for accurately quantifying agroforestry climate mitigation 
potential. 
The divergence among hybrids became more pronounced over time. Between 2005 and 
2023, Trichobel's MAIC increased by 34.8%, compared with increases of only 10.8%–
13.8% for the other hybrids. By 2023, Trichobel maintained a MAIC that was 49.0%–
77.5% higher than Beaupre, Robusta, and Gibecq. Similar trends were observed for 
PAIC. Although Beaupre exhibited the highest PAIC during establishment, Trichobel 
became dominant by 2005, exceeding the other hybrids by 36.8%–44.3%. This 
advantage continued through stand development, and by 2023 Trichobel's PAIC was 
171%–455% greater than the other hybrids. These findings indicate that Trichobel 
sustained substantially higher C sequestration rates at maturity, whereas the other 
hybrids showed a stronger age-related decline in productivity. 
Importantly, had the assessment been conducted only during the first decade following 
establishment, Beaupre would have been identified as the best-performing hybrid. 
However, the 30-year dataset showed that Trichobel ultimately accumulated 
approximately 65% more C than Beaupre and 95% more than Gibecq. This 
demonstrates that short-term studies may substantially misrepresent lifetime C 
sequestration potential and reinforces the need for mature-system datasets when 
developing C accounting methodologies, sequestration factors and agroforestry policy 
recommendations. 
Rather than directly explaining the SOC patterns observed in the mature silvoarable 
system, the hedgerow study complements the broader understanding of agroforestry 
highlighting how litter quality, residue placement, and nutrient dynamics influence the 
transformation of plant-derived C into soil organic matter. Fluctuations in N 
concentration and C:N ratio during decomposition reflected periods of immobilisation 
and mineralisation, suggesting that part of the decomposing organic matter may 
contribute to the formation of longer-term soil C. Overall, my research in Chapters 2-4 
demonstrates how mature silvoarable agroforestry systems store C through biomass 
accumulation and root-mediated soil processes, whereas Chapter 5 highlights the 
[PDF PAGE 190]
 
170 
 
contribution of litter decomposition to C and nutrient cycling within hedgerow 
agroforestry systems. Together, these findings show that agroforestry enhances C 
sequestration through multiple, interconnected mechanisms operating across different 
agroforestry designs and C pools. 
6.3.4.  Weather conditions  
Weather was an important driver of tree C accumulation throughout the study period. In 
Chapter 2, analysis of weather records from 1993-2023 showed that summer climatic 
conditions, in line with expectations (Kutsokon et al., 2015; Rosso et al., 2023; 
Gudynaitė-Franckevičienė et al., 2022), exerted the strongest influence on PAIC, 
particularly for Trichobel and Beaupre. Notably, Trichobel maintained high C 
accumulation despite climatic variability, indicating a greater capacity to tolerate 
environmental stress and sustain growth under adverse weather conditions. Genotypes 
with greater tolerance to weather variability are likely to provide more stable long-term 
C sequestration under future climate change scenarios (Navarro et al., 2014; Mishra et 
al., 2025). Overall, the results indicate contrasting climatic sensitivities among the 
hybrids. Higher soil temperatures reduced PAIC in Beaupre and Gibecq, suggesting 
greater vulnerability to heat and associated drought stress. In contrast, Trichobel 
responded positively to summer rainfall and wind speed, highlighting a greater capacity 
to maintain productivity under variable climatic conditions. Importantly, weather effects 
were not limited to the active growing season. Positive associations between autumn 
and winter wind speed and PAIC in Trichobel suggest that favourable conditions during 
dormancy and the pre-growing season may support carbohydrate storage, bud 
development, and subsequent cambial activity, thereby contributing to greater annual C 
accumulation. Conversely, the negative effect of spring wind speed on Beaupre 
indicates greater sensitivity to early-season climatic stress. 
These findings are particularly relevant in the context of UK climate change projections, 
which indicate continued warming, hotter and drier summers, more frequent droughts, 
and increased climate variability (Lowe et al., 2018; Kendon et al., 2019). 
Consequently, future agroforestry deployment strategies should account not only for 
current productivity but also for climate resilience. The superior long-term performance 
[PDF PAGE 191]
 
171 
 
of Trichobel suggests that genotypes that maintain growth under increasing heat and 
moisture stress are likely to provide more reliable long-term C sequestration. 
6.3.5.  Agroforestry as a climate mitigation strategy 
This thesis demonstrates that silvoarable agroforestry can make a significant 
contribution to climate change mitigation, despite the study system only being actively 
managed as agroforestry between 1993 and 2004, through C sequestration in biomass, 
roots, and soils and improvements in ecosystem functioning (Jose, 2009; Cardinael et 
al., 2015; Cardinael et al., 2018a). 
Scenario projections suggest that UK-wide adoption of silvoarable agroforestry could 
sequester 0.87-7.16 Tg CO₂ yr⁻¹, depending on the scale of implementation and hybrid 
selected. Carbon sequestration was dominated by tree biomass (AGB + BGB) 
accumulation (83%), with Trichobel consistently achieving the highest sequestration 
rates. As a result, agroforestry could offset approximately 3.1-17.3% of projected UK 
agricultural emissions in 2050, depending on adoption scenario (Department for Energy 
Security and Net Zero, 2022). 
The projected C trajectories indicate that sequestration rates are unlikely to increase 
indefinitely. As trees mature, growth rates decline and systems move towards a steady 
state, reducing annual C uptake over time (Truax et al., 2014). This pattern was evident 
in the present study, where PAIC declined during the final decade for Gibecq, Robusta, 
and Beaupre, indicating slower biomass growth and C accumulation with stand age. 
A key finding is the trade-off between maximum C sequestration and practical 
implementation. While high-adoption scenarios achieved the greatest C removals, 
lower-adoption scenarios reduced total sequestration by approximately 70-76% but may 
be more realistic and achievable within existing farming systems (Westaway et al., 
2023). Therefore, although agroforestry represents an effective nature-based climate 
solution, it is unlikely to deliver net-zero targets alone and will need to be implemented 
alongside other mitigation measures (Ntawuruhunga et al., 2023; Rosenstock et al., 
2023; Westaway et al., 2023). 
[PDF PAGE 192]
 
172 
 
In summary, the results indicate that agroforestry should be viewed as a multifunctional 
climate mitigation strategy capable of delivering substantial and sustained C 
sequestration while simultaneously supporting soil health, nutrient cycling, biodiversity, 
and agricultural resilience. However, the magnitude and permanence of these benefits 
depend on species selection, system design, management practices, and long-term 
stewardship. 
6.4. Policy implications 
My findings demonstrate that both agroforestry support policies and wider net-zero 
strategies should move beyond generic C sequestration estimates and recognise that C 
and nutrient outcomes vary according to species or hybrid, tree age, planting density, 
alley width, management history, and climatic conditions. Consequently, incentive 
schemes and C accounting frameworks should support site-specific system design and 
assessment rather than prescribing a single agroforestry configuration or C value.  
The management practices outlined in Table 6.1 are broadly consistent with the findings 
of this thesis; however, the results suggest that current policy frameworks could place 
greater emphasis on factors influencing the long-term stability of C sequestration. 
Existing schemes, including the Sustainable Farming Incentive (SFI), Environmental 
Land Management (ELM) schemes, and the Woodland Carbon Code, primarily 
encourage tree establishment, woodland expansion, and C storage (Table 6.1).  
In addition, while Table 6.1 highlights practices that enhance biomass production and C 
storage, the results of Chapters 3 and 4 demonstrate that substantial C benefits are also 
derived from belowground processes. Current C accounting approaches may therefore 
underestimate the contribution of root biomass, root-derived C inputs, and SOC. Future 
policy frameworks could adopt a more comprehensive whole-system accounting 
approach that explicitly recognises aboveground, belowground, and soil C pools when 
assessing agroforestry C benefits, improving the accuracy and credibility of C reporting 
and incentive schemes. Another policy implication is the need for robust monitoring and 
verification systems. The substantial variation observed among hybrids, cropping 
management treatments, and C pools suggests that the use of generic sequestration 
factors may result in over- or under-estimation of agroforestry C benefits.

The findings also support several management recommendations identified in Table 6.1, 
including longer-term tree retention, diversified planting, and maintenance of vegetative 
cover. However, the observed influence of seasonal climatic conditions on C 
accumulation suggests that policy mechanisms should move beyond static estimates of 
C storage and incorporate long-term monitoring requirements. Monitoring of biomass 
growth, soil C, root dynamics, and climate sensitivity would improve confidence in C 
accounting while helping to ensure the permanence of C benefits under changing 
climatic conditions. 
Finally, Table 6.1 emphasises management practices that increase C storage, but the 
findings of this thesis indicate that C sequestration outcomes are strongly influenced by 
interactions among management, species selection, and climate. Consequently, future 
agroforestry policies may benefit from a greater focus on adaptive, site-specific 
management approaches rather than applying uniform prescriptions across systems. 
Such modifications would better align policy incentives with the processes driving long-
term C sequestration identified in this study and strengthen the contribution of 
agroforestry to the UK's net-zero, climate adaptation, and biodiversity objectives.  
6.5. Limitations 
While this thesis provides a long-term assessment of C dynamics in a mature temperate 
agroforestry system, several limitations should be acknowledged. Across Chapters 2 
and 3, uncertainties in C stock and sequestration estimates arise from the use of 
allometric equations, as species-specific models for poplar hybrids are limited and 
consistent tree height measurements were unavailable over the 30-year study period. 
Consequently, AGB was estimated using diameter alone, while BGB was modelled 
using a fixed root-to-shoot ratio (BGB = 0.26 × AGB), introducing additional 
uncertainty into C sequestration estimates. The upscaling scenarios presented in Chapter 
3 should therefore be regarded as indicative assessments of potential C sequestration 
rather than predictions of future net-zero outcomes, as they do not fully account for 
uncertainties in farmer adoption, land suitability, management practices, economic 
constraints, or future climate change. In Chapter 4, root biomass estimates may have 
[PDF PAGE 195]
 
175 
 
been underestimated due to losses of fine roots during washing and sieving, while 
distinguishing tree roots from grass and crop roots was challenging, a limitation also 
reported in agroforestry root studies (Upson and Burgess, 2013). A key limitation of 
Chapters 2- 4 is that the silvoarable experimental site did not remain a strictly 
silvoarable system throughout the study period. Following the cessation of cropping, the 
development of permanent grass cover altered belowground processes and likely 
contributed to SOC accumulation through additional root inputs and reduced soil 
disturbance (Ma et al., 2020). Furthermore, because the study was conducted at a single 
long-term experimental site, caution should be exercised when extrapolating the 
findings to other temperate agroforestry systems differing in soil type, climate, 
management intensity, or tree species composition. Although the site provides valuable 
insights into long-term agroforestry development, broader geographic replication would 
improve confidence in the generalisability of the results. Therefore, the SOC 
sequestration rates reported here should be interpreted as reflecting the long-term 
evolution of a mature agroforestry system rather than a continuously managed 
silvoarable system. 
Finally, Chapter 5 was subject to methodological uncertainties associated with litterbag 
studies, including the influence of hedgerow management on litter inputs, potential 
mesh-size effects on decomposition processes, and challenges separating highly 
decomposed litter from soil material. Despite these limitations, the consistent patterns 
observed across biomass, soil, root, and litter C pools provide strong evidence that 
mature agroforestry systems enhance long-term C storage and ecosystem functioning. 
The study focused on C sequestration within biomass and soils and did not incorporate 
full life-cycle greenhouse gas accounting, including emissions associated with 
establishment, management operations, harvesting, processing or product substitution 
effects. 
While these limitations introduce uncertainty into some estimates, they are unlikely to 
alter the overall conclusions of the thesis because consistent patterns were observed 
across AGB, soil, root, and litter C pools. Collectively, the findings provide robust 
evidence that mature agroforestry systems enhance long-term C storage and support key 
ecosystem functions. 
[PDF PAGE 196]
 
176 
 
6.6. Future work 
6.6.1.  Improving carbon accounting and upscaling  
Improving Future research should evaluate C sequestration across mature agroforestry 
systems located on different soil types and along climatic gradients. Multi-site studies 
would improve understanding of how species composition, vegetation interactions, soil 
properties, temperature, and rainfall influence long-term C storage and sequestration 
rates (Jose, 2009; Cardinael et al., 2018b; Cardinael et al., 2025). Further work is also 
required to strengthen large-scale projections by better quantifying farmer adoption 
rates, land suitability, species- and site-specific sequestration rates, management 
impacts, and the long-term stability of C stocks under changing climatic conditions 
(Graves et al., 2010; Cardinael et al., 2025). Improved understanding of these factors 
would enable more robust regional and national-scale upscaling of agroforestry and 
provide stronger evidence for assessing its contribution to net-zero targets and 
informing land-use policy (Kay et al., 2019; Cardinael et al., 2025). 
Future studies should also assess the implications of harvesting trees at their biological 
rotation age, as extending rotations may increase both C storage and timber value 
(Fortier et al., 2017). In addition, C-credit valuation and C-pricing mechanisms should 
be incorporated into cost-benefit analyses to quantify the financial benefits associated 
with ecosystem service provision and C sequestration (Kumar et al., 2020; Awazi et al., 
2025c). Greater integration between scientific projections and policy implementation is 
also needed to support evidence-based climate mitigation strategies (Kay et al., 2019; 
Cardinael et al., 2025). 
 
6.6.2.  Improving understanding of belowground processes 
The findings of Chapter 4 highlighted the importance of root-mediated processes in 
regulating nutrient availability and soil C inputs. Future work should investigate how 
management practices such as pruning, thinning, pollarding, and lopping influence root 
turnover, nutrient cycling, and C sequestration (Jose, 2009; Udawatta and Jose, 2011). 
[PDF PAGE 197]
 
177 
 
Management strategies should be evaluated across spatial and temporal scales to 
optimise tree-crop interactions and maximise system productivity. 
Particular attention should be given to root exudates, rhizosphere processes, and root-
microbial interactions, which play important roles in nutrient cycling and may reduce 
dependence on synthetic fertilisers (Panchal et al., 2022). The contribution of different 
root classes to soil C sequestration also requires further investigation (Rasse et al., 2005; 
Ma et al., 2022). Deep-rooted species, such as poplar, may enhance C storage in deeper 
soil horizons through coarse-root turnover and long-residence-time residues 
(Tufekcioglu et al., 1999; Cardinael et al., 2018b). However, these processes may also 
stimulate C losses through increased microbial activity (Ma et al., 2022). Future 
research should therefore focus on quantifying the balance between C sequestration and 
C emissions in deeper soil layers and improving understanding of the mechanisms 
controlling long-term C persistence and stability within agroforestry systems (Cotrufo et 
al., 2013; Lavallee et al., 2020). 
6.6.2.  Improving monitoring and modelling approaches 
Improving the accuracy of biomass and C estimates remains a key research priority. 
Future studies should obtain detailed measurements of tree height, stem density, and 
crown characteristics, while destructive sampling should be used where feasible to 
develop and validate species-specific allometric equations for poplar hybrids. Such 
approaches would improve estimates of both AGB and BGB (Fortier et al., 2017; 
Cardinael et al., 2025). 
Emerging monitoring technologies, including LiDAR, UAV-based photogrammetry, 
and satellite observations, offer considerable potential to improve long-term 
assessments of agroforestry C stocks. These tools can provide more accurate and 
spatially explicit estimates of tree growth, canopy development, biomass accumulation, 
and C sequestration while reducing the need for repeated field measurements (Cardinael 
et al., 2025). 
In parallel, process-based agroforestry models such as WaNuLCAS, SCUAF, HyPAR, 
and HyCAS should be further developed and applied to investigate interactions among 
trees, crops, soils, and microbial communities under different management scenarios 
[PDF PAGE 198]
 
178 
 
(Matthews et al., 2004). Integrating improved field measurements, remote sensing 
technologies, and process-based models will strengthen national-scale C projections and 
support more reliable assessments of agroforestry's contribution to climate-change 
mitigation (Graves et al., 2010; Cardinael et al., 2025). 
Overall, future research should move beyond quantifying C stocks alone and focus on 
improving C accounting, understanding the biological processes governing C 
persistence, and developing robust monitoring and modelling approaches. This will be 
essential for increasing confidence in long-term sequestration estimates and supporting 
the inclusion of agroforestry within climate mitigation and net-zero strategies (Cardinael 
et al., 2018b; Cardinael et al., 2025). 
6.7. Conclusion  
My thesis demonstrates that agroforestry is a multifunctional land-use system capable of 
delivering substantial climate-change mitigation while enhancing soil health and 
nutrient cycling, with C sequestration governed by interconnected aboveground and 
belowground pathways operating across multiple C pools. While AGB represented the 
largest C pool, long-term C storage was also strongly influenced by root dynamics, soil 
organic C accumulation, litter decomposition, and nutrient cycling. Together, these 
processes determine how atmospheric C is captured, transferred, and retained within 
agroforestry systems. 
Across all chapters, species or hybrid selection, management history, climatic 
conditions, and system maturity emerged as the dominant controls of C sequestration 
outcomes. The long-term dataset demonstrated that hybrid rankings changed 
substantially through time. Although Beaupre exhibited superior early growth, 
Trichobel ultimately accumulated the greatest C stock and maintained the highest 
sequestration rates at maturity. These findings highlight the importance of long-term 
monitoring, as short-term studies may underestimate or misrepresent the lifetime C 
sequestration potential of agroforestry systems. 
The results further showed that agroforestry cannot be characterised using generic C 
sequestration values. Instead, sequestration potential is highly dependent on system 
[PDF PAGE 199]
 
179 
 
design, species selection, management practices, and site-specific environmental 
conditions. Consequently, both C accounting and policy development should adopt 
whole-system approaches that recognise the contributions of AGB, roots, soils, and 
organic matter dynamics, rather than relying solely on biomass-based estimates. 
At larger scales, the upscaling scenarios indicate that agroforestry can make a 
meaningful contribution to climate-change mitigation and national net-zero ambitions. 
However, the magnitude of this contribution will depend on rates of adoption, landscape 
suitability, management decisions, and long-term system performance. Therefore, future 
agroforestry deployment strategies should consider not only current productivity but 
also long-term resilience and stability under changing climatic conditions. 
Collectively, my thesis provides new evidence from a rare mature temperate 
agroforestry system and demonstrates that well-designed agroforestry can function as a 
resilient and multifunctional land-use strategy. By integrating C sequestration, soil 
health, nutrient cycling, and agricultural production, agroforestry has considerable 
potential to support climate mitigation, climate adaptation, and sustainable land-use 
objectives in the UK and beyond.

overall model significance. Lower AIC values indicate better model fit, whereas higher McFadden's pseudo-R² values indicate greater explanatory 
power.

230 
 
 
Supplementary Table A.1 shows the performance of weather-based models fitted for each hybrid group across six temporal periods (spring, summer, 
autumn, winter, summer-half, and winter-half year). Model performance was evaluated using Akaike Information Criterion (AIC), log-likelihood, 
McFadden's pseudo-R², and likelihood-ratio test statistics. Weather predictors included seasonal measures of air temperature, soil temperature, rainfall, 
sunshine duration, ground frost days, air frost days, growing degree days, wind speed, and windy days. These results indicate that weather variables 
during the summer weather were most strongly associated with variation in PAIC across all hybrids, whereas climatic conditions outside the summer 
season explained relatively little additional variations.

Supplementary Figure B.2 The scree plot displays the variance explained by the principal components for (a) soil nutrients and (b) root 
characteristics. 
The greatest amount of variance is explained by PC1 (≈25%) and PC2 (≈15%). The variance explained decreases gradually from PC3 to PC8, with 
each component contributing a moderate amount (approximately 5–10%) (Fig. B.2). Beyond this (e.g., PC9 and higher), the components add relatively 
little additional variance. An “elbow” in the scree plot appears around PC2–PC3, indicating that two to three principal components capture the main 
structure of the data. Nevertheless, including components up to PC8 could still provide additional detail, depending on the purpose of the analysis.
